Against the Current

by thetalee

Summary

Recovery from the ladder truck has been long and arduous; despite that, Buck has persevered. But at the end of his journey to return to work, the universe comes in with a one-two punch that leaves him having to swim against the current.

Notes

Here's the requisite playlist. Enjoy.

See the end of the work for more notes

Chapter 1

Recovery was painful, or so Buck was finding out. It was long, and it was arduous, and it hurt.

Back in May, a kid with a grudge decided that the best way to express his anger was through homemade explosives. Because one of the people he held a grudge against was Bobby, a bomb ended up being stuck on the undercarriage of the 118’s ladder truck. Or, at least, Buck assumed that’s how it went. He didn’t actually know, didn’t actually have the desire to know. Regardless of where the bomb was placed, the ladder truck ended up exploding while Buck was in it and then he found himself on the pavement with the truck on top of his leg.

Buck was no stranger to the pain and inconvenience of broken bones. When he was a kid, before Danny took him and ran to El Paso, he learned that getting hurt was the best way to get anything resembling his mother’s love and affection. And as a child who was so starved of that, he did everything he could to earn it. Which meant that he ended up hurt a lot, in various ways and through various means.

But this time was the worst he’d ever been hurt. His leg was casted up to his thigh, there was a rod and screws holding his bones together, and grafts of bone tissue. It might've been a relief when the cast came off, but it wasn’t the end of the road. Not by a long shot. Because afterwards came physical therapy appointments that left him shuddering and sobbing and snapping at everyone around him.

He didn’t want to be around himself during and after those appointments. The fact that anyone else chose to be, the fact that Maddie and Bobby and Eddie all chose to stay with him when he was the worst person to be around, was something that struck him as a miracle. The fact that Danny did it was… Well, that was about as surprising as the sun rising in the morning.

There was never any doubt in Buck’s mind about Danny being there for him. Never.

Not that Buck’s life was just pain and pain and more pain since the truck bombing, because it wasn’t. In May, before everything went to hell, Eddie had manned up and asked him out on a date. A proper date, somewhere fancy and somewhere public. Except Shannon’s death postponed that, for obvious reasons, and then the truck bombing postponed it even more. But Eddie kissed him at his shield ceremony, in front of their friends and family, and that was more than enough to appease Buck and hold him over for a while.

Eddie hadn’t spoken to his parents since, and communications with his sisters was sparse. But he had Pepa and Isabel, at least, and Danny had let go of his years-long grudge against the younger man enough to comfort and support him as well. Which was a very good thing, because Buck’s leg made going anywhere an absolute pain. A literal pain. Buck couldn’t easily go to Eddie’s, so Eddie - and Chris - came to him. It would be very awkward if Danny was still glaring at Eddie all the time.

Even after the cast came off, and crossing the living room was no longer a journey in and of itself, Eddie still came over to the apartment. With or without Chris to use as a buffer between himself and Danny. For movie nights, usually, like the old animated Alice in Wonderland, but sometimes just so Chris could watch Danny destroy both his father and Buck at various video games. It never failed to give Buck a heavy dose of deja vu.

Years ago, during high school, Eddie would be over at their apartment almost all the time, except for when Buck was over at his house. It wasn’t much different now, except this time they were usually joined by an eight year old and occasionally by Maddie and/or Austin Jones, the 118’s driver-engineer and Danny’s… Buck wasn’t sure what label they were using. If they were using a label at all, beyond “Not Boyfriends.” He just knew there was an awful lot of cuddling happening there, whenever Chris wasn’t doing his best to monopolize Danny’s time and attention.

There was a picture on Buck’s phone of Danny sitting in the arm chair, snoring away, with a sleeping Chris curled up in his lap. It got sent to Maddie immediately, because it was adorable. The one of Austin and Danny asleep together in that same chair got sent to Hen and Chimney, because Buck was a little shit and because Austin was unassuming enough that he generally managed to slide under the radar and avoid the ribbing everyone at the station gave each other. That picture should give them enough ammunition for at least a whole shift.

Though, he would much rather be at the station to join in with the teasing. It wasn’t the same, hearing the stories after the fact of calls that his friends went on. Being stuck at home, feeling like everyone was leaving him behind as he hobbled to catch up. The ache of a healing bone was only rivaled by the ache in his heart and the desire to just get back on the trucks with everyone else.

Sure, Buck knew that he was useful. If Danny got sick again, god forbid, Buck could fix it. That was the point of him, after all. But that was only if Danny got sick again. Buck was the fire extinguisher in the glass case that read, “in case of emergency…” If there wasn’t a fire to put out, then there wasn’t a need for him.

It took him so very long to find something to do with his life. So many places, so many jobs. He had been lost until Danny not-so-subtly nudged him towards firefighting, and it was one of the several, several reasons his big brother was the best. Firefighting gave Buck a purpose besides just being spare parts, it made him actually feel useful beyond just what his body could provide.

He wanted to be a firefighter again, he needed to be. The sooner, the better. So he went with the surgeries, he persevered through the physical therapy, he did everything he could to get back to the station as quickly as possible.

The pain was just a minor consequence.

Daniel loved his sister, and he would have gladly murdered Doug himself to keep her safe. But sometimes, especially lately, he didn’t particularly like her very much. Maddie had opinions about their brother’s path to recovery, concerns that he was pushing himself too hard and too fast, kept espousing platitudes about how Buck could find something else besides firefighting while ignoring the way their brother so very much didn’t want that.

And sure, maybe Daniel shared her opinions and concerns, and maybe he didn’t understand why his baby brother was so desperate to return to this job in particular. But Maddie hadn’t been there to face a Buck - an Evan - that was so very lost and seeking a direction. She hadn’t been there when their mother pushed and pushed for their baby brother to be nothing more than a china doll placed in a cabinet, safe and forgotten. She hadn’t been there to see how happy Buck was when he finally got to do what he wanted with his life and his body.

Buck had made his wishes and desires and goals clear, and Daniel was going to support him.

Truthfully, though, that was all merely an irritation. Something that he and Maddie would quibble over and then move past, because it wasn’t like her opinions and concerns and platitudes mattered. Not when she had no say over what Buck could or could not do. No, the reason that Daniel didn’t particularly like his sister at the moment was because of something simple yet simultaneously complicated: she told their mother what happened.

Daniel had fully intended to keep Mom and Dad in the dark for as long as possible. Forever, preferably. He didn’t tell them anything about Buck during their irregular phone calls, because he very much desired to keep as much distance between them and his baby brother as he could. There was a reason, after all, that he snagged guardianship of his brother and fled with him to another time zone.

Maddie, for as much as her relationship with them was rather fraught, seemed to have a more rose-tinted view of their parents. Back in Hershey, when she kept trying to get Daniel to leave for school, she would always insist that Buck didn’t need him. She was of the impression that their parents weren’t that bad, that they weren’t liable to utterly crush their brother’s spirit into a fine paste if he was left alone with them.

So, even though she hadn’t bothered to tell them when she left Doug, even though it had been left to Daniel to tell them when Doug died, even though she’d barely been talking to them before that point, Maddie called their parents - well, their mother - after the truck bombing and told them that Buck had gotten hurt. She also told them that he was a firefighter at the same time, something Daniel also hadn’t mentioned to them. Because, again, he kept as much space between his parents and his baby brother as he could manage, even to the point of keeping his name out of their talks.

There’d been exactly one conversation between Daniel and his mother after that, when she called him a couple days after the bombing. She’d immediately started demanding to know how he could be so stupid as to let Buck- no, sorry, as to let Evan put himself in danger like that. A firefighter, really, Daniel? And look what happened now, Daniel, didn’t she always try to warn them about Evan putting himself in danger?

Daniel, of course, responded in the appropriate manner - loudly and rudely - and then hung up. He’d ignored the many, many times she and Dad both tried calling him back, deleted the emails they sent him without even reading them, and was thankful they didn’t have any way of contacting Buck directly. Things had been tense between him and Maddie ever since.

“Mom said you still aren’t answering when she calls,” Maddie said while he watched from the sidelines. She’d called him to check in on how Buck was doing, and then wasted little time moving on to their topic of contention.

“How ya holdin’ up over there, Buck?” Daniel called over to his brother instead of answering his sister. 

“Just fine!” Buck snapped. He was gasping for breath, face red and twisted, as the therapist guided him through strengthening exercises. There were definitely tears making their way down his cheeks, not that any of them was going to point that out.

“Dan.” Maddie sounded reproachful, unappreciative of being ignored.

“You’re lucky I’m talking to you,” Daniel hissed at her. “Buck’s life is none of their business.”

“It’s Mom and Dad,” she said. “He’s their son.”

Daniel couldn’t help the way he rolled his eyes at her.

“Let’s take a break,” the therapist announced, letting up on Buck’s leg. “Rest for a bit, and then we can move on to the next set.”

Buck flopped back on the bench and raised a shaky hand to give a thumbs up. Daniel took the opportunity to hang up on their sister and the brewing argument, making his way over to crouch next to his brother.

“You’re doing good, squirt,” he said, running his fingers through Buck’s hair. He hadn’t bothered to put any product in, and so the curls were hanging loose and free. And sweaty, now.

“Fuck you,” was the weary sounding response, though no less sharp for the lack of energy.

“Isabel has Chris today,” Daniel said, entirely unperturbed by the vitriol. “We’ll go over when you’re done here, if you want.”

“If I want. Like she even phrased it as a request.” 

“If you don’t want to go, then she’ll get over it.” Because it really was as simple as that.

Buck closed his eyes for a moment.

“Yeah, sure,” he said. “Seeing them both sounds nice about now. Could do with a Christopher hug.”

“Alright then,” Daniel said with a nod. He glanced up, spotting the physical therapist making their way over again. “Your torturer’s coming back, looks like break time’s over.”

“Oh, goodie,” Buck spat, though he sat up all the same and swung his legs around. “Let’s get this over with.”

Daniel gave him an encouraging pat on the shoulder and then returned to his spot by the wall, settling in to watch once more.

Recovery was painful. It was long, and it was arduous, and it hurt. But by the end, all that pain was worth it. When Buck was cleared to recertify, he did so with flying colors and by breaking records. Putting on the gear again for the course was enough to make the world feel right again. Hearing the words “welcome back, Buckley,” from the instructor had him feeling better than he had in months.

The world was bright and it was beautiful and everything was coming up aces.

Athena invited him and Danny over for dinner to celebrate, and no way was Buck going to turn that down. Especially since, after she and Bobby eloped and then he moved in, he was going to get the captain’s cooking. He would never pass up a chance to eat the captain’s cooking.

What Buck was not expecting when Athena let the two of them inside was to be confronted by a wall of noise as a crowd of people shouted,

“Surprise!”

He just about leaped out of his skin and had to clutch the stair railing to keep from toppling over. Not only were the rest of the Grants plus Bobby present, but so was Maddie, along with Eddie and Chris and everyone from the station. Athena and Danny looked very pleased with themselves.

Buck hopped down the last couple of steps, meeting Eddie halfway when Bobby pushed him forward and out of the small crowd. They came together like they hadn’t seen each other in months instead of a mere couple of days, Eddie’s hands finding their way to Buck’s hips as Buck wrapped his arms around him. When they kissed, or rather when Buck kissed him, he felt Eddie stiffen for a moment before relaxing. Buck didn’t get much time to focus on it, distracted as he was first by the act of kissing him and then by Bobby bullying his way in for a hug of his own.

Afterwards, the crowd dispersed through the house and into the backyard. Buck was about to go that way, lured by the smell of food being grilled, when a tiny voice brought him to a stop on the patio.

“I made you a card,” Chris said, making his careful way to him with it clutched in his hand.

Buck crouched down as he took it, a smile threatening to tear his face in twain.

“Aw, thanks, buddy.” Written at the top was BUCK with (Bff) just beneath it. There was a drawing of a firefighter with a bespectacled kid holding a surfboard. “This is great.”

“It’s you and me,” Chris said.

“And uh, what’s that?” he asked, pointing at the drawing.

“It’s a surfboard, right?” Eddie said, leaning over them. “He’s obsessed.”

“Hey, surfing is fun!” Buck said, before flipping open the card. He read aloud, “Dear Buck, you are an awesome firefighter. Love, Christopher.” Oh God, he was going to cry. He wrapped his arms around the kid, squeezing him gently. “This is so sweet, thank you.”

Christopher beamed at him.

“C’mon,” Buck said, standing up. He resisted the urge to rub at his leg. It had been a little sore after the course he ran to recertify, but nothing compared to what it had been. It was just a minor cramp, the kind of thing to be expected after the summer he’d had. “Let’s go show Danny.”

Danny was standing with Austin, the driver-engineer pressed against his side, and talking to Hen and Karen. Buck wasn’t concerned about that, not initially. Not until he got close enough to hear what they were saying.

“So Alma picks me up, because the concussion wasn’t bad but I’m still not supposed to be driving myself anywhere,” Danny was saying. “And it’s a school day, so I’m expecting to come home to an empty apartment.”

Why did this story sound familiar? Wait. Wait, wait- Oh, God.

Buck and Eddie shared a look of abject horror.

“Hey,” Buck said, stepping in. He could hear his voice going squeaky. “Danny, you wanna see the card Chris made me?”

His awful, awful big brother just smirked at him.

“I walk in the door-”

“Danny. Danny, please-”

“-and I get an eyeful of both of them-”

“Danny,” Buck whined at the same time as Eddie. The Wilsons and Austin all broke into laughter.

“I screamed, they screamed,” Danny said, staring the pair of them right in the face as he ruined their lives. “Alma comes rushing in thinking something is wrong, so she gets an eyeful, too.”

“Oh, God,” Hen said, gasping for breath between laughter. “What did you do?”

“I told them to put on pants and wash their hands,” he said, which made them laugh even louder.

“And then he gave us the Talk,” Eddie said, covering his face. It was practically glowing with how red it was. “Most embarrassing day of my life.”

“Not that you apparently listened to it,” Danny said under his breath as he gave Chris a high five. “Then I made them clean the couch.”

“Gross.” Karen’s face was scrunched up in distaste.

“There are worse ways to come out of the closet,” Austin said. “Probably.”

“You’re all dead to me,” Buck grumbled. “I’m gonna go find Maddie, she doesn’t bully me.”

“Since when?” Danny asked with a scoff as he knelt down to chat with Chris. 

Buck ignored him in favor of seeking out his sister. He found her with Chimney, of course, the pair of them sequestered a bit away from everyone else.

“I cannot believe,” he said as he joined them, “that either of you managed to keep this a secret. Wait, there’s cake?”

“There was going to be two cakes,” Chimney said, passing him a plate with a slice on it, along with a plastic fork 

“Yeah, we weren’t sure how today was going to go,” Maddie told him.

Buck glowered, because he’d forgotten that she absolutely did bully him, too.

“The other one was going to be in case you crashed and burned,” Chimney explained. “Your brother crushed that idea, but it was gonna say ‘better Buck next time.’”

“Aw,” Maddie said, and then fed him a piece of cake.

“Just get married already,” Buck told them, feeling his face contort at the sight.

“And don’t forget to invite us,” a passing Michael quipped. Athena, following just behind him, rolled her eyes.

“Let it go,” she said.

Back in the spring, after the ladder truck crushed Buck’s leg, Bobby and Athena eloped. The only ones they told, the only ones they invited, had been May and Harry. Several people were still carrying grudges about that. Michael was very clearly one of them.

After leaving Maddie and Chimney to whatever odd courtship ritual they were doing, though it was no weirder than whatever the hell Danny and Austin were doing, Buck found Bobby. The captain greeted him with another hug, firm and warm. Buck had gotten a lot of them from Bobby the last few months, with the man taking him to surgeries and rehab appointments whenever Danny wasn’t able.

“They are sending me the paperwork in the morning, and then you will officially be back,” he told him. “How’s that feel?”

“I’m just glad it’s over,” Buck said. He took a breath and rubbed at his chest; there was a sharpness there, for some reason. “I hated not being with you guys.”

“We missed you, too,” Bobby said. “It’ll be nice to have the whole team back together, though you’ll have more paperwork to fill out then.”

The captain smirked at him, and then his eyes flickered over to where Eddie was talking with Danny.

“And imagine,” Bobby said, smirk growing wider. “You acted like you couldn’t stand him at first. Aren’t you glad I didn’t let you transfer out when you asked?”

“Oh, uh.” Buck coughed, cleared his throat. “You, uh. You remember that story I told you about on Valentine’s Day, before my date with Abby? About my senior prom?”

Bobby slowly nodded.

“I remember.”

“That, um. That was Eddie.”

Bobby’s face went blank. Then he closed his eyes and let out a breath from his nose.

“Texas. Latin Catholics.” He shook his head. “You both even have the same birthdays. How did I miss that?”

“You weren’t-” Buck paused to cough again. The captain frowned at him. “You weren’t looking.”

“Are you okay?”

“Yeah, I-” More coughing. “I’m-”

Buck couldn’t seem to stop coughing. He couldn’t catch a breath, either. He coughed and he coughed, something wet and coppery in his mouth, as darkness encroached his vision.

The last thing he saw, before that darkness blotted out everything, was Bobby’s terrified face.

Chapter 2

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Daniel had long grown tired of seeing his baby brother in hospital rooms. Buck ended up in them so often growing up, because he’d learned that getting himself hurt was one of the only surefire ways he could receive anything resembling care and affection from their mother. Hospital visits dwindled when the care and affection was eventually replaced with anger and exasperation, and then stopped almost entirely when Daniel moved them both to El Paso. Almost entirely; football was not exactly the safest of sports to play.

It felt like they’d spent so much of their time in the hospital these past few months. The initial recovery from the truck bombing, the surgeries that followed, the check-ups. And now, now, when they were supposed to be well past everything, they were once again back in the ICU. Because Daniel’s baby brother started coughing up blood in the middle of his party.

Jesus Christ.

“You got lucky,” the newest doctor said. It was him and the three Buckley siblings, plus Chimney. Daniel had no goddamn clue why Chimney was there. “Most people who suffer a pulmonary embolism don’t do it surrounded by trained medical professionals. It saved your life.”

Maddie opened her mouth, but Buck beat her to it with a shaky,

“Pulmonary embolism?”

“A blood clot that blocks an artery in your lungs. Usually forms in the deep veins of your legs,” the doctor explained, opening up the medical file in his hand. “You had three clots. The one that hit your lungs, and two more in your leg.”

“In- In the deep veins.” Buck was gripping at the thigh of the leg in question.

Daniel frowned at that. Laying on the bed in a hospital gown, with an oxygen cannula under his nose, his brother was looking pale and distraught. Maybe he was just upset about ending up in the hospital once again, just like Daniel was, but…

“Yes,” the doctor said, giving a slow nod. “It’s called a deep vein thrombosis.”

“So what caused the blood clots?” Maddie finally broke in to ask.

“Unclear,” was the doctor’s shitty answer.

“He just got a clean bill of health last week,” Chimney said. “This came out of nowhere.”

“Did it?” the doctor asked, turning to Buck. Not that Buck seemed as if he was paying attention to any of them, focused on his leg as he was. “No pain or tenderness in the leg? Sk-”

“I’m sorry,” Daniel interrupted. “Did you just ask the man who had a ladder truck crush his leg, who’s spent months going through rehabs and surgeries, if he noticed pain or tenderness? His tibia was put back together with screws and a goddamn rod, and you’re asking if he noticed some tenderness?”

“Dan.” Maddie sounded reproachful again as she rolled her eyes. “Buck’s been training to recertify for the LAFD.”

“That could explain it, then,” the doctor said. “Training hard can lead to dehydration, which can increase the risk of clots.”

“Have you done any tests?” Daniel demanded. “Any tests at all? Or did you get your medical degree from a pawn shop?”

“Dan!” Maddie snapped. Beside her, Chimney was looking a mite uncomfortable. Screw him, though, Daniel didn’t care about his discomfort.

“No, Maddie. We have a family history of leukemia, and this quack’s immediate thought is to blame-”

“Danny.”

His brother’s voice was quiet, far too quiet for how loud he usually was, but Daniel shut up all the same. Buck’s gaze was still locked onto his leg, fingers digging into the meat of his thigh. With his frown growing deeper, Daniel gently pried those fingers off before that grip could start causing bruises.

“We’ll move you to a room,” the doctor told Buck. “Keep you on the anticoagulants. Tomorrow, we’ll run more tests.” That sounded like it was meant to be a jab at Daniel, but Daniel honestly didn’t care. “And then we’ll see.”

The doctor checked his pager, nodded to the group of them, and then started out.

“Thanks, Doc,” Chimney said, and then glanced at the Buckley siblings. Maddie was scowling down at Buck; Daniel was scowling at her. “I’m, uh. I’m gonna go tell everyone that you’re okay. Let them know you’re staying awhile, alright?”

With a kiss to the side of Maddie’s head, Chimney scurried away.

“Was there any pain or tenderness?” she asked Buck.

“Maddie,” Daniel snapped.

“No, Dan, I’d like to know if he was just ignoring things because he was rushing to get back to work. If this had happened when he was alone-”

“Can I be alone, please?” Buck wasn’t looking at either of them, his eyes still fixed on his leg.

“Are you sure?” Daniel asked him.

“Please,” his brother repeated, and then, in a whisper, “I didn’t mean to…”

“I know.” He leaned down to press a kiss into his brother’s hair, ran his hand over it, and then stepped away. Maddie didn’t seem like she wanted to leave, but some pointed glaring had her moving.

“I said it at the beginning,” she started the moment they’d turned the corner and Buck was out of sight. “I wanted him to slow down, be careful, listen to his doctors-”

“Are you kidding me right now?” Daniel snapped at her. “Are you trying to make this his fault?”

“He coughed up blood because he was focused on getting back to work as fast as possible. Because he was being reckless.”

“He coughed up blood because we fucked up!” He didn’t shout it, but it was a near thing. His sister, eyes going wide, took a hurried step back from him. “You were a nurse! An ER nurse! And I wanted to be a fucking doctor. But between the two of us, we somehow managed to miss the most obvious fucking thing in the entire fucking world. Shit, it was even in my paramedics textbook. Crush injuries cause blood clots! And yet the two of us are too fucking stupid -”

“None of his doctors mentioned it,” she said.

“The fact that everyone else around him fucked up doesn’t mean we fucked up any less!” Daniel said. “Maybe it’s a good thing we have the jobs we do now. You manning the phones and me driving the truck. Because clearly, if we were working in a hospital, we’d be getting people killed left and right.”

A hesitant hand landed on his arm.

“Dan,” Maddie said. “It’s not our fault.”

Daniel scoffed.

“Keep telling yourself that,” he said, and then, “It’s not Buck’s fault.”

He didn’t think she agreed with him, but she also didn’t argue. It was probably the best they were going to get, and pushing for anything more would just result in him screaming at her. Even if she probably deserved it for blaming their brother for getting hurt, just like their mother always did.

That reminded him.

“Do not tell Mom and Dad what happened,” Daniel said.

“Dan.” Maddie rolled her eyes as she let her hand drop. “They’re our parents, they deserve to know.”

“No, they don’t,” he told her. “You want them in your life, fine. You can keep them all up to date about your dead husband and your new boyfriend. They do not get to hear a damn thing about Buck. Mom doesn’t get to know a damn thing about him.”

Honestly, Daniel was a bit terrified that they’d decide to suddenly care enough to make the trip to Los Angeles. Their last visit, during Buck’s junior year of high school, had been bad enough. Eddie accidentally let the cat out of the bag about Buck playing football, and Mom started in with how dangerous it was and how he wasn’t going to play it anymore, and then Daniel raised his voice.

Daniel would kill someone to keep his baby brother from being subjected to their mother again. Especially now.

“I’m serious, Maddie,” he said. “The last thing Buck needs is to have her scolding him for getting hurt. He doesn’t need it from her, he doesn’t need it from you.”

“Fine,” his sister said. “I’ll keep our parents completely in the dark about their son nearly dying. Again.”

“Thank you.”

Maddie’s “Whatever” was accompanied by an eye roll as she headed for the waiting room everyone was waiting in. Daniel lingered, uneasy with the idea of leaving his baby brother to himself. But Buck had asked to be alone, and so Daniel let out a sigh before following his sister.

The closest comparison Buck had to how he was currently feeling was that moment way back on his eighteenth birthday, when he overheard Eddie proposing to Shannon. He’d felt disconnected and drifting ever since the words “pulmonary embolism” and “deep veins” left the doctor’s mouth. Buck hadn’t needed the doctor to explain what a pulmonary embolism was, he’d recognized it the moment he heard it. He’d known what it meant. Everything that it meant.

His mother was right, wasn’t she? The entire time, she’d been right.

He was so selfish.

Buck felt disconnected and drifting, and his only life raft at the moment was his return to firefighting. It was at least something. Sure, okay, he’d managed to mess up so badly that he was practically worthless now, but it wasn’t a total loss. As long as he was a firefighter he was good for something. His entire world crumbled around him, but at least he had that going for him still.

He also wasn’t alone, so there was that. Danny was a constant presence in his hospital room, and probably would’ve used even more of his vacation days except Buck practically shoved him out the door. Eddie was there, too, with or without Christopher. It was almost enough to keep his mind off his failures.

There was a lot of poking and prodding and testing as the doctors tried to figure out the cause of the blood clots. And when they weren’t doing that, they had him up and walking around to keep the blood flowing. That’s what they had him doing when Bobby, still in uniform, swung by to visit.

“You sure it’s okay to be walking around like this?” Bobby asked, and Buck only managed to keep from rolling his eyes because he knew his captain was concerned.

“They want me up and moving, it’s the best thing I could be doing right now. Y’know, better for the blood flow in my legs,” he explained as the two of them did a circuit through the halls. Danny had split off in search of a vending machine. “Seriously, if I’m not being poked or they’re not testing me, they’re making me walk.”

“So all that testing,” Bobby said. “Do they have any theories yet as to what caused the clots?”

“Not yet, but the meds are working. They’re gonna send me home tonight,” Buck said. He was very much looking forward to it, because he was sick and tired of hospitals after the past year. Though that reminded him. “Hey, do I need the hospital to sign some kind of form or anything? Y’know, for the department? For my clearance?”

“Um,” the captain said and came to a slow stop. “But you’re not… You’re not cleared, Buck. Not yet.”

What.

“Uh, no. I- I passed my physical and my recertification test. I broke a record doing it,” Buck said. He heard the tremble in his own voice. “I’ve got everything I need.”

“It’s the blood thinners,” Bobby explained. “The department is concerned about liability issues, and since the doctors haven’t figured out what’s causing the clots…”

“I can’t come back to work?”

“Well,” Bobby said. “If we were out on a call and something happened to you-”

“I would have two paramedics standing next to me, the same as I would’ve back in May,” Buck said. “I would be fine!”

Bobby took a breath and straightened his shoulders, but he didn’t say anything. He just stared at him.

“They can’t- They can’t do this to me,” Buck said. “You can’t let them take away my job, too.”

“Hey, we’re not.” Bobby was probably trying to go for reassuring, but he only managed to hit patronizing. “You were injured in the line of duty, and no one is forgetting that. Chief Alonzo think that in a few weeks, if you’re doing okay on the meds, he can clear you for light duty.”

“Light duty?” No, no, no. “Like a desk job?”

“Now, listen, Buck, I-”

“Being out there, helping people, that’s where I belong,” Buck said, talking over him. “That’s what I have spent months fighting to get back to, that’s all I have left. And now you’re gonna stand there and tell me that I- I can’t?”

“Listen, I know this is hard, but at least light-”

“Fuck you,” Buck said. Bobby reeled, though the words hadn’t come out harsh. They were soft and quiet. “No, I do not want light duty. Neither would you. No, no, they are not putting me behind a desk.”

Firefighting was all he had left, it was what he’d spent so long trying to get back to, and now Bobby was going to stand there and tell him he couldn’t even have that? No, that was-

God, Buck was so tired of this. He was tired of fighting. What was the point, if this was where it got him?

“I quit.”

Bobby didn’t say anything, either too shocked or too guilt ridden or too something to speak up. Buck brushed past him for his room, ignoring Danny as he went by and the way he tried to call for him.

What was the point, now?

Daniel’s grip on his bag of skittles tightened as he watched Buck walk off, shoulders hunched up and head bowed. He turned back to see Nash looking guilty about something.

“What was that about?” he demanded, stomping up to the captain. “What did you say to him?”

“He’s not cleared to come back,” Nash said. “The chief is thinking of putting him on light duty in a few weeks, after seeing how he does on the medication.”

“Light duty? Like a desk job?” Daniel shook his head. What kind of idiot suggested that?

“They’re concerned about liability issues.”

“And yet they let you come back.”

Nash’s lips thinned as he glared at Daniel.

“I’m concerned as well,” he said. “He was laser-focused on coming back to work, so much so that I’m wondering if he pushed himself too hard. If you let him push himself too hard.”

Daniel breathed through his nose and felt his free hand flex into a fist. Nash’s eyes were drawn to the movement, and he lifted his chin like he was daring Daniel to do something.

“You’re not his father,” Daniel told Nash. “Hell, if they aren’t letting him come back, you’re not even his captain. So you can take your concern, stuff it up your ass, and get the hell out of this hospital.”

Daniel left the captain, feeling the man glaring impotently at his back as he turned and headed to find Buck. Which was an easy task to accomplish, his brother had just gone back to his hospital room. When Daniel walked in, he found him curled up in the bed with his back to the door.

“Hey,” he said, perching on the edge of the mattress. He ran his fingers through Buck’s hair, disturbing the way it was styled. “Nash told me.”

“What is the point of me now?” It was a whisper pressed into the pillow.

“What?” Daniel frowned, his eyebrows pinching together. “What do you mean, what’s the point of you?”

“I was only useful for two things,” Buck said, still speaking into the pillow. “And now I can’t even… Mom was right. She was right the entire time.”

“Hey, no, we do not say that in this family,” Daniel said, tugging at his brother’s hair. It didn’t get any response at all. “Buck, Evan, c’mon. This thing with the chief is bullshit and they’re a bunch of idiots, but it’s not the end of the world.”

“I threw a clot, Danny.”

“I know, I was there.”

“A deep vein clot.” The words came out of Buck’s mouth in a sob. “I can’t- It was the one thing I knew for a fact I was good for. It was what I was made for, and now I can’t even do that.”

“Buck…”

“I can’t be a firefighter, I can’t make you better if you get sick again,” Buck said. “I’m- I’m useless, Danny. What is the point of me?”

“You’re my brother.” Daniel felt like he was blindly grasping for straws. “That’s- That’s all you need to be.”

Buck just shook his head and pressed his face into the pillow, hiding himself from everything. Hiding himself from Daniel. And Daniel was left to sit there with no idea how to make this better.

Chapter End Notes

I just want you all to understand that Daniel was five seconds away from using the fire in St Paul as a shiv throughout that entire conversation. I kept having to delete lines like, "No, no, that's too far Daniel. Reel it in."

Chapter 3

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

It had been a week since Buck was given the bad news regarding his job, or lack thereof. Eddie had never been far enough north to witness the deepest, darkest parts of winter, the days where the sun never rose, but Danny’s apartment was seemingly a close representation of it at the moment. It felt dark the last couple of times that Eddie tried swinging by, only to find Buck secluded in his bedroom with the lights off and Danny tearing at his hair.

The fact that Danny hadn’t put up even a pretense of disliking Eddie’s presence was enough of a sign of how worried the man was about his brother.

“Maddie said Buck hasn’t left the apartment in a week,” Bobby informed them, while they were helping the man prepare lunch at the station.

“Yeah, she’s tried to get him to go out a couple times,” Chimney said, in the middle of tearing apart a head of lettuce. “But it’s always, ‘Why would I need to leave? Everyone delivers.’ And Dan isn’t being very helpful, or at least Maddie doesn’t think so.”

“He won’t even take my calls.”

“He’s just sulking, Cap,” Eddie assured the man. “He’ll get over it.”

Just because he would never, ever say that to Buck, or anywhere Danny would hear - and maybe he did a quick look around to make sure Austin Jones wasn’t close enough to eavesdrop - it didn’t mean it wasn’t true. It was just a job. Yeah, sure, it sucked that Buck wasn’t there with them, and Eddie spent every shift since the truck bombing feeling the man’s absence like a missing limb, but it was just a job. There were others out there that were just as meaningful, and it wasn’t like Buck had any dependents to worry about. Not like Eddie did.

Then again, Eddie was almost positive that this was about more than just the job itself. There was something deeper at play, even if his boyfriend refused to come out and say what the actual problem was. Danny certainly knew, and Eddie would assume that he told his sister, except from the sounds of it, Maddie seemed just as clueless.

“Hey.” Hen didn’t sound like she disagreed, necessarily. “I like to give Buck crap as much as any of you lot, but this was a body blow, guys. The guy’s allowed some time to mourn.”

“The question is how much time,” Chimney argued. “His daily routine of walking from the bed to the couch and back again isn’t gonna help the situation.”

Personally, Eddie wasn’t sure that Buck was even doing that much. Which was very concerning, considering his current health status. It was part of what was driving Danny spare, Eddie knew, the fact that Buck didn’t even seem the least bit interested in taking care of himself at the moment.

“I know this sucks, but that’s life, right?” Eddie shrugged at the others. “Whenever stuff didn’t work out for me, my dad always told me to brush it off, keep moving forward. It wasn’t easy but he wasn’t wrong.”

“Because anything your father says is worth listening to,” Hen said with a sharp glare.

Eddie looked away, feeling irritated but knowing he couldn’t really argue. He’d heard what his father had to say in the voicemails he’d left, the unkind words about Eddie and Buck, after both of Eddie’s parents went back to El Paso. But that didn’t mean Eddie’s father was wrong with his advice. Hell, it was how Eddie was dealing with his parents at the moment.

Brush it off, keep moving forward.

“Look, we all had lives, jobs before we entered the department,” Hen said. “And we have things outside of it. Aside from his brother, what does Buck have?”

“Buck has us,” Bobby declared, bending down to get the main course out of the oven. “Even though he may not believe that right now.”

Eddie let out a sigh, set down the knife he’d been using to slice vegetables, and used his arms to prop himself against the kitchen island.

“He has pretty much what he had the last time he was anything close to this upset,” he pointed out. “And back then, he ended up fleeing the state.”

Bobby frowned heavily at that. Beside him, Hen let out a snort.

“You would know,” she said.

Eddie glowered, but once again knew he couldn’t argue. Last time had been entirely his fault, he knew. There was a reason Danny still held a grudge against him.

“Buck wouldn’t do that now,” Chimney argued. “Maddie’s here, he wouldn’t just leave his sister.”

Except that Danny had been there, and so had Alma, and Buck still ran. Except that when he came to Los Angeles, Buck hadn’t bothered seeking out Abuela or Pepa. Except that when Eddie started at the 118, Buck was perfectly willing to leave Bobby and Hen and Chimney just for the chance of avoiding him again.

Instead of voicing any of that, though, Eddie said,

“I’m just saying, maybe Buck has us, but it’s not enough. Especially since he clearly doesn’t believe he does, ‘cause otherwise he’d be taking Cap’s calls.”

“Maybe Karen and I should swing by.” Hen was tapping her nails against the countertop in thought. “I didn’t see him often enough over the summer, and I haven’t seen him in person since the party.”

Now that Eddie thought about it, Christopher hadn’t seen Buck since the man got out of the hospital. Maybe…

“So what’s Buck gonna do for the rest of his life?” Josh asked, pouring them both coffee. They were in the break room, Maddie was spending the time between calls to vent about her youngest brother and how he couldn’t be a firefighter any longer.

“I don’t know. I’m not sure he even knows what he’s gonna do with the rest of his day,” she told him. “Dan’s not helping. When I brought it up, he just looked at me and said, “he’s gonna be a firefighter.’”

“I thought the blood thinners meant he couldn’t be anymore?” Josh tilted his head, looking confused, as he handed Maddie her coffee. Together, they started back to the floor and their stations. “Though, I don’t really understand why. There are firefighters that work on chemo, and they let your boyfriend go back to work with brain damage.”

That comment earned him a scowl from Maddie. He raised his hands to ward it off.

“Don’t give me that look, it’s true! The man had a piece of rebar go through his brain. It sounds like whoever made the decision about the blood thinners is just being paranoid.”

“Well I don’t mind them being paranoid as long as my brother’s safe,” Maddie declared. “It’s better than him getting cut on a scene and bleeding out.”

“Pretty sure if there was a situation where he was at risk of bleeding out from a cut, everyone would have more things to worry about than whether or not he was on blood thinners,” Josh pointed out. Maddie wished he’d stop being logical. “The blood thinners just make him bleed longer, not faster. Right?”

“I guess.”

“So maybe Dan’s right.”

Maddie just wanted Buck to be careful and safe, was that so wrong?

“I don’t know how to help him,” she said, switching topics a little. “And he seemed so lost when I was over there. I don’t know if I should just give him a swift kick in the butt and get him back out there, or hug him and tell him that it’s all gonna be okay.”

“There’s no rule that says you can’t do both,” Josh pointed out.

“True,” she agreed with a sigh, sitting down at her station. She picked up her earpiece and put it on, answering the call that was coming through. “Nine-One-One, what’s your emergency…?”

Daniel had two bowls sitting on the kitchen table and was about to pour the off brand fruit loops into both of them, because Buck was going to get out of bed to eat even if he needed to be dragged, when the door to the apartment opened. Months ago, shortly after Buck came home from the hospital with his leg in that cast, Daniel begrudgingly agreed to give certain people copies of their key to lessen the risk of Buck killing himself to let them in. People like Eddie, for example.

“Hi, Danny!” was Chris’ cheerful greeting.

“Morning, Chris.” Daniel set down the box of cereal and fixed both Diazes with a look. “What’s this?”

“Dad said I’m spending the day with Buck!”

“Did he now?”

Eddie ducked his head and shrugged as he closed the apartment door behind them.

“It’s Chris,” the younger man gave as an explanation. “It’s worth a try.”

Daniel couldn’t really argue with that. At this point, he was willing to try just about anything to cheer his baby brother up. Or to at least get him to do more than become one with his mattress.

“I was about to try and rouse the Jabberwock for breakfast,” he said, rounding the table and crossing the living room for Buck’s bedroom door. “Maybe we can get him to cook something for Chris.”

“I’ll, um. I can try getting him up?” Eddie offered.

Daniel paused in front of the door, and then waved the man ahead. Eddie pushed it open and went in like he belonged there, walking right up to Buck’s curled up form and yanking the covers off. Buck immediately let out an irritated huff, grabbing at the blanket and pulling it back up. The next time Eddie pulled it off, he threw it onto the floor with the dirty laundry.

Daniel felt himself smirking as he crossed his arms and leaned against the doorframe.

“Get up,” Eddie said.

“Eds, c’mon,” Buck groaned, burying his head beneath a pillow. “Why?”

“It’s morning. You have things to do.”

“No, I really don’t.”

“You need to get out of here.” Eddie yanked the pillow off, sending it to the floor with the covers. And then he sent its comrades after it before Buck could even contemplate hiding beneath them, too. “Take a walk around the block, get some fresh air.”

“Why?” Buck demanded, but the fire was lacking from his voice. “What’s the point?”

“The point is that your life isn’t over just because you’re not a firefighter.”

“Says the firefighter.”

“You know, the blood clot could’ve killed you,” Eddie said. “But it didn’t. You-”

“Maybe it should have.”

The words were quiet, but they echoed so very loudly in Daniel’s ears.

“What?” Eddie sent Daniel a look of horror, his hand poised above Buck’s shoulder. “What does that mean?”

“I mean, what’s the point?” Buck said again, words still hushed. “I’m useless, Eddie. What’s the point of me?”

Daniel closed his eyes and took a breath, trying and failing to calm himself down. If he ever saw their mother again, it would far too soon. If he ever saw their mother again, his actions would absolutely be justified.

The quiet tapping of crutches approached Daniel from behind. He opened his eyes and saw Chris peering around his leg, smiling brightly into the room.

“Hi, Buck!”

The kid’s voice had Buck sitting up in the bed to stare at him with wide, red-rimmed eyes.

“H- Hey, buddy.” His voice broke a little, turning squeaky. “Uh, what are you doing here?”

“Apparently he’s spending the day with you,” Daniel explained.

“Where’s Carla?”

“She’s in Morongo,” Chris answered.

“Right, yeah,” Eddie said. He shook his head, like he was trying to shake off a cobweb. “Uh, so. So take him out, have some fun. Get out of this room and this apartment.”

Buck stared at Chris for a long moment, throat bobbing as he swallowed. Then he nodded and swung his legs over the side of the bed.

“I’ll- I’ll go take a shower,” he said, pushing himself to his feet.

Oh thank God, Daniel thought but did not say. Maybe Eddie was worth keeping around after all. Just so Chris was kept on hand to lure Buck out of bed, if for no other reason.

“Perfect!” Eddie pulled Buck in for a quick kiss, running a hand along his neck. “I’ve gotta get to work, you guys have fun. Okay?”

He left the room, pausing to give Chris a kiss on the head and to share a loaded look with Daniel, and then he was hurrying out the door. Buck was slower as he gathered up a change of clean clothes and shuffled into the bathroom, but it was far more than what Daniel managed to achieve in the past week.

“Alright, buddy.” Daniel knelt down in front of Chris, who was quickly cementing his permanent place as one of Daniel’s favorite people. “Can I ask you to do something?”

“What is it?”

“Buck’s been very sad lately. So can you do me a really huge favor?” The kid nodded so fast and so hard that Daniel was worried his glasses would fly off, even with the strap keeping them in place. “Your mission, should you choose to accept it, is to help cheer Buck up. You think you can do that?”

“Yeah!”

Daniel smiled at the excitement, patting the tiny shoulder with a hand that dwarfed it.

“Thanks, buddy. I knew I could count on you.”

It wasn’t the first time that someone entrusted Buck with watching their children. He used to babysit Tia Alma’s daughters, Delia and Celia, while she was at work, so it wasn’t like this was an entirely new thing for him. But this was Chris, his boyfriend’s kid, that he was being trusted with for the day. That made things vastly different, especially when all he wanted to do still was curl back up in bed and let the world finally leave him behind.

He hadn’t been up for cooking, but Buck found it very difficult to say no to Chris’ request for pancakes when it was paired with the cutest set of puppy dog eyes. The obvious solution was to go out to eat somewhere, an idea that had Chris cheering and Danny looking relieved, even if he tried to hide it. 

Buck knew that his brother was worried about him, figured that much out from the way Danny was practically begging for him to get out of bed at one point. But what was the point? It didn’t matter what Buck did, or what he didn’t do. He finally fucked up severely enough that he’d ruined the one thing he was supposed to always be good for. And the worst part was that it wasn’t him that would suffer for it. No, he’d failed Danny.

That wasn’t something Buck thought he could ever forgive himself for.

After a breakfast of a full stack of pancakes for the both of them - Danny turned down the invite to join them, citing the desire to get chores done at home - Buck intended for them to hit the movies. That was the plan, anyway, and it was what he’d told Eddie they were going to do. But it turned out that the showings for the movie Chris wanted to see were all sold out, so after a spirited debate, they went with plan B: the Santa Monica Pier. 

Buck called Danny, to once again invite him out with them, and once again he got a negative.

“You’re the one babysitting, kiddo,” Danny said. “I’m not gonna deal with two children today.”

Beneath the California sun, with the ocean breeze in his hair and Chris chattering his ear off as the kid dragged him around, Buck managed to forget about the clots and everything that came with him. For a little while, anyway. They hit the rides at the Pacific Park, the ones that Buck wasn’t too tall for, anyways. Which was something that earned him a good twenty minutes of razzing from Chris. Afterwards, they took a break to eat cotton candy, because a growing boy needed his weight in sugar, and made goofy faces at the camera in a photo booth.

A game booth was their next target, one of those where they had to use a water cannon fixed to the table to hit a target. Buck helped Chris sit down on a stool and crouched behind him.

“Alright, this is for all the marbles,” he told him.

“Buck?”

“Yeah, buddy?”

“I don’t want the marbles.” Chris pointed at the prizes on display. “I want the bear!”

Buck burst out laughing.

“No, it’s just a figure of speech,” he explained “Now, you ready? I’ll hold here, you hold here…”

They, of course, won. Buck had mad skills at the fair games, he had to say. While the guy running the game fetched the giant stuffed bear that Chris demanded, Buck looked around. His attention was caught by the sight of paramedics attending to a guy in an alligator suit. And just like that, all the good cheer of the day vanished in a puff of smoke.

He was so focused on what the paramedics were doing, on what he couldn’t do, that he missed Chris calling his name at first.

“Sorry, here,” Buck said, his attention still half-on the firefighters even as he took the bear from him. “I’ve got it.”

“Are you okay?” Chris asked, sweet and concerned.

“The universe is mocking me,” Buck explained, trying his best to keep any bitterness from making an appearance. “Let’s go feed the fish.”

They found a bench at the end of the pier, along the railing. Buck got the bear situated, trusting the toy with Chris’ crutches and backpack, and then helped the kid climb up so he could peer over the railing. Buck kept a firm grip on Chris’ shirt as he sat down next to him.

“You ever think about what you want to do with your life?” Buck asked him. “What you want to be when you grow up?”

“Astronaut,” Chris answered. “Or a pirate.”

“Good choices. Cool outfits, too.”

“No, wait,” the kid said. “A firefighter!”

“Yeah, me too.” Buck tried to ignore how bitter the words tasted as they left his mouth. “But, Chris, if those career paths don’t work out, I… I hope you find something you love. Something that makes you feel like you matter, like you’re useful. I hope you find out who you are and what you’re meant to do, and I hope you get to keep that. And I hope it never gets taken away from you.”

Chris turned his head to frown down at him, a frown that was far too heavy for a kid his age. And then raised his tiny little hand, cupped Buck’s jaw with it, and said,

“You’re gonna be okay, kid.”

Buck smiled up at him, his heart feeling quite similar to how he imagined the Grinch’s did at the end of the movie. Out of the corner of his eye, he could see the ocean.

No, that wasn’t right. Out of the corner of his eye, Buck could see the ocean floor.

Slowly, with terror filling his bones, Buck stood up from the bench to get closer to the railing, his hand remaining fastened to Chris’ shirt. Around them, the crowd of people were also staring out at the ocean, at the lack of ocean, muttering in confusion and concern.

“Where did all the water go?” Chris asked.

Buck didn’t answer. He threw Chris over his shoulder and began to run, screaming for everyone else to run, too, while already knowing there was no hope. Behind them, the wave came barrelling towards the shore.

Chapter End Notes

That's a good place to leave off, right? =)

Chapter 4

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Buck’s bedroom wasn’t the messiest that Daniel had ever seen it, there had been days in El Paso that he contemplated calling the CDC, but it was very much a mess. Typically, normally, Daniel would leave it how it was, dirty clothes strewn across the floor and all, and let Buck deal with it. If he ran out of clean underwear, that was entirely on him. But circumstances these last few months were neither typical nor normal, and so Daniel was doing something he hadn’t done since they were both still kids in Hershey.

“How bad is it?” Alma asked, voice coming through the speaker of Daniel’s phone. He had it pinned between shoulder and ear as he gathered up laundry. “Do I need to make another trip?”

“Alma, no,” Daniel protested. He shook a pillow free of its case, tossing it - naked - onto the pile of others before dropping the case into the basket of clothes. “The girls are both in the dorms, you’ve got an empty nest. You should be taking advantage of that by doing nasty things with Iggy.”

“I can bring Iggy with me and we can do nasty things in a hotel room there, instead,” she said. “But seriously, Dan. You sounded worried when you called me. How bad is it?”

Daniel sat back on his heels with a sigh, running one hand through his hair and taking hold of his phone with the other.

“I told you how depressed he’s been,” he said. “And it’s- It’s bad.”

“How bad? Whole tub of rocky road to himself, bad? Or fleeing the state for the next three years, bad?”

“He…” Daniel rubbed at his eye with a thumb. “When Eddie pointed out- I think he was trying to get Evan to focus on the positives, how he was lucky that the blood clot didn’t kill him, y’know? And Evan said… He said that maybe it should have.”

There was silence on the other end of the line, broken only by a quiet,

“Oh.”

“Yeah,” Daniel agreed. “That bad.”

“Where is he now?”

“Santa Monica Pier,” he said. “Eddie lured him out with Chris. I’m using this time to make his bedroom habitable again. And try to figure out what the hell I’m gonna do.”

“The offer of visiting again is still on the table,” Alma reminded him. “Not just for Evan. If you need me there, I’ll be there.”

“Thanks.” The word came out of Daniel’s mouth a bit hushed, a bit choked. “But uh, depending on how things go, maybe we’ll come visit you.”

“Are you sure?” Alma asked, sounding dubious. “The two of you left El Paso for a reason.”

“Well, he’s dating Eddie again, or at least they’re doing something like it.” Daniel got up and yanked the fitted sheet off Buck’s mattress, balling it up and tossing it into the laundry basket. “And avoiding him was half the reason we never went back.”

“Uh-huh. And what about the people you were avoiding?”

“It’s not like I’m talking about moving back.” He juggled the phone a bit as he hefted up the laundry basket and balanced it on his hip. “I just don’t know what else to do, except get him away for a bit. Have a change in scenery.”

“Well you both always have a place to stay with me, you know that.”

“Thanks, Tia.”

They didn’t chat for much longer after that, the both of them hanging up to get finished with the various household chores they were tackling. Daniel sat the phone down on top of the drier as he dumped Buck’s clothes into the washer, thankful that his brother’s preference for the bright and colorful meant that there weren’t many whites to separate out. He’d just thrown in a detergent pod and started the cycle when his phone gave a low drone with an SMS warning.

A tsunami warning.

For Santa Monica.

Daniel stared at it for a moment, and then another, unable to tear his eyes away from what it said. And then, with a great inhale of breath, he scooped up the phone and ran for the front door, almost tripping over himself as he scrambled to put on his shoes without slowing down.

When he tried to call his brother, it just went to voicemail.

The calls so far had not been very exciting, nothing worth writing home about. At the moment, Eddie and the others were finishing up with a food truck fire. Judging from the name on the food truck - Dogtown - along with the pictures, Eddie was gonna guess that it used to sell hot dogs. At the moment, it wasn’t gonna be selling anything.

There was a lot of standing water that they were splashing through. Had they needed that much for such a small fire?

“Any word on how Operation Buck Up Buck is going?” Hen asked, taking advantage of the lull. She, like the others, had been very approving when he came in and told them that he managed to lure Buck out by throwing Chris at him.

Eddie hadn’t told them about what Buck had said, though, because… Well, he wasn’t entirely certain why he hadn’t. Except for maybe the way he was trying very hard not to think about it, especially not while he was at the station. Hearing those words leave Buck’s mouth made him want to sit quietly in a dark room and stare at the wall for a while.

Hen’s question was a good distraction from that train of thought, and Eddie dug out his phone to show both her and Chimney the picture Buck sent of himself and Chris at some diner.

“This was this morning,” he told them. “A full stack of pancakes, and then I think he said maybe the movies. By now, Chris has come down from a serious sugar high, and Buck is regretting his life choices.”

Mostly, Eddie was just glad to see Buck smiling. Hell, he’d deal with Chris on a sugar high, and the come down that came with it, every day if it meant Buck was in a brighter mood.

 “Hey, did someone leave a hydrant open!?” Bobby called out, looking around.

In Eddie’s hand, his phone buzzed and let out a droning tone. On the screen, an SMS warning for a tsunami popped up over the faces of Buck and Chris.

“I don’t think it’s a hydrant, Cap,” he said, holding his phone up so Bobby could see it.

The captain’s face went grim.

“Alright, everyone, finish packing everything up,” Bobby ordered. “Double time it. We’re about to be very busy.”

Eddie nodded with the others, stuffing his phone back into the pocket of his turnout and hurrying to finish rolling up the hose they’d used.

Thank God, he thought, that Buck and Chris were both inland and safe.

If Buck thought the universe was mocking him before, with firefighters responding to a downed man mere feet away from him, then he had no idea what his current situation was. Not particularly good, for a start.

When he realized what was happening, what was coming, Buck grabbed Chris and tried to run. When he realized that running was useless, he’d tried to find shelter. Except that had been useless, too, in the face of the ocean itself decided to kill everything. Their shelter might as well have been made of cardboard and tissue paper, that’s about how well it held up against the wave. And for all that Buck tried to hold on to him, Chris was still torn from his arms.

Somehow, though, they were both still alive. Somehow, though, Chris managed to find a still standing lamp post to cling onto, long enough for Buck to get to him. Somehow, though, they’d found a fire engine to clamber on top of.

If He existed, then God had a sick and twisted sense of humor. But Buck wasn’t exactly going to criticize Him for anything at that moment.

Chris’ crutches were lost to the water, left behind with the giant stuffed bear when Buck ran for their lives. Buck’s phone had been lost, too, yanked out of his pocket by the current. He still miraculously had his wallet and his keys, though he wasn’t sure how much help the latter would be. From the looks of how far inland they’d ended up, he was pretty sure the jeep was a loss, as well. He was not looking forward to arguing with his car insurance over it.

Chris’ glasses had survived, at least. Buck would have to ask where Eddie got the strap for them, because they’d stayed on the entire time. It deserved the best review: 5/5 stars, completely unbothered by Poseidon throwing a tantrum, would recommend to friends and family.

“You good, Chris?” Buck asked, panting a little, as he pressed his face against the kid’s wet curls. Said face stung in places, long cuts irritated by salt water. They weren’t gushing blood, though, which he was absolutely going to bring up when they got out of this.

See, Bobby? See, Maddie? Being on blood thinners didn’t mean he’d die from a papercut. He could still be a firefighter. He could still be useful.

“Yep,” Chris chirped. “I took surfing lessons.”

“Maybe you can teach me,” Buck said, laughing a little breathlessly. He pushed himself up a little, onto his knees, and took a look around their tiny island. “It looks like we’re gonna have to hang out here for a little longer.”

“We have a firetruck,” Chris happily informed him.

“Yeah, we do.”

“Help!” That was a woman, somewhere… Over there! She was over there, clinging as best she could to the side of a building. When she saw Buck was looking, she lifted an arm to wave. “Over here! Help me!”

“I’ll help! I- I’ll come for you!” Buck shouted at her, even as his mind tried working through the problem. He wasn’t sure how, exactly, he was going to get to her. “Chris, I- I need you to sit right here for me, okay? Can you promise me that?”

“Yeah.” Chris nodded, wet curls bouncing a little.

“Thank you, buddy. You’re perfect.”

How was he going to do this, though? There was quite a bit of distance between him and her, cut off by rushing currents. There were cars sticking out of the water like tiny islands, and trees and poles that were still standing. It all reminded Buck a bit of the old platformer games he used to play at Eddie’s house, before they found better things to do in the privacy of a bedroom.

But what did Buck have on hand? This wasn’t the 118’s engine, so it was slightly different from what he was used to. Some things were universal, though, such as the coils of hoses, and there was an extension ladder.

He eyed the distance to the nearest car island. Yeah, that should work.

While the lady kept yelling for his help and for him to hurry - couldn’t she see that he was trying, damn it? - he stretched the ladder between the engine and the nearest car. Then he slung one of the hoses over his shoulder, one of its ends attached firmly to the engine, and made the precarious climb across. From there, he had to swim across a gap to another car, stuck on something so it was lifted out of the water, where he found that she was not the only person in trouble.

“Help!” the man in the driver seat begged. “I can’t get out.”

“I have to get to her first, okay?” Buck told him. This guy was staying put, after all, and wasn’t in immediate danger. “I’ll come back and get you out, I promise.”

He knew he couldn’t get to the woman. She was too far upstream, and the current too strong, and the hose keeping him anchored to the engine was too short. That wasn’t the plan, though. Instead of trying to swim to her, he dove into the water and swam across the road-turned-river for one of the trees. Sturdy things that they were, this one was standing tall and stubborn despite all the debris that had gathered around it.

Buck tied the free end of the hose to its branch, making sure the knot was secured, leaving it stretched across the water.

“You can let go!” Buck shouted at her. “Let go, you’ll be okay!”

After several seconds where he could tell she struggled with the idea, she did as he bid. Buck used the hose to pull himself against the current to the middle of the water, shouting encouragement to her the entire time. When she got close enough, he reached out his hand and snagged her by the arm, pulling her close.

Buck kept the litany of encouragements going as he pulled the both of them to the engine. Once there, he helped her climb onto the top and then paddled over to check on Chris. The kid seemed perfectly content to be sitting there, watching. At least until he raised an arm and pointed with a tiny finger.

“Buck! People!”

Buck turned his head, spotting them and hearing their cries for help. A dozen of them, maybe, all caught in the current and being dragged along for the ride.

“Hey,” he said, getting the attention of the woman he rescued. “Will you watch him?”

She nodded, and Buck got to work.

The day had been hellish, and it wasn’t getting better. Maddie remembered, when she was first starting at dispatch, how one of the former dispatchers, Gloria, had been broken by what she called “dead-end calls.” The ones where you could hear everything happening on the other end and knowing there was nothing you could do. Nothing except sit there and listen to someone die.

Maddie had taken so many of those calls today, calls where she tried and she tried to get them to safety, only to get stuck with damning silence. And then the call ended, the next one came in, and she got to do it all over again. The others weren’t having it any better: Linda, one of her coworkers, was on the line for twenty minutes without any word from her caller, because she refused to hang up, to give up. She’d argued with Sue about it, until the supervisor did it for her.

After that, Sue tasked Maddie with putting her former skills as an ER nurse to use and start triaging. Not the calls, but the dispatchers. To step in and hang up for them, if she had to. To yank them off the floor and force them to take a break. To do her best to keep them from breaking just like Gloria had.

The biggest problem they were being faced with at the moment was that hospital space was currently at a premium. They could send as many ambulances out into this mess as they wanted, but the ambulance had nowhere to take their patients. St Mary’s in Culver, UCLA, it seemed like all the hospitals in the city were filled beyond capacity with people in need of medical care.

She was appreciative of the distraction when her phone buzzed with an incoming call from Chimney.

“Hey, you guys okay?”
 “We’re all fine, but I’m hoping you can help me cut through some red tape,” he said. “We keep pulling Level Ones out of the water and we have no place to send ‘em.”

Ugh.

“We’re sending victims to every dry hospital we can find,” she told him. “We have a system in place.”

“Well, whoever’s in charge of that system is doing a terrible job.”

Maddie paused on the way to what had become mission control, glowering at nothing even though she knew Chimney couldn’t see it.

“That would be me.”

There was an awkward silence on the other end, and then,

“A terribly good job under extremely difficult circumstances.” That was much better. “So, what, the hospitals recall all their staff?”

“It’s not about staff, they’re flooded with staff.” Maybe not the best word usage at the moment. “It’s about beds and space. What we need is a field hospital.”

“A triage, closer to the water. Stabilize and move ‘em inland as beds open up.”

“Wait.” Maddie tapped her finger on one of the maps someone had spread out over a table. There were red lines drawn across it, crude estimates of how far in-land the water now reached. “The VA hospital on Sawtelle, it’s not far from where you are now and it’s out of the flood zone.”

“Why aren’t we sending patients there now?”

“Because it was decommissioned two months ago.”

“So it’ll be totally empty,” Chimney said. “See, I said you were doing a good job.”

“Uh-huh. I’ll talk to Sue, see if we can make this happen.”

“Sounds good, but one other thing: How are you?”

“Honestly?” Maddie glanced around. “Happy to hear your voice. And weirdly grateful that Buck has a serious blood clot condition and is nowhere near this nightmare. Is that wrong?”

“Not at all,” Chimney assured her.

“Maddie.”

That was Sue’s voice. Maddie spotted her near the elevators, standing next to-

“Gotta go,” Maddie said, hanging up on Chimney and rushing over.

She hadn’t seen Daniel cry since before Buck was born, she was pretty sure, but he was crying now. Tears streaming silently down his face, broad shoulders shaking. He looked… Broken, that was a good word for it. He looked broken.

“Dan?” she said, sharing a concerned look with Sue. “What are you doing here?”

“I- I can’t get to him,” he said, voice cracking. “All the roads are blocked, and- and his phone’s going to voicemail, and I can’t get to him, and I don’t know where else to go.”

“Get to who? Whose phone?”

“Buck,” he said, and Maddie felt something shatter within her. “He- He took Chris to the pier, and now he’s not answering his phone, and I couldn’t get to him, Maddie. I- I can’t-”

Fuck. Fuck, fuck, fuck.

“Alright, let’s take a seat over here,” Sue said, taking over for her. And Maddie was so very thankful for that, because she wasn’t sure what to do here.

The older woman urged Daniel to the break room and into a chair, Maddie’s brother looking so very young at that moment. Young and lost, and Maddie had no idea how to make this better, because all she wanted to do was rush to the flood zone, too. But if Daniel couldn’t manage it, then she knew that she definitely couldn’t either.

Fuck.

Fuck, fuck, fuck.

“Did you talk to her already?” She hadn’t even realized she’d called Chimney, not until his voice was in her ear again. “That was fast-”

“Chimney.” The seriousness of her tone shut him up. “Buck took Chris to the pier. And Dan can’t get a hold of him.”

“...Fuck.”

Chapter End Notes

The problem with the tsunami arc is that there is no problem with it. This episode is a perfect hour of television and I feel awful for sullying it with my dirty hands.

Also, in canon, the dramatic irony is everyone think Buck&Chris are perfectly safe, when they're absolutely not. The dramatic irony here is that Daniel thinks Buck&Chris are dead, when they're actually fine. For the moment.

Chapter 5

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

How many people can you fit atop a standard fire engine? It sounded like the start to a word problem from a high school math class, or maybe a riddle, but it was also Buck’s life at the moment. Currently, there were maybe a dozen bedraggled survivors crammed onto the back of the 136’s engine, clinging to each other and doing their best not to focus on the destruction around them.

How do you keep an eight year old calm in the middle of a disaster? For as many times as they’d encountered kids on a call, Buck didn’t really have an answer to that question. Not that Chris was having much trouble staying cool under pressure, because he was taking to everything with his usual sunny cheer. Buck really wished he could manage that attitude in his everyday life, he truly did.

Still, though, they were stuck for the foreseeable future, until someone came by on a boat or another wave took them away. They needed to do something to stave off the boredom and doldrums. Nevermind that Chris, angel and sunshine personified he may have been, was still a kid; Buck was going to go crazy if they didn’t.

Danny had been stuck with this problem before, Buck remembered, during that road trip to and from Boston, and then when he drove them down to El Paso. Buck as a kid had not been nearly as tolerable as Chris, especially when confined to a small metal box while struggling with the constant need to move, and so Danny needed to break out all sorts of games to keep from killing them both through sheer irritation. They were coming in handy now to keep both Buck and Chris entertained.

“I spy with my little eye,” Buck said, “something that… Moves people around.”

“Um… Oh!” Chris perked up and pointed. “A scooter.”

“Yeah, good job.” He nudged the kid. “Okay, genius, your turn.”

Chris grinned and looked around.

“I spy… A shopping cart!”

“What? No, c’mon.” Buck nudged him again with his elbow. “That’s not how the game works, you can’t just yell stuff out.”

“But yelling stuff out is the fun part!”

Buck shook his head, laughing a little. The others were glancing their way, smiling a little.

“You amaze me, buddy,” he said.

“Why?” Chris asked.

“Because… Because the other day, I lost something very important,” Buck answered. “And I didn’t want to get out of bed for a whole week. But here you are, after the day you’ve had, with a big smile and bustin’ a gut.”

He reached over and poked at Chris’ stomach, making them both laugh again.

“You never gave up,” Buck said. “Even when that water was rushing over you back there, you just kept swimming.”

“Like Dory,” Chris chirped.

“Yeah, like Dory. And not just today, y’know, but every day. You never say no, you never complain. How do you do that?”

“I complained once,” Chris said, sounding so very wise for someone so small. Like a bespectacled Yoda. “It didn’t work.”

“So what did you do?”

“Just kept swimming.”

“Like Dory…”

One of the people Buck rescued gave a shout, soon joined by the others. Buck sat up, looked for where they were pointing, and felt his stomach drop. The water was bringing more than debris, now: there were more people in the water. They weren’t struggling, they weren’t shouting or splashing. They were simply floating.

“H- Hey, Chris.” Buck moved the both of them so the kid’s back was to the grim scene. “Um…”

He needed to do something to keep Chris suitably distracted as the bodies passed by. More I Spy? Another game? Or- Oh.

“Alice was starting to get very tired of sitting by her sister and having nothing to do,” Buck recited. Chris looked up at him, head tilted, clearly recognizing the words. “A couple of times she’d looked into the book her sister was reading, but it had no pictures or conversations in it.”

Danny had read him the story so many times, growing up, and Buck had read it himself so many times more. He wasn’t certain how much of the words he could remember, but it didn’t have to be the entire thing. It just had to be enough of it.

“And what’s the use of a book, Alice thought, without pictures or conversations?” Buck continued, reaching up to cup the sides of Chris’ face and making sure he couldn’t see anything in the water. “So she wondered…”

Hen’s expression, when Chimney gave her the news, was filled with the same amount of horror that he felt when Maddie said the words. The moment he’d hung up, Chimney ran through the tents and crowds of bustling paramedics to where he’d last seen his friend. 

“Buck was on the pier with Chris?” she repeated. “The pier that’s no longer there?”

“That’s what Dan told her,” Chimney said. “He didn’t say much else, except that Buck wasn’t answering his phone.”

“Shit.” Hen covered her mouth, half-turning to look over the destruction wrought by the wave. “Shit.”

“I used stronger language,” he said, reaching for his radio. Hen’s hand suddenly gripping his wrist stopped him. “What? We need to tell Bobby. We need to tell Eddie.”

“Over the radio?” Hen asked. “You want to tell Eddie, over the radio, that his boyfriend and son might both be…?”

She didn’t say the word, but they both still grimaced beneath the weight of it.

“He needs to know, Hen!”

“I agree, but not like that,” she said. “And we don’t know what he’s doing right now. The moment we tell him, that’ll be all he can focus on. It’d be all I could focus on, if it were me.”

Hen was right, and Chimney hated it. Because they didn’t know what Eddie was doing at that moment. He could be in the middle of treating a patient, or working to get someone free from debris, or some other action on which someone’s life hung in the balance. Telling Eddie now would distract him enough that someone might die. And unfortunately, at the moment, there wasn’t exactly a private line available to them so they could tell Bobby alone.

“So what do we do then?” he asked, because he had no damn clue.

“We wait until we run into them in person,” Hen said. “Or maybe until Bobby radios in. We’ll know if they’re in the middle of something then, and maybe he can tell Eddie himself.”

“Okay,” Chimney agreed, nodding. “But what if they find them before then?”

“Then hopefully they’ll be able to tell us that everything’s fine and we’re freaking out for no reason.”

Though neither of them said it, they both knew that was probably the least likely of outcomes.

Eddie was still trying to wrap his head around what his eyes were telling him. Ahead, as he and Bobby rode on one of the Zodiac lifeboats, was the sign for the Santa Monica yacht harbor. Around them should have been a park, restaurants, a children’s museum that Buck took them to in celebration of his leg being cast free. None of it was visible now, though. The sign for the harbor was jutting out of the water, the same for the Ferris wheel just beyond it.

They were going down Colorado Avenue in a boat, because the ocean had decided to claim everything for itself.

(Thank God, he thought, not for the first time that day and definitely not for the last, that Buck and Chris had gone to the movies. They were probably having the time of their lives, or so Eddie hoped.)

As they got closer to the Ferris wheel, they found makeshift rafts of floating debris. They were tangled up in ropes and strung together like beads, gently bobbing with the motion of the waves. There were people clinging to some of them, and more on the Ferris wheel itself, and they all started shouting as the boats drew near.

One of them, a small family of three, was speaking in Spanish, so Eddie responded in kind.

“[Did you all do this?]” he asked them.

“[No, it wasn’t any of us,]” the father answered. Then he pointed up above them, at one of the Ferris wheel’s carriage. “[It was the firefighter.]”

“Who helped them?” Bobby asked.

“La bombera,” Eddie answered, the phrase the man had used.

“Hey!” A woman’s voice, from up above. “We could use a hand up here!”

Eddie couldn’t see all of her, his view mostly blocked by the carriage she was leaning out of, but he could tell she was in an LAFD uniform. It looked like they’d located one of the missing firefighters from the 136.

“Fire lady,” he translated for Bobby.
