“Alright, grab an extra harness, rope, pulleys, figure eight plates,” the captain instructed. “I’ll coordinate evac and transport some down here.”

“Sounds like a plan, Cap.”

Grabbing the bags of extra gear, Eddie began to scale the Ferris wheel. He was reminded of Buck’s story about Devon and the rollercoaster, along with the accompanying videos. It was a precarious climb, one he made with his heart in his throat the entire time. When he reached the carriage with the firefighter in it, he tossed the bags inside and then had to scramble for a better hold when things groaned and tilted.

“That’s not part of the ride,” Eddie joked, hiding his nerves.

“No,” la bombera agreed. “The spokes are coming off the hub. This thing’s been thrashed.”

“What about you? Are you hurt?”

“No” Mostly a truth, maybe; there were cuts and scrapes all over her face. “And neither is he.”

She was talking about the obviously terrified man clinging to the central pole of the carriage.

“I’m not going anywhere,” the guy said. “Not until the water’s gone.”

“Sir, if you don’t come with us,” la bombera said, “this whole thing will be gone.”

“Don’t worry, buddy,” Eddie told him, get the gear ready to help everyone down. “I’m gonna get you down safely. I’m just gonna put this harness around you, okay?”

Eddie didn’t actually give the guy a choice, he just started strapping him into the harness.

“Eddie Diaz, One Eighteen,” he said, introducing himself as he worked.

“Lena Bosko,” la bombera said. “One Thirty-six.”

“You were on the pier when it hit.” He was stating the obvious, he knew that. “Where’s your crew?”

“Got separated,” Lena answered. “It was a helluva spin cycle.”

Getting the terrified civilian down to Bobby and the boats was not as easy as it sounded. Especially not when the Ferris wheel shifted and rumbled beneath them as they lowered him down. Afterwards, he helped Lena slip into the spare gear and then the two of them free climbed to another carriage with another person in need of their help.

That one required an evac by way of Coast Guard helicopter. Done in the nick of time, too, because suddenly the ocean changed directions. Below them, Bobby shouted out a warning about debris coming their way. It was a wall of it, washed out to sea with no care of what was in the way.

Eddie and Lena, with little choice, leaped into the water to be scooped up by Bobby in the Zodiac. They zoomed off for the relative safety of the shore as the Ferris wheel gave one long, final moan, and then collapsed.

There were very few positives in Maddie’s life right now. One of them was that the use of the VA as a field hospital had been approved, and that it would soon be operational. Even if it meant Maddie had to request for body bags - so many body bags - to be sent over. 5000 to start with, Sue had said. To start with. Meaning she fully expected them to run out and need more.

That was a number of dead that Maddie’s mind was having trouble comprehending. It was made worse when she realized that it would only be the number of recovered bodies. Because sometimes, when the ocean took something, it never gave it up.

One other positive right now was that Daniel, at least, was where she could see him. Daniel, at least, was sitting there alive and whole and unharmed. It was more than she could say about Buck. She hadn’t given herself time to dwell on it, busying herself with everyone else’s needs.

But every time she stopped for a moment, every time she saw Daniel…

Daniel had not moved from the chair Sue put him in. He’d stopped crying some time ago, but he hadn’t gotten up and he hadn’t spoken. He was just staring down at his knees, hands laying limply in his lap. It was a complete flip from how he’d been the night of the ladder truck bombing.

That night, Daniel had been a caged tiger. Pacing, growling. He’d bitten the heads off everyone that came near and ran off two nurses that couldn’t tell him what he wanted to know. It was the night that Austin Jones earned Maddie’s respect, because the man had weathered her ill-tempered brother without even blinking an eye. Whatever the hell the two of them had going on, Maddie very much approved of him.

But now, ever since Daniel told her about Buck, the middle Buckley sibling was… Silent. Still. Detached from the world around him.

Maddie was almost jealous.

A wailing alarm drew her attention from one disaster to the other. On one of the screens of their impromptu mission control, the visual of the flood zone showed movement again. On another screen, the news showed video clips of the waters and the destruction they had wrought to the city.

“We’re getting reports of another surge,” an anchor reported. “Our correspondent…”

“Oh, God, no,” Maddie whispered. “Not another wave.”

“No, same wave,” Sue said, coming to stand beside her. “It’s just going in the other direction.”

“Back out to sea.”

Sometimes, when the ocean took something, it never gave it up.

Apparently, impressively, Buck knew more of Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland by heart than he initially assumed. Not all of it, but a large portion. Not that it mattered, because it served its purpose of distracting Christopher from the grimness of the reality around them. They were currently sitting on the truck, everyone bored and doing their best to keep their spirits up.

“I don’t know what I’m gonna tell your father,” Buck told Chris. “He trusts me to take you out one time on my own, and uh… Look what happens.”

He’d never taken Celia or Delia out anywhere, except occasionally the park near the building they lived in. Usually, though, they were perfectly content with remaining in Alma’s apartment, forcing Buck to play with dolls and do tea parties and act the part of model for their make-up talents. Nothing had ever happened when he was trusted with them.

Next time, if there was a next time, the two of them were going to stay inside and make a pillow fort in the living room. 

“You saved me,” Chris told him, tucking his head against Buck’s chest. “And you saved them.”

Buck hid his smile in the kid’s curls.

“No, we did that together,” he said. “You and me, we make a great team. I’m proud of you, gimme a high five.”

He held up his hand. Chris grinned up at him and slapped his palm against it.

“I just figured it out,” one of the people he rescued said. The first one, actually, the woman he’d tasked with helping keep an eye on Chris. “Where I remember you from.”

“Uh.” Buck shared a wide-eyed look with Chris. “Oh?”

God, please don’t let her be one of the catfisher victims. His luck today had been bad enough as it was, that would just be added cruelty.

“You were the guy under the firetruck,” she said.

“Oh, uh. Yeah.”

“I was there, y’know,” she continued. She had her back against the railing, her knees drawn up to her chest. “I was uh, I was one of the people that helped lift it off.”

Oh. Buck didn’t really know what to say to that, except that he knew he needed to say something. So he wet his lips and gave a very lame,

“Uh, thanks. I appreciate it.”

The woman threw back her head and laughed.

“You appreciate it? You kept me from drowning,” she said. “Save your thanks, I won the karmic jackpot.”

That had the others laughing, too. Until the waters began rushing again with debris, beating against the fire engine and rocking it to and fro. At the tail end of the vehicle, the rocking sent someone overboard. While the people nearest did their best to fish him back out, Buck pulled Chris close to him and hunkered down.

The water brought with it more people, screaming for help as they were dragged out to sea. They banged against the cab and Buck crawled over the roof to try and drag them up.

The engine shifted, rocked violently.

Behind him, there was a splash.

The woman he’d been talking to let out a scream.

Buck shoved himself back, frantically looking around and shouting Chris’ name. But the kid was nowhere to be found, no sign of his presence to be seen. He’d gone into the water.

Buck didn’t think, not anything beyond the fact that Chris was gone (gone, gone, gone),  he simply acted.

He dove into the water after him.

Chapter End Notes

Fun fact about this universe: When I was planning things out at the beginning, I almost had Daniel be at the 136 intead of the 133. Also, Buck trying to recite Alice's Adventures in Wonderland from memory while they're stuck on the engine was another scene that I'd been envisioning since Spare Parts.

Chapter 6

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

The ocean water splashed up Eddie’s leg as he stepped out of the raft, not that it made much difference with how soaked through he was. He grabbed his bag and swung it over his shoulder while Bosko did the same beside him, though not as smoothly. A ghost of a grimace crossed her face. Several feet ahead of them, Bobby stood talking over the radio. The captain’s back was to Eddie, but he saw the way his shoulders sagged and his head bowed.

He lifted it when he heard Eddie and Bosko coming closer, turning to face them with a blank expression on his face.

“Cap?” Eddie asked, feeling a mite worried, now.

“Our team’s still stuck on the other side of everything,” Bobby said, something that did not explain his general air of grimness. “Our trucks can’t get through just yet. Earth movers are still clearing the roads.

“One Eighteen’s hoofing it for a while.”

“Seems it,” the captain agreed. “Hen and Chimney are readying turnout backpacks until we’re mobile again. Bosko, your crew from your house are all alive and accounted for except for Captain Cooper. He’s still MIA.”

“Well, if it’s all the same to you, Captain,” Bosko said. “I’m gonna stick around and look for him.”

“Why are you holding yourself like that?”

Bosko was standing a bit stiffly, Eddie saw. Like merely existing at this moment was causing her pain. She was also trying, and failing, to hide the way she so very clearly wanted to cradle her torso with her arms. She’d start to wrap them around herself, only to catch herself at the last minute and drop them back to her sides.

“It’s nothing,” Bosko claimed. “Bruised rib.”

That could explain it, but…

“Let me take a look at it,” Eddie offered.

“I said it’s nothing.”

“Clearly, it’s not.”

“Are you going to drop this?” Bosko asked, face twisting into a scowl.

“Hey,” Bobby cut in. “Let him have a look. I’m not asking.”

Eddie knelt down in front of her, ignoring both the water and the way she rolled her eyes and glowered like Buck at his moodiest. He took two fingers and, none too gently, pressed them against the side that he figured was the “bruised rib.” She jumped with a curse as he felt what was definitely more than a bruise.

“Yeah, pretty sure that’s broken,” Eddie announced, standing back up. “Must be hurting like a bitch.”

“Yeah, well.” Bosko tried to shrug and looked like she regretted it immediately. “Breathing isn’t super fun.”

“I’m calling it,” Bobby said. He half-turned, waving at an incoming truck. It was high enough, with large enough tires, to get through the standing water, and someone attached an impromptu bulldozer blade on the front to move things out of the way. Eddie thought it might have been a tow truck in a past life. “You’re off the field.”

“Sir, I said I’m fine,” Bosko argued, even though she very clearly was not.

“USAR Command is setting up at the VA hospital on Sawtelle, you’re both going there,” Bobby told them. Then he looked at Eddie, straight at him, for the first time that entire conversation. He seemed to droop as he stood there. “Eddie, I need to tell you something, but you have to promise that you’ll take Bosko to the VA and that you’ll stay there.”

“Cap?”

“Promise me,” Bobby repeated. “I will make it an order, if I have to.”

“Fine, yeah, I- I promise.”

The captain stared at him, clearly not believing him. Though Eddie wasn’t sure why. Why wouldn’t he stay at the VA if he needed to? He was a medic, he regularly stepped in to fill the gaps for Hen and Chimney, and they would undoubtedly need all the medics they could get at the VA. Why wouldn’t he stay there if needed?

“Have you heard from Buck?” Bobby asked.

Eddie was momentarily thrown by the odd question.

“Uh, not since that picture of their breakfast,” he answered. “They went to the movies. I’m not sure what they’re doing now. Why?”

Bobby knew all this, didn’t he? He’d been there when Eddie was talking to Hen and Chimney, before this mess started.

“According to Maddie,” Bobby said, “Daniel showed up at Dispatch, upset because he couldn’t make it through the traffic and roadblocks to get here.”

“You just said the earth movers are still clearing the roads,” Eddie pointed out. “Wait, why was he trying to get here? Was he called in?”

Danny had completed his paramedic courses in the spring, right before the ladder truck bombing. Just like it would make sense for Eddie to be needed at the field hospital, he imagined the same would be true for Danny. 

Off to the side, Bosko was looking between them with a confused frown. She didn’t know who any of the people were that they were talking about, and so was obviously lost.

Bobby opened his mouth again, paused, let out a breath and drew in another. Readying himself for something terrible.

“Buck didn’t take Chris to the movies,” he said, and Eddie was already shaking his head in denial before the man said the next part. “They were at-”

“No,” Eddie cut him off. “No.”

“Daniel said-”

“Danny’s wrong,” he snapped, swinging his bag off his shoulder to dig out his phone in its waterproof pouch. Buck’s number was high in his contacts. “Come on, come-”

“Hey, it’s Buck! You can leave a message, but a text would be better!”

“Fuck!”

The second and third time calling got the same result, with Eddie being sent to voicemail without it giving him the courtesy of even pretending to ring. He stuffed his phone back into the bag, and made it two steps before Bobby was stopping him.

“Let me go,” he snarled. “I need to find them.”

“I told you I would make it an order,” Bobby reminded him, keeping a firm grip on Eddie’s arm. It reminded him far too much of that night Buck got hurt, with him being held back from rushing forward. “Do you even know where to look?”

A venomous glare was Eddie’s answer. Because no, he didn’t know where to look. But he was perfectly willing to look everywhere if he had to.

“They’ll send everyone through the VA,” Bobby said. “You’re more likely to find them there.”

“Cap-”

“Eddie. Take Bosko to the VA, and then the both of you stay there. We’ll keep an eye out while we work, but you can’t be out here with us. And you’ll be more helpful there.”

“Fine,” Eddie bit out, turning on his heel and going for the truck that had pulled to a stop.

“Who’re Buck and Chris?” Bosko asked, sounding hesitant as she followed him.

“My- My partner,” he answered, stumbling over the word. “And my son.”

Bosko fell silent as they clambered onto the truck bed, and Eddie was thankful for it.

Buck wasn’t certain how long he had been walking through the ruined, flooded streets as he called out for Chris. The way his leg ached, the scratchiness of his voice, told him it had been a while. But he didn’t stop, he couldn’t. Chris was out here somewhere, alone and scared, because Buck looked away for a single moment and lost him.

All he’d found so far was Chris’ glasses, tangled up with some fronds from a plant. They hung from Buck’s neck by their strap, a weight that kept him moving forward.

“Hey! There’s a kid under here!”

The woman’s cry had Buck twisting around on instinct. He spotted a group of people surrounding a metal sign for a liquor store that was laying on top of a pile of debris, feet sticking out from under. Buck bounded forward, Chris’ name tearing from his mouth as he felt his heart jump with hope.

“You!” He pointed at one of the men, bigger than the rest of the group. “Me and you, come on.”

They gripped the edges of the sign and, together, leveraged off and to the side. The sharp edges, from where it was shorn off its building, cut into Buck’s arm. His attention, however, was focused on-

Oh. His heart dropped a little as he saw who they just rescued: a teenage girl, bloody and crying. Not Chris.

The others cheered and clapped as they helped the girl to her feet. They started helping her away, no doubt to somewhere safer and drier. Buck swallowed his disappointment, and the guilt that came with it, and waved them down before they got too far. A few of them stopped and turned back to him.

“I’m looking for an eight-year-old boy,” he said, the same as he’d said to every other person he’d encountered since he dove into the water after him. “His name is Christopher. He’s always smiling, and- and he’s got CP, Cerebral Palsy. He’s got brown hair, a yellow shirt…”

“Yeah, yeah, I think I saw him,” one of them, a guy in a wetsuit a few years younger than himself, said.

Buck felt his heart skip a beat.

“Where?” he demanded.

“He was headed with a group to that Cupcakery place,” the guy said. “I heard they’re handing out water.”

“Cupcakery? Wh- What is that?”

“You know, cupcake bakery,” the guy said, and then pointed. “It’s about six or seven blocks south of here on Strand.”

“Thank you,” Buck said, already turning and running through the water in that direction. He called out another “Thank you!” over his shoulder, ignoring the way the cut on his arm stung and the way his leg complained loudly about every step.

He had to find Chris, that was all that mattered.

The sun was fully set, which made the flashing lights surrounding them all the more vibrant. Athena stood in front of a group of other uniformed officers, on the edges of the disaster zone. The wave itself hadn’t made it this far inland, which meant they weren’t in any standing water. It would be worse, she knew, the farther into Santa Monica they went.

The sergeant was giving the patrol officers their marching orders. It mostly amounted to keeping the peace by sheer force of presence, making themselves seen and known to discourage anyone from taking advantage of the chaos. People, she knew, could be wonderful and kind and selfless creatures, but they could also be dumb and cruel and selfish. Especially in the dark, when they could show their true colors without fear of being caught.

“If you see anything that looks like more than a misdemeanor, call for backup,” Athena instructed. “We are not looking the other way on this, and we’re not going to take unneeded risks. Arrests will be made. Understood?”

She waited until everyone nodded and she could be sure that her words were heard.

“Alright, move out,” she said. “Be smart, stay safe.”

Everyone dispersed for their cruiser, ready to do their jobs. Almost everyone, at least. One of them stayed behind, talking to a group of bedraggled civilians: a man, a teenage boy, and a woman carrying a child covered in a red jacket. Athena walked over to see what was up.

“Everything okay, Suze?” she asked the officer.

“Sergeant, they’re asking for help finding their people,” Suze explained. “I tried to tell them...”

Athena nodded, understanding what was going on now.

“Folks, for your own safety, we need you to clear the streets,” she told them. “Make your way to the emergency shelter.”

“My wife is missing,” the man said, sounding angry. Athena couldn’t help glancing at the woman, because she’d assumed- Well, there was that saying about making assumptions. “You’re not going to do anything?”

“We are doing everything that we can,” she said. “There are people at the shelter who can take your name and reconnect you with your wife or whoever is missing.”

The man huffed in anger and stomped off with the boy, presumably his son, as dispatch started requesting Athena’s attention through her radio. The woman lingered behind though.

“Muchas gracias,” the woman said.

“You’re welcome,” Athena replied, grabbing her radio as the woman walked away with the child. “Dispatch, Seven-Two-Seven L Thirty.”

“Multiple reports of possible burglary suspects.”

“Copy that, dispatch, I’ll-” Athena cut herself off, because as the woman passed her by, she bent her head down to the sleeping child and said,

“Maybe we’ll find your Buck at the shelter.”

“What did you just say?” Athena demanded, stomping towards her. “Find who?”

“His Buck,” the woman repeated, staring at her wide-eyed.

Athena reached out and tore the hood off the child’s head, revealing curly brown hair. The boy blinked his eyes open and rolled his head to look at her.

“Athena!”

“Christopher.” She reached for her radio again, gesturing for Suze to stay as she did. “Dispatch, put me through to the One Eighteen.”

The field hospital at the VA was fully set up when Eddie arrived with Bosko, tents raised in the parking lot with people bustling everywhere like lives depended on it. Which, mass casualty event, they most definitely did. There was a nurse with a clipboard, and he practically dragged Bosko over in her direction, eager to drop her off and then ignore Bobby’s orders to stay there.

“Hey!” Eddie waved her down for her attention. “My friend here has at least one broken rib and is showing signs of-”

“Can I get your names for the list, please?” the nurse interrupted.

“You have a list?” Bosko shot forward, snatching the clipboard out of the nurse’s hands. 

Eddie was, admittedly, not far behind her. But thanks to Carla, he knew very well that these things went faster if he paid attention to protocol, so he snatched the clipboard and handed it back to the nurse.

“I’m Eddie DIaz,” he told the nurse, who nodded and started writing it down. “This is Lena Bosko.”

“Have you seen a Ronnie Cooper on the list?” Bosko demanded. “Fire captain, station One Thirty-six.”

“Or an Evan Buckley?” Eddie asked. “Or a Christopher Diaz?”

The nurse started going through the sheets, but she was already shaking her head.

“No,” she told them. “And they don’t sound familiar, either. Go inside, someone will be with you.”

The inside wasn’t any less busy than the parking lot. Cots filled every piece of available space with doctors fluttering over patients. There was one, a woman with a nasty gash on her head, whose hand was clutching at her worried son.

Eddie guided Bosko inside, over to what would have been the nurse’s station if this place was still operational, and waited for someone to get freed up.

“Okay, Diaz, you got your wish, I’m here,” Bosko told him. “You don’t haven’t babysit me.”

“I get it, okay?” Eddie told her. “My- Buck and Chris are both out there, and I want nothing more than to go find them. But my captain told me to stay here, and honestly? He was right, I don’t have a damn clue where to even start looking. They could be anywhere, or they could be…”

The wave had started going out, back to sea, and dragging everything with it. He tried very much not to think about that possibility, a task helped by focusing on the goal of getting Bosko here. But now that they’d arrived, now that he wasn’t putting one foot in front of the other, his mind had room to wonder and imagine.

“So I get it,” he said again, looking past her to the kid with his mother. “I really do.”

Bosko followed his gaze.

“He remind you of your son?”

“A little,” he admitted, frowning at the sound of the boy coughing.

“I’m sorry,” she said. “I’m- I’m sure he’s safe. Maybe he’s on his way here.”

The boy was still coughing, and now he was gagging.

“Kyle, what’s wrong?” his worried mother asked, headwound forgotten.

“I think he’s drowning,” Eddie said, rushing over.

He picked the kid up and laid him on the ground on his side, reaching for his flashlight while holding his mouth open with one hand. There was an obstruction, something sticking partly out of the kid’s throat and blocking his airway. There was no time for niceties, and so Eddie reached in with his fingers to grab it.

When Eddie pulled it out, he found that it was a leaf of some sort. Probably swallowed with the water that Kyle was still coughing up, and then when it started coming back up it got stuck. With it out of the way, tossed onto the ground, the kid was breathing better. Not well, but better.

Eddie looked around and grabbed the nearest O2 tank he saw, placing the mask to Kyle’s face. A nurse came with a make-do stretcher, and they worked to get the kid onto it to be taken away for an x-ray.

“Are you a paramedic?” a doctor, the one who was working on the mother’s head wound, asked.

“I was a medic in the service,” Eddie answered.

“Well, we’re severely shorthanded,” the doctor said. “Any chance that you could-”

“Yeah, sure.” It would be something for him to do, instead of worrying about Chris and Buck. “Just point me to where to wash up.”

The doctor looked relieved.

In Eddie’s pocket, his phone - removed from the water proof pouch - began to ring. He scrambled for it, feeling more than a little hopeful, and answered it without checking the caller ID.

“You don’t have a radio,” Bobby stated.

“No,” Eddie said. “Why? What’s…?

“Athena found Chris.” At the captain’s words, Eddie had to clutch the nearest bed for support as his knees turned to jelly. “He’s fine, an officer is escorting him and the woman he was with to you.”

“The woman… Wh- What about Buck?”

There was silence on the other end, several beats of it.

“Chris doesn’t know where he is,” Bobby finally answered. “They got separated by one of the surges.”

Chapter End Notes

Rewatching this episode makes me realize that Chris is perhaps just as dramatic as his fathers. He's literally within reach of Athena, and he continues to hide beneath the jacket. He ends up on the mail carrier with Chimney and Hen and Bobby, and he says nothing. No, no, I am convinced that he instinctively knew when the most dramatic moment would be, after Buck tells Eddie what happened, and waited until then for the reveal.

Chapter 7

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Christopher’s arrival to the field hospital took far longer than Eddie would have liked, even though he knew they were doubtlessly having to weave around obstacles. When he finally did get there, escorted by two women Eddie didn’t recognize - a hispanic woman named Gabrielle and an officer Chris called “Suze” - Eddie picked him up and refused to set him back down for several minutes, directing the two women to the nurse with the clipboard. He spent the time basking in his son being okay, taking stock of everything from his missing glasses to how damp his clothes still were to the fact that there wasn’t a single scratch on him that Eddie could see.

Unfortunately, however, there was still work for him to do. Chris’ crutches were gone, probably swept out to sea, but Eddie didn’t feel comfortable putting him on a cot and leaving him there. So he commandeered a wheelchair and stuck Chris in it, parking him nearby while he went through rows of cots and groups of new patients, and then pushing him along when he moved to a new area. Gabrielle, who wasn’t suffering from anything except maybe exhaustion, volunteered to continue keeping Chris company while Eddie worked.

Treating the ills of the people around him kept Eddie’s mind off his own problems, much as it had before he was reunited with Chris. Just as Bobby had reported to him, Chris didn’t know what happened to Buck. Apparently they’d taken refuge on a fire truck, Eddie was willing to guess it was the 136’s, where Buck managed to rescue “a lot of people, Dad,” from the water. They’d played games such as I Spy, and at some point Buck decided to try reciting Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland from memory for some reason, and then the water started rocking the truck. There were more people shouting for help, and Buck was trying to grab them when Chris fell over the side.

There’d been no word of Buck since. Not from Bobby and them, not from Athena. Every time Eddie closed his eyes, his mind conjured up the worst possibilities. He saw again Buck trapped under the ladder truck, Buck coughing up blood, except this time he was alone. Every fiber of Eddie’s being demanded that he run off and search for Buck himself, but he couldn’t leave Chris and he still had no clue where to look.

Chris couldn’t even tell him where the fire truck was.

“Looks like you’re gonna have to get used to being a southpaw for the time being,” Eddie told his current patient as he helped get his arm into a sling.

“Best time to work on your change-up and curveball,” Bosko said from behind him.

Eddie turned where he saw and saw she was still standing and holding herself rather stiffly.

“You a pitcher, too?” he asked, checking to make sure the sling wasn’t going anywhere before getting up and walking over. “Nice to know we have more in common.”

“Class A regional all-star, two years straight.”

“Nice. We always seemed to get overshadowed, damn Bulldogs.” Eddie gestured at her middle. “You all fixed up?”

“All taped up, pain killed, and rarin’ to get back out,” Bosko answered.

“Seriously? You’re kidding me.”

“My captain’s still somewhere out there.”

“There are already people looking for him,” Eddie pointed out.

“There’s gonna be one more,” she insisted. “C’mon, I know you want to be out there, lookin’ for yours. I’ll keep an eye out for him, too, just tell me what to look for.”

He was about to answer, except he spotted Athena just beyond Bosko’s shoulder. At first he started jogging over to see if she had any news, and then he was running because the temporary light fixtures made the blood on her hands and her uniform shine.

“Athena!?”

“It’s not my blood,” she reassured him, holding her hands out so she didn’t get it anywhere else. “It’s his.”

There was a stretcher being escorted by medics. On it was a man in a firefighter’s uniform, and Eddie didn’t have to ask what happened to result in so much blood getting all over Athena. The man’s left arm ended just above the elbow. Bosko let out a cry of “Coop!” before rushing to his side. She bent her head down to talk to him, disappearing with the group into the hospital proper.

“Was he like that when you found him, or…?” Eddie trailed off, the sergeant’s expression answering the question for him.

“I had to use pruning shears,” she said, looking ill.

“It looked very clean,” he said, for lack of anything else to say. Field amputations were never pleasant, he knew that from experience.

“Did Chris get here okay?” Athena asked, clearly eager to talk about anything else.

Eddie nodded and pointed over to where Chris was chatting happily with Gabrielle.

“I’d hug you, but.” They both looked down at the blood covering her. “Any word on…?”

“No,” she said, shaking her head. “But he’s a survivor. We’ll find him.”

Eddie really wished he had her optimism.

Maddie’s phone buzzed with an incoming call from an unsaved number. She picked it up, preparing herself for yet more bad news. That had been all her day had been so far, ever since the wave crashed into Santa Monica. Bad news, bad calls, bad ends. It seemed like everything was determined to be as awful as possible. And then, once reaching the point, to get even worse.

“Hello?”

“Maddie.”

“Buck!” She sat up in her seat and then shot to her feet. “Where are you? Where are you calling from? I don’t know this number.”

“I borrowed someone’s phone. Danny didn’t answer.” Of course Buck would call their brother first. And of course Daniel didn’t answer. Sue had managed to get him to drink something in the break room, but he was still quiet, dull, and muted. Utterly despondent. He probably hadn’t bothered looking at his phone, if he’d noticed it ringing at all. “I need your help.”

“Are you hurt?” Maddie asked, rushing across the floor and past all the other stations, ignoring the wide-eyed stare from Josh as she about bulldozed him over. “Where are you?”

“Maddie, no, listen to me,” her baby brother begged. “Eddie dropped Christopher off with me, to- to get me out of the apartment. And I- Maddie, I took him to the pier.”

“I know.”

“What?” Buck’s voice broke on the word.

“Dan told me,” she told him, coming to stand beside the brother in question. She put her phone on speaker and set it down on the table in front of him. Daniel frowned at her, which was more of a reaction than she would’ve gotten a couple hours ago. “You didn’t answer me, where are you and are you hurt?”

“That doesn’t-”

“Buck!?” It was like a switch was flipped in Daniel. He lunged and grabbed her phone, clutching at it like a lifeline. “You’re- Are you okay?”

“Listen to me, that doesn’t matter.”

“The fuck it doesn’t! Where are you? I’ll-”

“I lost Chris!” Buck snapped. “Christopher is gone and it’s- I can’t find him. I checked the camps at the Promenade, at the high school-”

“Did you check the VA hospital?” Maddie broke in, before either of her brothers could spiral any further. “On Sawtelle? They’ve turned it into a command center.”

“I’m here now,” Buck said. “He- Oh, God. God, God, no.”

Daniel was already out of his seat and ready to rush to Sawtelle, but he drew to a stop at the change in Buck’s voice, at the way he sounded so suddenly frightened.

“What?” Daniel asked. “Buck, what is it?”

“Eddie’s here.”

Maddie didn’t know what Eddie had been told. She hadn’t heard from Chimney since she told him about Buck and Chris, and she hadn’t been able to keep a ear on the calls or any of the radio chatter involving the 118. There’d simply been too many things demanding her attention.

“Good, that’s good,” she said. “Go to him and-”

“And tell him I lost his son? How do I do that?” Buck asked. Maddie shot a look at Daniel and got a quiet headshake in return, because neither of them knew the answer to that, not even after the day they’d both had. “No, no, I need to keep looking. I need to find him.”

“You need to stay where you are,” Daniel told him, glaring at the phone like Buck could see it. “You didn’t tell us if you’re hurt. You’re at the hospital, stay there.”
“No, I can’t stay here. I need to find Chris.”

“And you- Damn it.” 

Daniel cursed as the screen indicated that Buck had hung up. He tossed the phone back to Maddie, leaving her to fumble to catch it and then to stumble after him as he rushed for the elevators, giving Sue a rushed explanation as she went by.

His phone ringing in his pocket had Eddie dropping everything to pull it out. The fact that it was Danny calling him meant he nearly broke his thumb by slamming it onto the screen in his rush to answer.

“Dan-”

“Do you see him!?”

Eddie jerked the phone from his ear a little at the volume.

“See who?” 

“Buck,” was Danny’s impatient answer. “Be fucking useful and- No! Maddie, stop, give-”

“Buck just called me.”

“What!?” It felt like a jolt went through him. “He’s-?”

“He’s wherever you are, right now,” Maddie said. “He saw you and I think he hid.”

“What?” Eddie looked around, relief and confusion warring as he spun in place, searching for the other man. There was a mail carrier that had arrived, Bobby hopping out the back and shouting for help. “Why would he…?”

It was a question that no longer mattered, because there were more important things to focus on. Like the fact that Buck was standing mere feet away. He looked… Awful, honestly. Bedraggled. There were a series of nasty cuts across his face, like something had clawed at him, and a dirty makeshift bandage was wrapped around his arm, and Christopher’s glasses were hanging from his neck. And he seemed frightened at the sight of him.

“Eds…”

Eddie didn’t even give the courtesy of saying goodbye or hanging up, and was thankful for the sturdy case as his phone slipped from his hand to hit the ground. He rushed to cross the distance between them, throwing his arms around Buck to pull him in close and not caring even a little about the grime. He felt Buck stiffen in his hold and struggle to get loose, but he only held him tighter.

“Eddie.” Buck’s voice was muffled where his face was pressed into Eddie’s shoulder, but the hoarseness and the way it cracked was still audible. “Eddie, I- I’m sorry, I-”

“Where are you hurt?” Eddie asked, stepping back and holding him at arm’s length. “Your face, God, that has to sting. What happened to your arm?”

“Eddie, Eddie, listen to me,” his boyfriend begged, sounding anguished. “Chris and I, we- We went to the pier, and- I tried, Eddie. I swear, I tried-”

“Buck,” Eddie interrupted. “I know.”

“You…” A shattered expression crossed Buck’s face. “You know?”

“He’s right over there, Buck,” Eddie told him, moving out of the way so Buck could see where Chris was sitting. “He’s fine. He’s worried about you, we all are.”

“He’s…”

Buck blinked once, and then again, as he stared at Chris like there was nothing else in the world. He blew out a shaky breath and drew in another, then did it again. 

And then Eddie had to rush to catch him when he collapsed, a puppet with its strings cut.

Daniel rushed through the hospital hallways, heart pounding a drumbeat in his chest. Maddie was somewhere behind him, he’d outpaced her in the parking lot filled with tents and cots. He danced around medics and patients alike, constantly scanning for anyone he recognized. 

“Buck!?” he called out, not breaking pace. “Buck! Evan!?”

“Danny!”

He skidded to a halt, caught himself on a door frame, looked down the hall the shout came from. He spotted Eddie waving at him, surrounded by others that he recognized from Buck’s station - Nash, Chimney, Hen. Not Austin, though. Austin was presumably still somewhere in the mess outside.

(There were a lot of unanswered texts and missed calls from Austin on Daniel’s phone. But that was something he’d make up for later.)

Daniel pretty much shoved Nash out of the way without a single ounce of guilt when he hurried over, in too much of a rush to see his baby brother to even pretend to care about the captain. And there in front of him, laid out on a cot, was Buck. Unconscious, hooked up to a line of fluids and getting a blood transfusion, with his face scratched up and the inside of his forearm bandaged up. Christopher was curled up on the cot beside him, taking a nap of his own.

The sight of him there, alive if not unhurt, was enough to have Daniel’s chest unclenching. He eased himself to sit on the floor beside his head and started to run his fingers through Buck’s tangled curls.

“He’s fine. Mostly,” Hen said. “Exhaustion and some minor blood loss. They’re giving him saline and antibiotics.”

Daniel nodded, mind caught on the “fine” part, unable to look away from his sleeping brother. Because he’d spent the day in a world where Buck was gone and he was still shaking that death shroud off. Because he’d spent the day being utterly useless while his baby brother stumbled through the ruins of Santa Monica. Alone.

“There’s a problem, though,” Eddie said, crouching down next to him. The others were talking amongst themselves and to Maddie, who’d finally caught up. “Chris didn’t want to leave Buck, and he’s gonna wake up when we move him. I don’t feel like dealing with a tantrum tonight.”

“I’m not sleeping on your couch,” Daniel told him, because he could easily guess where this conversation was headed. And he didn’t particularly feel like letting Buck out of his sight at the moment, either.

“Sleepover at yours it is.” Eddie slapped him on the back; Daniel was still too grateful for everything to pretend to be aggravated by the man’s mere presence. “Just like the old days.”

“Are you sure you’ll be okay?” Danny asked, even as he went through his work bag on the kitchen table and double checked that everything was in it. “I’ll call in if you need me here.”

“I’m fine, Danny,” Buck insisted. He was curled up on the couch, the empty bowl and spoon from breakfast sitting on the end table beside him. “You’ve used up enough of your PTO this year already.”

“Mehta was just grateful I finally used some of it,” his brother said, looking unconvinced. “You know what? I think I will call in, I’ve covered for guys on shorter notice.”

“Danny.” Buck threw back his head with a whine. “Go, I’ll be fine.”

Danny had been hovering since Buck woke up at the field hospital to everyone, including his brother, around him. He’d hovered throughout the impromptu sleepover when they got home, and he hovered throughout the day afterwards as Buck went through the pain of getting another phone. If Buck was in the other room for too long, even if it was the bathroom, Danny came looking for him to make sure he hadn’t coughed up another clot or fallen over and hit his head or some other disaster that Danny managed to come up with.

“I’m probably just going to spend the day arguing with our insurance,” Buck told him. The jeep was… Somewhere, he didn’t really know. With how close he parked to where the pier used to be, it could’ve been in one of the piles of twisted metal that was still being cleared or it could be on the seafloor. “I’ll be fine. You can go.”

His brother probably would have argued more, but there were a series of tiny knocks at the door. They both frowned at it and then at each other, before Danny went over to answer it.

“Hi, Danny!”

At the sound of Chris’ voice, at the sight of him and Eddie standing in the doorway, Buck jolted to his feet and made several unsure steps in their direction.

“Are those new crutches?” Danny asked, stepping aside for them to come in. 

“Spares,” Eddie answered, waltzing in like nothing was wrong.

Chris immediately made for Buck, leaning into his stomach as Buck hugged him, before going to the couch to take the spot that was just vacated. Buck shot Danny a look, who shrugged in response and went over to grab his bag.

“Well I’ve got to get to work,” he said, giving the room a general wave on his way out. “Have fun, ya crazy kids.”

“Wait, but-”

“Bye, Danny!” Chris called, cheerfully waving from his seat before the door closed.

“Okay!” Eddie was at the kitchen table, going through what Buck recognized as Chris’ backpack. “There’s a morning snack and midday snack, two coloring books, and a bunch of Legos.” He turned his head to check on Chris, and then lowered his voice. “You know how he just likes sticking them together, he’s never built anything that kinda looks like anything.”

“Eddie-”

“There’s twenty bucks for pizza, because I kinda figured you wouldn’t feel up to making anything,” his boyfriend continued, like he hadn’t said anything. “Eat a couple extra slices, you look like you’re wasting away to nothing. Don’t make me send Abuela over here to make sure you eat. She’ll do it, you’re one of her favorites.”

“Eddie,” Buck tried again. “I don’t- What is this?”

The other man scrunched up his face at the question.

“Uh, I thought that was obvious. You’re watching Chris today while I’m at work,” Eddie said. “Or, he’s watching you. You’re watching each other?”

“I lost him.”

Eddie let out a sigh and walked towards him, reaching up to put a hand where Buck’s neck and shoulder met, thumb rubbing circles into his pulse.

“See, the way he tells it,” he said. “You saved him, and then you saved a bunch of people, and then he lost you.”

“You trusted me to keep him safe,” Buck said.

“Which you did,” Eddie said, so simply that Buck reeled from it. “And then you nearly killed yourself trying to find him again.”

“Eddie-”

“Buck.” He cupped his chin and leaned forward to kiss him. It was chaste, compared to some of the kisses they’d shared before, but it still left Buck feeling light headed when Eddie pulled back. “I love you, I trust you.”

Eddie stepped away, leaving Buck struggling for a proper response to that, not that the other man seemed to care. He went to Chris for a quick goodbye hug, and then he was heading out the door again, though he paused on the way to give Buck another kiss.

“Maybe try going to the zoo this time,” he tossed over his shoulder as he stepped out. “Something inland.”

Buck stared at the closed door for a moment, still reeling, before turning to look at Chris beaming at him from his seat on the couch.

“Hey, uh.” He wet his lips. “How do you feel about a pillow fort?”

Chapter End Notes

Buck's life probably would've been easier if Eddie had remembered to put Christopher's name on the list. Woops.

Chapter 8

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Daniel wasn’t entirely certain what woke him at first. He sat up and rubbed his eyes, trying to figure out why he was awake at - according to his alarm clock - the other side of three in the morning. There was a noise coming from Buck’s room, which wasn’t unusual. Buck was not a silent or still sleeper by any means and Daniel had long ago gotten used to the snoring, to the point that he had difficulty falling asleep in a silent apartment after Buck first left for his Neverending Gap Year Road Trip. But what he was hearing now wasn’t snoring, it was more like… It was the sound of someone crying in their sleep.

He threw his covers off and got to his feet, padding out of his room to the one beside it. Buck’s bedroom was, as ever, a mess. Not as bad as it had been when he was a teenager, but it was obvious that there wasn’t much care or concern where things ended up. At least the clothes were making it near the laundry hamper. 

Buck himself was in his bed, very clearly not sleeping peacefully. His face was twisted unhappily, and the sheets were tangled up around him to the point that Daniel wondered how he could move at all. As he watched, Buck tossed his head back and forth, letting out a pained cry as he did.

A nightmare. Another nightmare. There’d been so many in recent nights, cropping up more the further from the tsunami they got. When they were still living in Hershey, if Buck had a nightmare, he’d wake up Daniel by climbing into his bed. Now, Daniel sat on the edge of Buck’s and gently shook his shoulder.

“C’mon, squirt, wake up.”

Buck let out a gasp and shot up with a cry of, “Chris!” And then he started looking around frantically, as if trying to spot the kid somewhere in the room with them.

“Hey, hey.” Daniel grabbed at his chin, made his brother look at him. “Chris is fine.”

“No, no, I- I lost him, Danny,” Buck said, still as tangled in the nightmare as was his bedding. “I can’t find him, I can’t-”

“Yes you did,” Daniel insisted, trying to get him to recall. “He was with Eddie. He was safe and sound with Eddie. And then you two built a pillow fort in the living room, remember? I came home to find the two of you passed out on the floor and all my pillows missing from my bed.”

“But I… I…” Buck looked around again, blinking blearily. “He was…”

“It was just a nightmare,” Daniel told him, rubbing at his back. “Chris is fine.”

“Chris is fine,” Buck repeated, quiet and not quite believing it.

“Do you want to call Eddie?” Daniel asked him. He frowned at the state of Buck’s face and wiped at some of the tears that had fallen down his cheeks. “Have him check on Chris?”

“No, no, he’s-” Buck cut himself off and turned to look at his alarm clock. “Shit, Danny. It’s late. You should be asleep.”

“I was. And then I heard my baby brother having a nightmare.”

“I’m-”

“Don’t,” Daniel said, cutting him off. “Do you want to talk about it?”

“What’s there to talk about?” Buck asked in return, shrugging a little. “I lost Chris, couldn’t find him. You woke me up. Y’know, the usual. You should go back to bed, Danny, I’m fine.”

Daniel’s brother was never really the best at lying directly, he was better at talking so much that he never actually got to what he was lying about. And claiming that he was fine was most definitely a lie, perhaps his worst yet.

“You gonna try going back to sleep yet?” Daniel asked instead of calling him out.

“Maybe,” Buck said, running his hand through his hair. “I think I might stay up a bit.”

“Want me to stay up with you?”

“You have a shift in the morning,” Buck reminded him.

“So do you.”

“I don’t think it matters if I fall asleep at my desk, Danny.” A bit of bitterness slipped through the words.

After the tsunami, Buck had opted not to just quit entirely and instead accepted the light duty that was being offered. He wasn’t happy about it, neither of them were, but it was only temporary until the brass got their heads out of their asses. It was supposed to be, anyway.

A desk job was not the safer alternative for someone who’d had a DVT, no matter what idiot in charge decided it was. And if there was one thing that the tsunami should’ve shown everyone, it was that the blood thinners were not the liability people thought they were. Not if Buck was capable of walking around all day with multiple open wounds, only to end up collapsing at the end because of exhaustion instead of blood loss.

But no one was asking Daniel’s opinion about these things, and he contented himself with just being glad that Buck wasn’t allowing himself to waste away in the dark. Even if the longer he was stuck being a fire marshall instead of a firefighter, the more it seemed he might end up right back there.

“Are you sure?” Daniel asked him.

“I’m sure.” Buck pushed at him a little. “I don’t need the beauty sleep as much as you do.”

“Brat.” Daniel tugged his head down so he could press a kiss to his hair. “If you need me, wake me.”

“Yeah, okay.”

Daniel, with great reluctance, got up and left the room for his own. When he got there, he didn’t lay back down right away. Instead, he sat down on the edge of his bed and picked up his phone off the nightstand, where it was plugged in to charge. It took little debate, and no guilt, to shoot Eddie a text telling him about Buck’s nightmare.

Maybe the guy would be useful and, Daniel didn’t know, send Buck pictures of Chris or something.

“My God,” Chimney said once they’d all hopped out of the trucks. “That’s the biggest damn fire I’ve never seen.”

They’d been called to an office building in downtown LA. The 144 were already there, had been for some time by all appearances, not that there was any actual emergency that required rushing.

“I hate fire drills,” Hen complained while they got the hose attached to the nearest hydrant.

“City mandates we do ‘em every three years,” Eddie told her. He knew that because Buck had told him the evening before. At the time, he’d just thought it was one of the random bits of trivia that his boyfriend enjoyed learning and then sharing. Now, in hindsight, it was a not-so-subtle hint about today. The little smirk as he said it should’ve clued Eddie in, but he was just happy to see him smiling. “Can’t fight city hall.”

“First alarm was triggered at Twelve Twenty, call came in thirty seconds later,” Bobby reported, standing a bit away from them. He was looking at his watch and was holding the tablet they kept in the truck. “Boots down, hoses out by Twelve Forty-three.”

“That’s, what?” Eddie got up from where he was crouched to join him.

“Four minutes over our allotted response time.”

“Ouch,” Chimney said, hose draped over his shoulder. “That’s gonna cost us some points.”

“Somebody’s gonna have to explain that to the new fire marshal,” Hen said.

She and Chimney, as a single unit, focused their gazes on Eddie. Eddie, in turn, directed his at Bobby. The captain sighed and stomped off in the direction of the entrance doors. Eddie, eager to see his boyfriend, followed after him.

Buck was just inside the lobby, clipboard in hand, wearing the fire marshal uniform of a white shirt with black pants and tie. As Eddie and Bobby walked up to him, he checked his watch while he tapped his foot and then gave an exaggerated shake of his head. Bobby let out another sigh.

“What took you so long?” Buck asked.

“There was a stalled bus blocking both directions on Grand,” Bobby answered, walking past without slowing down. Buck fell into step with him while Eddie followed behind, because… Okay, Buck looked very good in those pants and from the back was the best view. “Vehicles couldn’t clear a path, which delayed our arrival.”

“And yet the One Forty-four managed a response time of-” Buck paused for effect and flipped a page on the clipboard. “Under six minutes, a full seventeen minutes ahead of the One Eighteen.”

Bobby finally stopped, fixing Buck with an exasperated glare.

“The One Forty-four is five blocks away.”

“I hear you, Bobby, I do, and- and I wanna help,” Buck said, all earnesty. “But I gotta be impartial here, y’know? I’m stuck with this kind of light duty until I can be back with you guys, and I gotta take it seriously. Gotta be strictly by the book, just how you would want me to be.”

Sometimes, Eddie forgot how petty Buck could be about things. He really shouldn’t have, all things considered, though all the sunshine and cheer could blind a person to it. But Buck grew up the youngest of three with parents that, by everything Eddie had been told and had seen, were the epitome of passive-aggressiveness. Eddie figured a petty streak was to be expected.

“For what it’s worth, I’m proud of you,” Bobby said, not rising to the bait. “I’m glad you didn’t throw away your career just ‘cause you had a little setback.”

Buck’s smile was suddenly looking a little fixed.

“Be careful not to logjam at the stairwells,” he advised.

“You’re enjoying this too much,” Bobby said, shaking his head, before walking off.

“A little setback,” Buck mocked under his breath, dropping the smile. “And enjoying this too much? This is fucking miserable.”

“It’s not too bad,” Eddie drawled.

Buck turned his head to glower at him.

“Don’t you have a job to be doing?”

“And I’m doing it,” Eddie said. “I’m sweet-talking the guy scoring us.”

Buck let out a laugh and crossed his arms, clutching the clipboard to his chest. He rocked back on his heels, a real smile back on his face.

“Alright, sweet-talk away.”

“Well, uh.” Shit, hm. “You… Are looking very good in those pants.”

Buck rolled his eyes and dropped his arms.

“I look better in suspenders.”

“I, for one, think you look good in anything. Even better in nothing.”

“Keep going,” Buck said, clicking his pen and writing something down on the clipboard. “You’ve shaved two whole minutes off your response time.”

“Just two minutes?”

“Pretty sure I’ve said that to you before, actually.”

“This is a fake fire, why can’t we use a fake hose?” Chimney complained, interrupting Eddie before he could come up with a suitable comeback. He had a hose over his shoulder, with Hen and some of the others right behind him. “Hey, Buck, how’re we doing?”

“You do not want to know,” Buck answered, and then told Eddie, “You should probably go do something useful.”

Eddie let out a melodramatic sigh.

“Yeah, probably.”

He started to walk off to go be useful, but Buck’s hand on his arm stopped him. His boyfriend started leaning in, clearly intending to go for a kiss goodbye, and Eddie… Eddie jerked away, doing his best to ignore the way he was certain that everyone around them was staring. He couldn’t ignore the way Buck’s face fell.

“You probably shouldn’t look like you’re playing favorites,” Eddie said, tamping down on what was most definitely not panic.

“Nah, playing favorites would be, let’s say…” Buck tapped his pen against the clipboard, face screwed up in thought. “Someone telling their brother the exact time, date, and place of their station’s scheduled fire drill.”

Austin Jones, walking by at just that moment, came to a stop to glare at him.

“You warned Dan, but you couldn’t tell us?”

“You’re not my brother,” Buck answered with a shrug. “You’re just his… Whatever you are.”

“I’m not sure where we rank, but it’s definitely somewhere below Danny and Chris,” Eddie told Austin. “And Buck’s always been a brat.”

“Wow. What happened to sweet-talking me into a better score?”

Eddie opened his mouth, about to come up with something that would shave another minute off their dismal response time, when there was a commotion at the stairwell. People were rushing to get out, and he spotted someone spilling out of it onto the floor. As he rushed over to check on them, he heard Buck let out a sigh behind him and say,

“I warned him about the logjam…”

How much would he have to sweet-talk, Eddie wondered, to make up for this?

“So how is being a fire marshall?” Eddie asked, setting Buck’s drink down on the coffee table. The glass clinked against the wood. “Really?”

“I told you, it’s miserable,” Buck answered. He was sitting on the floor, his left leg stretched out in front of him and with an exercise strap hooked around his foot. He paused in the stretch to frown at the bottle. “I’m not sure I’m supposed to be drinking on the blood thinners.”

“Damn, there goes my idea to get you drunk so I could take advantage.”

“I mean, I don’t need to be drunk for you to take advantage.” Buck winked at him, before returning to the stretch he was doing. “Anyway, I hate being a fire marshal.”

“You seemed to be enjoying yourself at the fire drill,” Eddie pointed out, taking a seat on the couch. He nudged at Buck’s thigh with his foot. “Y’know, strutting around in those tight pants and making snide remarks at everyone about how we were doing.”

“Constructive criticism is important, Eds,” Buck argued. “But that was like, one day. The rest of the time, I’m sitting at a desk doing busy work. Eight hours, Eddie. I’m sitting at a desk for eight hours. Do you remember how much trouble I got into at school because I couldn’t do that?”

“I remember.”

“It was a lot! And like, I’m on the blood thinners because I coughed up a clot-”

“I remember that, too,” Eddie said, tone grave.

“So they have me doing a job that makes that more likely!?” Buck threw up his hands, accidentally tossing the exercise strap across the room. Not that he noticed, with how much he was working himself up. “Bobby said they weren’t letting me back because they were concerned about liability issues, but they’re fine with me maybe coughing up blood again!?”

“What does Danny say about it?” Eddie asked, leaning over to grab the strap where it had landed on the other end of the couch.

“Nothing nice or kind,” Buck said. Specifically, the last time they’d talked about it, Danny had called whoever made that decision a “giant fucking dumbass” and made suggestions as to just where upon their persons he could insert his bat. Lengthwise. “I just- I want to go back to being a firefighter already. I want to be useful again.”

Eddie frowned down at him, holding out the exercise strap for him to take back.

“You…” He trailed off and shook his head. “You’re plenty useful, Buck.”

“Sure,” Buck scoffed, taking the strap and dropping it on the table beside the bottle. He picked up the drink. “I probably shouldn’t be drinking this right now anyway, blood thinners or not, with how late it’s getting.”

“Danny’s working tonight, isn’t he? Just spend the night here.”

“Still hoping to take advantage of me, I see,” Buck teased. He wasn’t really sure if he should spend the night, however appealing it sounded. Not when he was waking Danny up most nights with his nightmares. “I dunno. You have to get up for work and to get him to school. I’m not sure I should, I might keep you awake.”

“I’d get woken up anyway.” Eddie let out a sigh. “Chris has been having nightmares.”

“Oh.” Just hearing that broke Buck’s heart. “How bad?”

“Bad,” he said. “Probably about as bad as yours.”

Buck scowled.

“Danny’s a tattler.”

“C’mon, stay the night,” Eddie said. “Maybe it’ll help you. Maybe it’ll help him, because… I have no damn clue what I’m doing there.”

Buck let out a sigh of his own, having lost this particular battle a while ago.

“Yeah, okay,” he said. “I didn’t bring anything to sleep in, though.”

“Oh no.” Eddie’s lips curled into a smirk. “Whatever shall we do?”

The nightmares were always the same. Buck, searching through the flooded ruins of Los Angeles, desperately trying to find Chris. Sometimes, the dream would start with the pair of them on the pier, feet glued in place as the wave came crashing down. Other times, it would start with Chris already lost and Buck in the middle of his quest. But always, always, he would dream of trying to find the boy he lost.

Usually, he either woke himself up or was woken up by Danny. Tonight, it was Eddie.

“There’s your pretty blues,” Eddie whispered, frowning unhappily as he wiped at Buck’s face with the corner of the bed sheet. It was a fresh sheet, having just been put on that night. “Chris is fine. Do you want to go see him?”

Buck opened his mouth, to apologize for waking up Eddie, to say that he wanted nothing more than to see Chris. Except, the boy himself chose that time to scream out. Buck’s own nightmare forgotten, the pair of them scrambled out and down the hall. Eddie flicked on Chris’ light as he entered, rushing to the bed to take Chris into his arms.

“She was drowning,” the boy whimpered. “She was drowning.”

“Oh, it’s okay,” Eddie told him, rubbing at his back. “It’s okay, buddy. I’m here, it was just a dream. It’s just a dream, you’re safe.”

He tucked Chris’ head into his chest and pressed a kiss into his hair. Buck, feeling guilty, feeling unsure, crept across the room to join them on the bed. He lifted an arm and wrapped it around the both of them, not knowing what else to do.

Neither he nor Eddie got much sleep that night, but at least Buck was growing used to it.

Chapter End Notes

I enjoy putting in moments of parallelism between Daniel(&Buck) and Eddie(&Chris). There are other examples of it in this series, including one in Spare Parts that is very blatant.

Chapter 9

Chapter Notes

content warning: Mention of and reference to Dr. Wells and all that entails.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Buck was hit by an odd conflict of emotions as he strolled into the station. It felt sorta like coming home, something similar to what he felt the first time he came back from his Neverending Gap Year Road Trip for the holidays and stepped into that apartment in El Paso. A lesser version of that. But it felt wrong, somehow. Different. Going into the station after so long away had him feeling off-balanced and with the niggling impression that he’d forgotten something.

The lack of a bag on his shoulder, holding everything he needed for a shift, was probably the cause for it.

“Careful, guys,” Hen called out. She’d spotted him from across the bay, and the smirk was audible in her words. “Hall monitor’s here.”

He grinned at her, and at Eddie and Chimney where they were coming out of the locker room.

“Did you guys paint?” Buck asked, making an exaggerated look around. “Why does the place look smaller?”

“I think your head just got bigger,” Chimney quipped.

“Well, Interim Captain Han managed to fit his in here just fine, so I think I’ll be okay.”

Buck beamed at the glower that the words earned him.

“Did you start missing me already?” Eddie asked, waltzing over to him. “You just saw me this morning.”

“Who says I’m here to see you?” Buck teased, even as he stepped closer.

“Oh, my God!” Hen cried. She was close enough now to see Buck’s neck, and she reached up to tug down his shirt collar so she could see even more. “Eddie! Were you roleplaying as Count Dracula or something!?”

Eddie’s face turned bright red and he was suddenly incapable of looking anyone in the eye, feet shuffling in place as he glanced around awkwardly.

“Shoot, the blood thinners.” Buck hadn’t thought about this as a consequence. Though it did explain why Carla had started laughing at the sight of him that morning, when she arrived to take Chris to school. “How bad does it look?”

By the twin expressions of silent laughter that Hen and Chimney were sporting, he figured the answer was clear. Danny was going to have a field day with this.

“So Fire Marshal Buck decides to drop off his report in person.” That was Bobby. Buck turned to see him stepping off the steps to the loft. “Is this a sign of maturity? Or revenge for something I’ve done?”

“I just haven’t seen most of you in a while. And I figured I’d hand you the drill results in person,” Buck told him, and then held out the papers he’d been carrying. Bobby took them and started flipping through. “You passed, by the way. I bumped up the numbers, got fancy with the math. You can thank Eddie’s sweet-talking for that.”

Behind them, he heard an amused Hen say under her breath to Chimney, “Is that what we’re calling it now?”

“You don’t know math,” Eddie reminded him, no doubt hearing Hen as well and electing to ignore her as he walked around to peer over Bobby’s shoulder at the results.

“Neither do you, but that’ll be my excuse if anyone calls me out on it,” Buck said. But that reminded him! “Speaking of, did any of you guys get a call from that lawyer?”

“The ambulance chaser?” Hen sounded dubious.

“He wants to talk to me about the building violations.” The guy, Chase Mackey or whatever, had called him the night before while he was with Eddie. Buck hadn’t answered, but Mackey had left him a message. “Think somebody might be suing them.”

“Yeah, he was passing out cards like candy,” Chimney said.

“Hey, Diaz!” That was a new voice. A buff woman he didn’t recognize, over in the station gym. “I need a spot over here.”

“In a moment!” Eddie responded.

“Who’s that?” Buck asked, curious. Though that curiosity soured when he spotted Hen and Chimney sharing another look, the kind people gave each other when they foresaw an awkward moment forthcoming, and quickly scurried away.

Bobby did not seem very impressed by them, and even Eddie was looking uncomfortable, which did nothing to make Buck feel better.

“Her name’s Lena Bosko,” Bobby said. “Her station was smack in the middle of the impact zone and her crew was temporarily reassigned, so I brought her over here.”

“She’s only stuck with us until the One Thirty-Six reopens,” Eddie hurried to add.

“Well.” Buck did his best to wrangle his feelings, trying to keep the hurt and anger out his words. “It’s, uh. It’s a good thing you have someone filling in for me until everyone stops being stupid and I can come back.”

“I promise you.” Bobby’s tone was probably meant to be reassuring, but it just came across as vaguely condescending. It did nothing to help Buck’s mood. “Your place will still be here for you when you’re ready.”

“Great!” Buck chirped. “Go ahead and kick her ass to another station, then, ‘cause I’m ready right now.”

The captain’s face went grim. Standing to the side, Eddie’s eyes darted between them.

“Listen to me-” Bobby started to say, but the alarm chose that moment to go off. He started jogging for the trucks, calling over his shoulder, “We will talk soon!”

“I’ll see you when I get off,” Eddie promised, darting forward to kiss him on the lips before hurrying away as well.

Soon, Buck was left glaring at the empty spaces where the trucks used to be and feeling the urge to break something.

One morning, a couple mornings before the fire drill, Eddie was stopped on his way to the locker room by Austin calling out to him and asking,

“Who’s Bosko?”

“Uh.” Eddie stopped in his tracks, adjusted his grip on his bag. Chris had another nightmare the night before, waking and keeping them both up, so he wasn’t running on all cylinders just yet. “She was one of the firefighters that was caught in the tsunami. Why?”

Austin, standing in his civvies by the cages for their turn-out gear, didn’t give an answer beyond pointing at something with his finger. Curious, Eddie went over for a look and didn’t like what he saw. There was tape covering up most of Buck’s name tag, leaving only the B showing to fill in for the B in Bosko.

The coat hanging from the hook wasn’t Buck’s.

“I guess her rib’s doing better,” Eddie remarked a bit dumbly, and then, “Wait, who did this?”

“Cap, I’m guessing,” Austin answered with a frown. “I can’t tell Dan about this, he’s already threatened Cap once.”

Eddie did a double take.

“Wait, what?”

“You didn’t hear that.”

Eddie debated pressing for more info, but he knew that Austin was fully capable of clamming up and not saying a damn thing. According to Buck, and to Danny’s amusement, it had greatly annoyed the detectives investigating the bank robbery. He chose to focus on the fact that his boyfriend’s name had been taped over. Something easily fixed, and done without much thought: he simply reached up and tore the tape off, not that it really fixed much of anything.

“There’s an empty cage there,” Austin said, jerking his thumb in its direction. “Dunno why Cap didn’t put her name on it, instead.”

Eddie didn’t know, either, and didn’t spend any time wondering about it. Instead, he hefted his bag onto his shoulder better, pulled the turn-out coat off its hook, and carried it over. Austin shrugged and started to help with the rest of the gear.

When Bosko got in, she joked about the missing B in her name..

Buck didn’t have much experience with lawyers. The one and only time he interacted with one was when Attica Rose defended him from the detectives trying to pin the blame on anyone they could for the bank robbery without actually bothering to do any detective work. He had liked Attica, and he could see why Danny liked her, too. Not just because she was a very pretty lady, but also because she was kinda badass.

Chase Mackey just came across as slimy and smarmy, the epitome of every lawyer stereotype.

“So we can agree,” Mackey said, settling into his seat. The guy’s office was nice looking, with lots of wood finishing and tasteful decorations. There were little knick-knacks on the desk and on the bookshelves, along with plants, that probably made it impractical to take out the books behind them to read. “There was some negligence during this fire drill.”

“Uh.” Buck took a moment to feign thought, and then shook his head. “Nah, I wouldn’t agree with that, no.”

“Are you sure?” Mackey leaned forward, flipped upon a folder he had sitting in front of him. Buck frowned at its contents, because when had he the time to get that? “Your after-action report says the LAFD exceeded the evacuation time requirement by twelve minutes.”

“It’s a thirty-five story building, I’d say that’s a decent time.” They probably would have made better time if Bobby had listened to him about the stairwells, but Buck wasn’t going to just come out and say that to this lawyer. “I thought your client was suing the building? Why are you asking about the department?”

“Clients,” the lawyer corrected. “Forty-two injured people that the LAFD failed to protect. This is a class action against the city on all of their behalfs.”

“You’re suing the city?”

“Mhmm.”

“And you think I’m gonna help you with that?”

“Mhmm.” Mackey nodded, giving him a wide smile. “I find that disgruntled employees often make the best witnesses.”

“I wouldn’t say I was disgruntled.” Although, maybe he would. He was feeling quite disgruntled after his visit to the station.

That seemed to amuse Mackey.

“I looked into you,” he said. “I know about the accident, how you’ve been fighting with the LAFD to return to full duty because of the blood thinners.”

Well, that felt a bit violating.

“It’s someone being a dumbass, somewhere,” Buck said, the same thing as Danny kept telling him. And if Danny said it, then Buck was going to believe it. “It’ll get cleared up, and then I’ll be one of those guys you’re trying to sue.”

“Right.” Mackey’s condescension had a different flavor to it than Bobby’s. Probably the derision that it was mixed with. “There was something about liability concerns, so it sounds like they’re just protecting their own asses. So who’s protecting you?”

Danny was the most immediate and most obvious answer. But that wasn’t the one he gave while he pushed himself out of his chair, because “my big brother” sounded a bit childish even to his own ears. Instead he said,

“I can take care of myself.”

“I’m sure that you can,” Mackey lied. He opened a drawer and pulled out a card, stretching over the desk to give it to Buck. It had the lawyer’s contact info. “Just in case you change your mind about anything.”

“Right,” Buck scoffed, though he pocketed the card all the same. “Good luck with your lawsuit, I hope you don’t ever have an actual fire in the meantime. Would make it awkward for everyone, I think.”

He didn’t give Mackey the chance to answer, exiting the man’s office and closing the door behind him firmly enough to rattle the frame.

The one and only time Buck had ever dealt with a therapist, the one Bobby referred him to after the roller coaster call, it hadn’t exactly worked out the way he’d hoped. Talking to the sister of the guy who’d fallen to his death had done more to help him feel better than that singular appointment had. In fact, he’d left Dr. Wells’ office feeling significantly worse and oddly dirty.

(It had just been sex. Sex was great! Sex felt good! So he was still more than a bit confused, even a year and a half later, as to why having sex with the therapist left him shying away from wanting to have sex with Abby for so long.)

Anyways, this therapy session was not for him. After so many nights where Chris woke the house up screaming, and with Carla’s help finding a doctor that was covered by the LAFD’s insurance and who seemed like a good fit, Eddie had gotten the kid an appointment with a Dr. Lin. Buck hadn’t intended on tagging along, hadn’t thought it was his place considering Chris was his boyfriend’s kid, not his own.

It turned out, though, that Eddie had just assumed he was coming, and so Buck did.

Dr. Lin’s office was significantly different from Wells’. It was obviously geared towards children instead of adults, with various toys and a collection of art supplies, and decorations that were bold and colorful. It struck Buck as somehow being tastefully whimsical. There was a window through which Eddie and Buck could stand outside and watch Chris with the doctor, something that made Buck feel less anxious for some reason.

Buck hadn’t even realized he’d been anxious about this, not until he saw that they could keep an eye on things.

Watching a kid’s therapy session wasn’t really all that exciting. They could see, but they couldn’t hear any of it, so all Buck knew was that Lin asked questions and Christopher talked. And all the while, the kid kept drawing. Eventually, Lin took an interest in the drawings, seeing something that had him frowning. From the way he pointed and tapped at the paper, it was clear he was asking Chris about them.

In the meantime, Buck and Eddie didn’t have much to do but talk. And not much new to talk about, considering that Eddie was one of the few people he spoke to on the regular these days. He’d fallen out of touch with Connor and his other former roommates while he focused on his recovery, and he’d needed to make excuses to visit the station so he could actually lay eyes on Hen and Bobby. 

“Oh, hey,” Eddie said suddenly. “Has, um… Has Chimney spoken to you? Or Danny?”

“Uh, no,” Buck said, tilting his head in curiosity. Chimney he saw a bit more often than the others, but it was whenever he and Danny went over to torment Maddie and they happened to be together. “Why?”

“Before you showed up with the fire drill results,” Eddie explained. “He was venting to me about something going on with Maddie. Apparently she broke a plate, freaked out about it, and fled his apartment.”

“Doug is the gift that keeps on giving.” Buck could feel himself sneering at the mention of his late brother-in-law. “I kinda wish Maddie hadn’t killed him just so we could all take turns stabbing him.”

Eddie let out a snort of laughter.

“I told him to talk to you or Danny about it,” he said. “Y’know, since she’s your sister and all.”

“Well, he hasn’t.” Buck shrugged and tried not to feel hurt by that fact. 

Everyone had their own lives, and their own families, and being stuck with someone for upwards of twenty-four hours at a time meant you didn’t often have to go out of your way to stay in touch with them. Which in turn meant, or so Buck figured, that you didn’t realize you needed to reach out to people when you weren’t seeing them on a scheduled basis anymore. Or at least that was the explanation Buck was going with whenever he was feeling particularly kind and generous.

(Athena was the only one - besides Eddie - who’d made the effort on her own and entirely independent from someone else, sending him an invite to dinner at the Grant-Nash residence. Buck was looking forward to getting some of Bobby’s cooking again, and to try and recruit his captain into helping him get around whatever dumbass was blocking his way back to work.)

On the other side of the window, Dr. Lin got up from the tiny table while Christopher stayed and focused on yet another drawing. Lin came out to join them in the side room, a manilla folder in hand. Eddie straightened up from where he’d been using the wall to prop himself up.

“Well?” He sounded impatient, just on the border of being rude.

“Christopher and I got to know one another, we talked about school and the such. Why you brought him here.”

“Has he talked about the tsunami at all?” Eddie asked, folding his arms in front of him. “I’m still not sure what he saw that day.”

Neither was Buck. He was more than a bit terrified to find out.

“We’re getting there,” Lin said. “Christopher talked about being at the pier with Buck, how they played games, that kind of thing.”

“That part isn’t the reason he’s waking up every night screaming,” Eddie pointed out.

“His subconscious is still processing the trauma. He’s working his way through it,” Lin said. “I know it’s hard. I know you love your son and you want to fix this, but it’ll take some time.”

“I just wish he’d talk to us about it.”

“Maybe he’s trying to communicate in other ways,” Lin suggested, opening up the folder. Eddie froze at the sight of its contents, and then carefully took them from the doctor.

The drawings Chris had made were drenched in ocean blue. One had a half-submerged Ferris wheel with people in the water and HELP written above them in big, red letters. One was a flooded city street with the fire truck they took refuge on. The third and last was of a woman, crying for help and about to be hit by a wave.

Buck wanted to vomit at the sight.

He took them from Eddie so he could flip through them for himself, losing track of what his boyfriend and the therapist were talking about. Buck just couldn’t tear his gaze away from the drawings, not until he felt Eddie’s hand on his arm and he looked up to find that Lin had gone back into the other room again.

“I’m sorry,” he blurted out.

Eddie’s face immediately scrunched up in confusion, until his eyes landed on the drawings. Then he sighed and reached over to take them from Buck, tucking them back into the folder.

“It’s not your fault,” Eddie told him.

“Not my fault?” Buck scoffed. “I took him to the pier, I lost him. He- He saw all of that, he saw a woman drown, because I had one job and I fucked it up. Again!”

His boyfriend laid his hand on the back of Buck’s neck, using his grip there to tug him close. Close enough to rest their heads together, close enough to be breathing the same air.

“No,” Eddie said. “Your job was to keep the both of you alive. And look! You did that.”

“He’s having nightmares because he saw a woman drown,” Buck whispered to him. “I tried, Eds. I promise that I tried to keep him from- But I had to go and play hero and I let him fall and-”

“Not your fault,” Eddie whispered back. “You wouldn’t be you if you hadn’t tried to help them.”

“Dad? Buck?” The sound of Chris’ voice had them both stepping away from each other and turning to see him making his slow way towards them. “Can we go home now?”

“Yeah, sure,” Eddie said, tapping the folder against his leg. A folder that held the drawings showing how much Buck had failed. “Let’s get out of here. Maybe we can get something to eat on the way.”

Chapter End Notes
