I'm pretty sure I've ranted about this topic before, elsewhere, but I am convinced that half the driving force behind the Lawsuit was Buck getting so isolated and that if more people other than just Athena made the effort to reach out to him then it never would have gotten that far. You cannot change my mind on this.

Chapter 10

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“So.” Daniel placed his drink down on the table, propped his chin on his fist, and gave his sister his brightest smile. “Anything going on in your life?”

Maddie, sitting across from him, put on an expression of vague confusion.

Buck had come back from hanging out with the Diazes with both a new aura of guilt - apparently Chris spent a therapy session drawing images of the tsunami, including a drowning woman, which… Shit. - along with some relevant gossip. The kind of gossip one gets from a friend of a friend. Honestly, it shouldn’t have been something he heard about third hand at all. Chimney should’ve come to him or Buck about it, if he was so concerned.

Anyways, Daniel, being the concerned middle sibling that he was, called up his sister and invited her out to lunch. His treat, he’d said. Free food was always a good lure, and it worked as well on her as it did the youngest Buckley. So here they were, sitting in some diner not far from Metro Dispatch, eating food that honestly wasn’t good enough for how much Daniel knew he was going to be charged.

“Nothing?” he prompted when Maddie continued to stare at him. “There’s nothing you can think of?”

“Are you talking about the call-? Wait, you’re not “not dating” someone at dispatch so you can spy on me, too, are you?” 

“No.” Daniel frowned at her. “What call?”

“Then what are you talking about?” Maddie asked, continuing to parry his questions with ones of her own.

Daniel didn’t answer at first, reaching over to steal a fry from her plate and pop it into his mouth. She glared at him, pulling her plate closer to herself and glancing pointedly at his own pile of fries, sitting right next to his elbow. 

“Chimney’s worried about you,” he finally said.

“Oh, God.” Maddie rolled her eyes. “This is about the plate?”

“The plate that you broke and apparently ran away from.” Daniel nodded. “Yeah.”

“What, did Chimney tattle on me to you about it?” She sounded upset, now. Agitated. “Or to Buck?”

“Neither. Apparently he mentioned it to Eddie, who mentioned it to Buck.”

“He had no business-”

“Your boyfriend’s worried about you,” Daniel interrupted, cutting off that line of bullshit at the knees. “And he didn’t know what to do, so he vented to his friend. But because Rebar Head’s also an idiot, he didn’t think to mention it to his girlfriend’s brothers. Good thing I taught Eddie better years ago.”

“Well I’m fine,” Maddie said, stabbing her fork into her food. It scraped against the plate.

“I can tell,” Daniel said. “You’re perfectly fine.”

His sister glared at him.

“So what call were you talking about?” he asked, drawing in ketchup with a fry as he spoke. “The one you thought I was talking about.”

The change in Maddie’s demeanor was instant. She let out a sigh and slumped in her seat, looking absolutely miserable.

“There was… There was this woman, who called,” she said. “I could barely get anything out of her, and then a man - I think her husband - showed up, asked who she was talking to, and she told me I had the wrong number before hanging up.”

“Okay…”

“I checked,” she continued. “And she’s called before and hung up each time without ever saying anything. I think she was going to ask for help until he came back.”

Daniel let out a sigh and popped the ketchup covered fry into his mouth.

“Sucks,” was his succinct summation of things, given through a mouthful of potato. Buck wasn’t there, he didn’t need to pretend to have better table manners. “But there isn’t anything you can do, so don’t tear yourself up over it.”

“Someone has to do something,” Maddie argued. “Josh wanted to do a welfare check, except I know how much worse that can make things. But there has to be something I can do.”

“There isn’t,” Daniel said with a shrug. “Except wait for her to call again and hope she asks for help this time.”

“Dan, you don’t understand, you haven’t been where she is right now. I have.”

“You’re right,” he agreed. “But I have been where you are right now.”

His sister opened her mouth to spit out a reply, and then she caught herself at the last moment. She sat back in her chair and ducked her head a little, not quite able to meet Daniel’s eyes. 

“I just want to help her get out like I got out,” she finally said.

“I know.” And Daniel did. Definitely not in the same way, but still. “You could always just murder the husband, God knows I spent years regretting not going with that option.”

Maddie just rolled her eyes, even as her lips twitched a little.

“How’s Buck doing?” she asked, switching topics.

“He’s still having nightmares.” The answer had her grimacing in sympathy. “Athena invited him over for dinner. I think he’s gonna ask Nash for help getting around whatever or whoever’s keeping him from going back to work.”

The dinner invite hadn’t extended to him, which was probably a good thing. Nash had firmly settled himself on Daniel’s Shit List since the time he slammed Buck into a wall, and so dinner would’ve gotten awkward. Though, if the man managed to help Buck get back to firefighting and stop being miserable, maybe Daniel would reassess his opinion of him.

“I’m still worried,” Maddie said.

“I know,” Daniel said again. “But they have him doing a desk job right now, Maddie. Nevermind that it’s slowly killing Buck, it might literally kill him.”

“Yeah, that’s…” She shook her head. “Someone’s being a dumbass.”

“They are, and I wanna hit ‘em with my bat.”

Maddie rolled her eyes once more before checking her phone.

“I’ve gotta get back,” she told him, grabbing her purse and standing up. “Go ahead and eat my fries.”

“I am paying for ‘em.”

“Yeah, yeah.”

Buck pressed his finger into the doorbell and listened to it ring, rocking back on his heels while he clutched the tupperware he brought with him. The collared shirt he had on was buttoned up high enough to hide the marks that Hen had so gleefully pointed out at the station. There wasn’t a long wait before the door was opening, Bobby standing in the doorway with his customary wide smile. It seemed kinda off, though Buck might’ve just been seeing thing.

“Hello, Fire Marshall,” the man said. Buck let out a huff. Bobby’s gaze moved past him and he frowned. “You got a new car?”

“Huh?” Buck looked as well, out of reflex. “Oh, yeah. I had to, since the last one’s somewhere in the Pacific. I think.”

His new ride was also a jeep, if a slightly newer model since he had insurance money to play with. Buck had managed to talk Eddie into helping him break it in properly, which was a very fun experience. Even if it left him with a massive bruise in the small of his back in the exact size and shape as one of the seat belt buckles.

Bobby’s eyes dropped to the tupperware, and the frown became more pronounced.

“And you brought…”

“Sopapillas!” Buck held them up with a smile. “Quick, easy. Appeases the WASP-y instincts.”

They were his go-to thing to bring to someone’s house, and had been since Tia Alma taught him how to make them, precisely because of how quick and easy they were to make. They were just a nice little dessert pastry that he baked and then dusted in cinnamon sugar before calling it a day. No muss, no fuss, and it kept the phantom of his mother’s judgmental comments at bay.

“Well, come on in.” Bobby stepped to the side, allowing Buck to enter the house. “You have good timing, everything is almost done.”

They both paused at the top of the steps that led to the main floor of the house, because Buck’s stomach growled at the mention of food.

“I, uh. I didn’t eat much at lunch,” Buck explained, feeling his face heat up in embarrassment. “Didn’t want to fill up too soon.”

“I made plenty of food.”

When they made it downstairs and into the kitchen-slash-dining area, Athena was setting the table. There was a bottle of wine sitting out, along with a bowl of something in the center of the table that smelled wonderful. Or, Buck assumed it smelled wonderful, because there were a lot of things in the kitchen that smelled wonderful at that moment.

He frowned, though, at the number of plates that Athena was putting down.

“Where’s Harry and May?” he asked, handing the sergeant the tupperware when she reached for it.

She opened it, nodded approvingly, and went to grab another plate, presumably to put them out on.

“They’re with Michael,” Athena said, sharing a look with her husband that Buck couldn’t get a read on. “Do these go with anything?”

“Uh.” Buck shrugged. “They’re good with honey?”

“Good thing we have some, then.”

There was something awkward about how they were acting, not that Buck could figure out why he thought that. Athena kept shooting Bobby looks that Bobby, in turn, kept dodging. He wondered what it was about, if he’d interrupted an argument of some sort when he rang the doorbell.

“Oh! So I went and talked to that ambulance chaser,” Buck said, accepting a glass of water from Athena with a grateful smile.

“What for?” Bobby asked, carrying over the last of the food, a platter of what looked like country-fried steaks. The bowl that was already on the table when he arrived turned out to have candied yams in it.

“To see what he wanted,” Buck said. “Good thing I did, too, ‘cause the guy is trying to sue the city. Tried to convince me to help him.”

There was another exchange of loaded glances between husband and wife.

“Sue the city?” Athena asked, filling up a glass in front of him with white wine and then pouring herself some. Bobby had a glass of orange juice. “What for?”

“There was a logjam at the stairwell during the evacuation of the building,” Buck said, purposefully not looking Bobby’s way. He had warned the man to look out for that. “A… Few people got hurt, and they’re blaming the department. The lawyer guy got ahold of my report, and he thought I’d help him because I’m disgruntled.”

“So what did you tell him?”

“I pointed out that when everything gets cleared up and people stop being dumbasses, I’ll be one of the ones he’s trying to sue.” He shrugged. “So I told him to go screw himself. Not in those words, but still.”

“Well.” Bobby shared yet another loaded look with Athena. “Appreciate that.”

“Figured you would,” Buck said. He looked down at the food the captain had served him, practically salivating. “I have missed your cooking, Cap.”

“I missed your eating,” Bobby said. “Never have to worry about leftovers.”

“Danny used to complain about me being a bottomless pit.”

Athena chuckled, and held out a bowl.

“Collard greens?” she offered, but Buck was quick to shake his head.

“Nah, can’t. Too much vitamin K, it’ll mess with the blood thinners.”

“Oh.” Athena nodded, moved on to shoveling a serving onto Bobby’s plate. “Sounds like you’re taking your health very seriously.”

“Yeah, Chris and I spent a day going through and looking up what foods had too much of it for me to eat,” Buck told her. Though it made Eddie’s life as a parent a bit more difficult, because suddenly Chris had an excuse for them to banish broccoli and spinach and other greens from the house. “But I figure the better I manage this, the sooner I can go back to work.”

“That’s very mature of you.”

“Thank you,” he chirped, a bit pleased that she recognized that. “Hey, that, uh. That lawyer did get me thinking, though. There’s strength in numbers, right? So- So maybe I could get everyone to sign a statement of support or something? Show the higher-ups that you guys don’t think I’m a liability. They’d have to listen then, right?”

“Buck,” Bobby tried. “I want you to listen to me.”

“If you told them I was ready,” he said. Across from him, Athena let out a sigh and looked discomforted. “I mean, then these dumbasses, they wouldn’t have a right to keep me from-”

“I’m the dumbass.”

Bobby’s words were loud and clear, dropping onto the dining table between them with all the grace of a boulder. Buck fell silent and turned his head, staring at him in disbelief. No one said anything for the longest of moments.

“Uh,” Buck said dumbly. “What?”

“You’re not ready,” Bobby said. “That’s what I told them when they asked.”

Athena suddenly moved, grabbing a plate at the end of the table and holding it out between them.

“Would anyone like any cornbread?” Her smile looked liable to crack at any moment.

“You’re the reason they won’t let me back?” Buck asked, ignoring the cornbread. Blood was pounding in his ears.

“The medication is the reason.”

“Bobby is just worried about you, that’s all,” Athena tried to defend her husband. “We all are.”

“I thought you were on my side,” Buck said. “I thought you were my friend.”

“I am your friend,” Bobby lied. “I’ve also got a station full of other firefighters whose lives and safety depend on decisions I make, and I can’t put them at risk. If you’re not operating at a hundred percent-”

Buck interrupted him by slamming his fist onto the table. Both Bobby and Athena jumped in their seats.

“I am at a hundred percent!” he shouted. “I walked through Santa Monica for hours, bleeding, and didn’t stop until I got to Eddie and Chris. I am at a hundred percent, maybe even more.”

“Buck.” The patronizing way Bobby said his name had him grinding his teeth. “I know that you went through a lot in that tsunami and maybe you feel like you can survive anything-”

“Anything,” Buck scoffed. “Like a knife in my back.”

He pushed his chair out and stood, tossing his napkin onto the table next to his plate of uneaten food. His appetite had shriveled up and withered away.

“Um, Athena? I just wanna say thank you for inviting me,” he said. “But I’ve gotta go. And Bobby? Go fuck yourself.”

He left then. They tried calling him back, but he did not listen. The sound of the door slamming behind him as he left the house seemed to rattle the entire neighborhood.

“So where’s Buck?” Austin asked, settling onto the couch.

“Dinner with Athena and Nash,” Daniel answered, popping open a drink and handing it to him, before opening his own. “I didn’t get an invite.”

“Can’t imagine why.”

“I know, I’m a perfectly well-behaved guest,” Daniel joked, and grinned at the answering laughter. “How’re things at the station?”

“They’re fine. Kinda quiet, honestly. I hadn’t realized how loud your brother makes everything.”

“Oh, God.” Daniel flopped into the seat next to Austin, closer than he really needed to be, and kicked his feet up on the coffee table in the exact way he’d fuss at Buck for doing. “I love my brother, but he has always been loud. Like one time there was this woman at the store, right? And she had this giant mole on her cheek, pretty sure it had its own face. Anyways, Buck - he was like, eight - he tried to point it out to me and Maddie in a whisper. Except it was his version of a whisper, and it was not very quiet.”

Austin laughed again.

“Oh, no.”

“Yeah. She gave us the nastiest glare.”

“I bet.” Austin elbowed him, so Daniel elbowed him back. “Oh, Cynthia’s pregnant again.”

“That’ll be an age gap,” Daniel commented. “Your sister’s a couple years older than Maddie, right? Oh, that’s a geriatric pregnancy.”

“Emmett said she looked ready to strangle the doctor when he used that term, so I advise you not to,” Austin told him. “But guess who else is pregnant?”

“Have you peed on any sticks recently?”

“Hilarious. And impossible for so many reasons. No, Irene.”

“Sister and your sister-in-law. Nice,” Daniel said, and then, “Did that sound as weird to you as it did to me?”

“Yes. Anyways, Ethan’s starting a bet on whether his kid or Cynthia’s is gonna be born first. You want in?”

Daniel thought about it, and then he shrugged. 

“Fuck it, sure, why-”

The apartment door flew open and Buck stomped in, blindly tossing his keys onto the table just inside the apartment. They hit the wood and slid off onto the floor, not that Buck seemed to even notice. He was too busy going directly for one of the drawers in the kitchen area, yanking it open and digging through the random bits and bobs they threw in there.

Daniel shared a look with Austin. Then he got up, sat his drink down on the table and went to check on things.

“Buck? What’s going on?”

“I’m an idiot,” was Buck’s answer. Daniel got close enough to see that his face was red and tear-stained. He was immediately thrown back in time to finding him crying in his bed on the night of his senior prom, and felt a renewed urge murder Eddie. Though he knew, logically, it wasn’t him this time. Probably. Hopefully. “I’m just- This entire time, it was him!”

“What are you talking about?” Daniel hovered at Buck’s side. “You were supposed to be having dinner with Athena and Nash, what happened?”

“I told you, it was him!” Buck said, viciously tossing the contents of their junk drawer onto the floor and counter. “It’s Bobby! He’s the one keeping me from my job!”

Daniel sent Austin a bewildered look, the other man thankfully looking just as thrown as he felt. Buck let out something that might’ve been a cheer if not for how bitter it sounded, now holding a business card in his hand.

“What do you mean, Nash is keeping you from your job?”

“He- He told them I wasn’t ready,” Buck explained. “I asked him to help me, and he just- He stabbed me in the back, Danny.”

“Okay, fuck him.” Daniel watched as Buck pulled out his phone. “What are you doing?”

“I’m calling that lawyer,” Buck explained. “He wants my help suing the department? Fine! We can sue them and Bobby and-”

“Woah, woah, woah.” Daniel reached out and grabbed his brother’s wrist. “Let’s just- Let’s take a moment, okay? You’re angry, I get that. I’m angry, too. But let’s take a moment and think this through.”

“Think this through? Danny-”

“What do you want?” Daniel asked him. “Right here, right now. What do you want?”

“I-” Buck looked at him, eyes shining with tears building up again. “I want to be a firefighter again. I was only useful for two things, Danny, maybe three, and now I can’t- I’m pointless. There’s no point to me. When I was a firefighter, I could help people. I was useful! I- I want to be useful again.”

“Buck…”

“I want to be useful again,” he whispered. Begged, even. “I want to be a firefighter again.”

God, Daniel hated their mother so much at that moment. But for once, there might be someone he hated just as much, if not more. He was going to burn Nash.

“Okay.” He let go of Buck’s wrist, only so he could pluck the business card out of Buck’s hand and throw it somewhere behind him. “The lawyer’s a stupid idea.”

“Danny!”

“No. He’s an ambulance chaser,” Daniel told him, cupping his brother’s face with his hands. “You and Austin have both called him that. All he’s interested in is a payday. A lawsuit will get you money, that’s it. Is that what you want?”

Buck swallowed, shook his head.

“No.”

“Exactly. So here’s what we’re going to do,” Daniel said. “When’s your next check-up for the blood thinners?”

“In- In a couple weeks, why?”

“Not soon enough. You’re gonna call them tomorrow, get an appointment as early as you can. You’re going to tell them we want your leg checked for new clots, okay?”

Buck blinked at him. He was confused, clearly, but he also trusted Daniel, so he simply nodded and said,

“Okay.”

“I’m going to talk to people,” Daniel said, and wondered if those words sounded as threatening to Buck’s ears as they did his own, once they came out of his mouth. “Mehta, some others, whoever I can think of. See what they can do, if they can help side-step the asshole. And if that doesn’t work, then you’re going to the union.”

“The- The union? But they- Danny, they barely did anything for Bobby.”

“That was Nash.” Nash had, after all, lied to the LAFD about some very, very important things when he was hired. Like, for example, why he left the Saint Paul Fire Department. There wasn’t much their union could do in the face of nearly 150 deaths. “And if the union can’t help, or won’t, then we’ll sue the department into bankruptcy, okay?”

Buck let out a shuddering breath, leaning forward and letting Daniel hold his weight.

“Yeah,” he said, the word shaking in the air. “Yeah, okay.”

“So tomorrow, you set up that appointment,” Daniel recapped. “And I’ll start talking to people.”

On the couch, a silent Austin was looking somewhat worried.

Chapter End Notes

Someone start prayin' for Bobby, he's gonna need it.

Also, I know that some fics like to have Maddie falling on Chimney's side of things, and therefore Bobby's, but all we have to support that would be her comments at the end of 2x18 and her comments in the hospital scene of 3x01. Which is, coincidentally, the last time we, as an audience, see her and Buck together. Barring one single phone call, where they are an entire city apart, we don't see her and Buck interact again until 3x08. She has like, a single scene and barely any lines during the lawsuit arc.

And it is VERY interesting to me that they have Bobby citing the blood thinners for why he's blocking Buck's return to work, but also show that the man cooked him something that would react poorly with the blood thinners.

Chapter 11

Chapter Notes

I feel like there should be a content warning, but I don't know what the warning would be? Uh. Go into the last scene knowing that a certain someone isn't going to be very nice, I guess.
okay, CW for past child death, canon alcoholism, and... Let's go with a character having their past trauma weaponized against them.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Taking Chris to see the therapist hadn’t really helped with anything, or so Eddie found. In fact, it might have made things worse, because not only was Chris still waking up with nightmares, but there was another layer of guilt for Buck to needlessly bear. Eddie knew that his boyfriend had tried so very hard to protect Chris, not just from the tsunami itself but the horrors it caused, and he loved him all the more for it.

Chris falling into the water wasn’t Buck’s fault, and neither was whatever Chris was exposed to after that. Eddie just wished that Buck would believe him when he said that. He just wished that Buck understood he had done the one and only thing he’d needed to do that day, by keeping the both of them alive through something that had killed countless others.

When Eddie walked into the station for his next shift, he was irritated and agitated and more than a little sleep deprived. Because the nightmares hadn’t just disappeared after a single therapy session. Because Buck hadn’t called him after his dinner with Bobby and Athena, like he promised he would, and his texts this morning had been somewhat curt.

Eddie wished that the man would give him more than that, because it was clear that something had happened at that dinner last night. It was clear in the way that he could tell, even over just texts, that Buck’s mood had flipped on its head from the day before. It was clear in the way that Bobby, upon laying eyes on Eddie for the first time that shift, seemed to brace himself for some sort of reaction. It was clear in the way that Austin’s silence grew a little bit frostier whenever he had to share space with Bobby, which filled the atmosphere in the engine with awkward tension that none of them could manage to entirely relieve.

It was clear in the way that Bobby, after they got back from a call, stopped him at the trucks and hesitantly asked if he’d heard from Buck since last night, and then danced around why he would want to know that.

None of this helped Eddie’s mood any, and he ended up changing into his workout clothes and hitting the station’s gym to relieve some of that agitation. Specifically, he opted to start hitting the punching bag. Repeatedly.

Bosko seemed to have had the same idea, because she was using the equipment when he got there. Eddie wasn’t entirely certain how he felt about her. He liked her well enough, sure, and she was good enough at the job. But she wasn’t Buck, and working with her wasn’t as seamless as working with him was. Eddie still took the opportunity, when she came over to curiously poke and prod, to vent about Christopher and the therapy appointment.

“And when I asked if there was anything I could do in the meantime,” Eddie complained as he punched at the bag with a gloved fist, the other glove tucked under his arm. “He just told me to keep loving him.”

“Keep loving him.” Bosko’s voice dripped with derision as she repeated it. “Did he write that on a prescription pad?”

“It’s not bad advice, I guess,” Eddie said. “It’s just… I was kinda hoping for more, you know? I guess I was hoping to walk in there and have the guy tell me what to do to make the nightmares stop. I could’ve gone to Danny and gotten better advice.” At the confusion on her face, he answered her unasked question with, “Buck’s brother.”

“Your partner, Buck.”

“Uh, yeah.” His arm slipped and he had to fumble to catch the unused glove before it hit the ground. “My partner.”

Bosko nodded, and then thankfully moved on, saying,

“Headshrinkers have their place. It’s a very touchy-feely place, but that works for some people.”

“Not you?”

“I went to one, once,” she confessed. “When I was a kid, after my dad died. She had hand puppets.”

Eddie couldn’t help the way he scoffed at that. Hand puppets? Seriously?

“Did that help?” he asked, already figuring the answer.

“I made her cry,” Bosko said. “And then I told my mother I didn’t want to go back.”

“Great.” Eddie rolled his eyes. “I feel encouraged.”

“Look, everyone deals with trauma differently,” she said. “I don’t know about your kid, but I know what worked for me. It was when my mom told me how she was feeling, that she was sad and that it was okay. However we felt, we were in it together, no matter what.”

“See, that?” Eddie rested his fist against the punching bag. “That’s helpful. Also sounds like the same kind of advice I’d get from Danny.”

Actually, he was fairly certain it was a longer version of the advice that Danny already gave him, last Christmas: “Talk to your fucking kid.” And sure, he tried to follow it, but that ended up being more trying to get Chris to talk to him. 

“She’s a good mom,” Bosko said, a bit quietly. “He sounds like a good brother.”

“He is.”

Mehta’s office didn’t look too terribly different from the office of Daniel’s previous fire captain, Simon Paz, down in El Paso. There was a distinct lack of ranch decor, sure, but both desks had the same kind of clutter sitting on them with the same kind of knick knacks decorating the shelves. Honestly, the most obvious difference was that the flag of Mexico wasn’t hanging on the wall.

(And, Daniel was willing to bet, there wasn’t a stash of mezcal in the desk drawer.)

“So what’s going on?” Mehta asked, leaning back in his seat.

“Captain Nash is an asshole.”

“Yeah, you said that,” his captain said. “That doesn’t really tell me much of anything.”

“Buck’s been trying to figure out who’s keeping him from returning to work.” Daniel was gripping the back of a chair tight enough that his knuckles went white. “He got invited to dinner with his captain and his wife, and Buck decided to take the chance to ask Nash for help. Only it turns out that Nash is the person blocking him, telling the higher ups that Buck’s a liability and isn’t ready.”

“Well,” Mehta said, and Daniel already knew he wasn’t going to like the words that came out of the man’s mouth. “Your brother’s on blood thinners, isn’t he?”

“Blood thinners because he threw a clot, but clearly no one’s actually concerned about his health or else they wouldn’t have him doing a goddamn desk job.”

Mehta grimaced, because he, at least, was smart enough to catch the implications of that.

“Still, though,” the captain said. “I can understand being concerned about-”

“He was on them when he survived the tsunami,” Daniel said, cutting him off sharply and ruthlessly. “He was on them when he saved I don’t know how many people, however many could fit on top of the One Thirty-six’s truck, on his own. And he was on them when he was stumbling through Santa Monica, still helping people, even while bleeding from a wound he only got because he was doing all that without his gear.”

That was the thing, right there. Everyone kept bringing up the blood thinners as if Buck was liable to slice himself on something his first call out and bleed to death at the scene. As if the gear they wore wasn’t designed specifically to protect from that kind of thing. If Buck managed to get sliced or stabbed through his turnout coat, it would be a source of concern whether he was on blood thinners or not.

“Why are we having this conversation?” Mehta asked him. “What is it that you’re asking for?”

“I want you to help get Buck back to active duty,” Daniel answered. “I mean, there has to be something you can do, right? Because I gotta tell ya, he’s desperate and he’s got that ambulance chaser nagging in his ear, looking for a payday.”

Not that he was going to let that happen, the lawyer’s business card was torn to shreds and thrown away, but Daniel wasn’t above leveraging the threat to get what he wanted. It had worked against his parents, after all. Judging from the expression on Mehta’s face, it was working here, too.

“I’ll talk to Miranda,” the captain said. “See what I can do. I can’t promise anything, though. If Nash is the only thing keeping him back-”

“He is.”

“Then there shouldn’t be any reason your brother can’t be placed back on full duty,” Mehta said. Daniel swallowed back the cheer that wanted to burst out at the comment. “Worse comes to worse, he just gets transferred to another station. How attached is he to the One Eighteen?”

“I’m… Not certain, sir,” Daniel said. “I think he cares more about returning to firefighting than he does about where he returns to do it.”

“We do have an opening,” Mehta said, seemingly more to himself than to Daniel. Daniel wasn’t really certain how he felt about Buck working at the 133 with him, and so let it go without comment. “Like I said, I’ll talk to Miranda. She can at least see about getting him assigned to something better than a desk job.”

“Thank you, sir.”

Daniel started to leave the office and get started on checking their fire engine over, when the captain calling his name stopped him at the door. He half-turned, wondering what Mehta wanted now.

“Do you play poker?” the captain asked him.

The apartment was quiet. Danny was on a shift, which meant Buck had the place to himself. Normally, these days, he’d go to Eddie’s place and hang out with him. But ever since that dinner at Bobby and Athena’s, ever since that betrayal made itself known, all Buck wanted to do was hide and mope. 

He had trusted Bobby, had thought the man was his friend, had believed him when the man claimed to be keeping a place at the station for him. But that was all a lie, wasn’t it? Bobby had been lying to him the entire time, and there was an incessant voice at the back of Buck’s head asking if maybe the others had known.

Had they been laughing at him?

Had they known that Bobby was already fishing for his replacement?

Did they gossip about it behind his back? Did they talk about it with derision or with pity? With relief?

Buck didn’t think Austin had known, the man seemed pretty surprised to be hearing about it. And he’d liked to believe, to hope, that if Eddie knew, then Eddie would have told him. But the others? Chimney, who he only really saw or talked to when they both happened to be with Maddie at the same time? Hen, who Buck hardly heard from at all and barely saw anymore?

Had they known? Was that why they ran away from the conversation when he asked about Bosko? Had they known she was there as Buck’s potential replacement and not simply a fill-in?

He didn’t want to think that, he truly didn’t. But the apartment was quiet, and he was never very good at keeping his thoughts from going off on the worst of tangents when he was bereft of distractions. He really should go to Eddie’s, he knew that, if only to keep from ending up in the same miserable state as before the tsunami.

But going to Eddie’s meant facing Chris. Spending the night at Eddie’s meant being there for another nightmare that he was responsible for. And Eddie was already asking about the dinner with Bobby and Athena, asking how it had gone. It was easier to avoid that conversation over the phone than it was in person. It was easier to avoid asking Eddie if he’d known, because Buck would like to believe that he hadn’t, but… 

But, but, but.

So, instead, Buck was moping on a couch that needed replacing half a decade ago, trying to find something to watch that would fill his head with more than his own thoughts. It was proving to be a fruitless endeavor, because nothing that he stumbled across managed to pique his interest. He was about to give it up as a loss and try to find something in their collection of DVDs, when his phone rang.

The name on the screen made him frown and hesitate, but he answered it all the same.

“Hey, Hen. What’s up?”

“So we had a call,” Hen said. “It got me talking to Karen, and it got me thinking of you, and I realized that I really, really, really dropped the ball.”

“Hen…”

“Before the tsunami, I meant to swing by and see you,” she continued. “But it didn’t happen. And I can’t say why it didn’t happen, except that I just dropped the ball.”

“You have a kid,” Buck told her. “And a job with weird hours, and you’re doing that IVF thing with Karen. You’re busy, I get it.”

“None of which kept me from at least picking up the phone,” Hen said. “Exhibit A, we’re having this conversation now. I work with almost everyone I know, and most everyone else I see when I take Denny over to hang out with Harry. I just… Didn’t think about it, and I dropped the ball.”

“Well. You’re calling now.” Buck put his feet up on the coffee table, relaxing a little now that he not only had a distraction, but a distraction in the form of his friend talking to him. There was the brief urge to ask if she knew about Bobby, but he squashed it down. “So what about that call had you thinking about me?”

“There were these two sisters,” Hen said. “ They were fighting over their dead mother’s ring. We had to cut it off one of them, and the other only wanted it because she’d already sold it. They just kept saying the nastiest things to each other while we were there.”

“And these two ladies made you think of me?”

“No. I mean, kinda. They guessed I was an only child-”

“I figured that out my first week knowing you.”

“How!?”

“The biggest sign was that you didn’t guard your food around me like Danny and Maddie do.”

“Yeah, I don’t think that’s a sign of being an only child,” Hen said, sarcasm lacing her syllables. “I think that’s more a sign that I didn’t grow up with a raccoon.”

“Raccoons are adorable, so I’ll take that as a compliment.” Buck’s phone beeped in his ear, and he pulled it away to glance at the screen. “Hey, Eddie’s calling-”

“Answer it, then,” Hen told him. “I’ll talk to you later. And I will make time to see you, too.”

“Bye, Hen,” he said, feeling lighter as that call ended and he answered Eddie. “Hey.”

“Hey.” Eddie sounded weary. “I just put Chris down for bed.”

“Here’s hoping he doesn’t have another nightmare tonight,” Buck said with a frown. “Is, uh. Is everything okay?”

The line crackled a little with Eddie’s sigh.

“It’s Shannon.”

“It’s- What?”

“The drowning woman, it’s Shannon,” Eddie said. “She’s who the nightmares are about.”

“God. How did you…?”

“I saw another drawing he did, made the connection,” his boyfriend said. “He didn’t tell me because he didn’t want to make me sad.”

“God,” Buck said again. “I don’t even… I’m pretty sure I just made Danny sadder, whenever I tried to pull that trick.”

“Yeah,” Eddie agreed. “But… This is just his brain, I don’t know. Conflating the two. The tsunami and Shannon dying.”

“Yeah, I- I guess…”

“So it’s not your fault,” Eddie said. “He’s not having nightmares because you didn’t protect him well enough. He’s not having nightmares because of anything he saw, if he even saw anything. It’s not your fault.”

“But-”

“It’s not your fault,” his boyfriend repeated. “What about you? You said you’d tell me how the dinner with Bobby and Athena went, but you never did. I’m figuring it didn’t go well, from how everyone’s acting.”

“Yeah, it… It’s Bobby.”

“What’s Bobby?”

“The reason I’m not back to work,” Buck told him. “He’s telling them I’m a liability, that I’m not ready.”

“That you’re- What the fuck!? You saved my kid!”

“And you… You didn’t know?”

“No!” was Eddie’s vehement answer. “No one else does, either, I don’t think. They haven’t said anything to me, anyways. Does Danny know? I mean, I’m guessing he does. It explains why Austin was so frosty to Bobby today. Shit, should we warn Athena?”

“I don’t know, I’m not feeling very charitable towards him right now,” Buck confessed. “Danny said he’d talk to people, see what they can do, before going to the union. And I have an appointment to have them check my leg for clots.”

“Is something wrong!?”

Eddie sounded so worried that Buck immediately started shaking his head, before remembering that Eddie couldn’t see him.

“No, no,” he hurried to assure him. “It’s- I dunno, Danny said to do it, so I am. I already had an appointment for the ‘thinners in a couple weeks, I just got it moved up.”

“Okay, okay. Good.” Eddie let out another sigh. “Danny’s got a shift tonight, doesn’t he? Just come over, Buck. I don’t know why you didn’t to begin with.”

“Yeah, yeah okay. I’ll, uh. I’ll pack a bag and head over.”

“Good. Then I’ll see you when you get here,” his boyfriend told him. “I love you, I’ll leave the porchlight on.”

Buck sat on the couch, which needed to have been replaced a long time ago, and simply smiled at his phone after the call ended, feeling much better than he had prior to his phone ringing. Then he pushed himself up and went to go pack that bag.

In the retelling, for the sake of dramatization, Eddie might say that Danny’s arrival was heralded by the gathering of storm clouds that blotted out the California sun, by a black cat running into the truck bay to yowl in panic, by the lights of the station flickering exactly thirteen times, or perhaps by the sirens on both trucks and the ambulances going off on their own. In truth, the only omen that foretold his coming was Austin getting a text on his phone and then loudly proclaiming,

“Oh, fuck.”

They were in the middle of having lunch, which had been delayed by a trip to rescue a woman who’d gotten stuck in a barrel at a rage room. Eddie was sitting as far away from Bobby as possible, joining Austin in the cold front they were directing at their captain. The icy attitude had not gone unnoticed by the others, but he was refusing to talk about it on account of the fact that he wasn’t sure what Buck wanted others to know. And honestly, he didn’t want to be pissed off at Hen or Chimney if they turned out to agree with their captain. Which, now that he thought about it, might explain why Buck was so hesitant to tell anyone else.

(There was a part of Eddie, the part that went through drill after drill at boot camp until he no longer thought, until he just listened and did, that urged him to fall into line and respect the chain of command. Maybe he would have listened to it in another life and supported Bobby’s argument that Buck wasn’t ready. Thankfully, he wasn’t currently living that life.)

Anyways, in the middle of everyone eating their late lunch, Austin pulled out his phone, checked the text he just received, and swore. It drew the attention of everyone at the table, because Austin, for the most part, was silent and easily ignored. He did not typically interrupt everyone with panicked cussing.

“What?” Chimney asked, from farther up the table, next to Hen and Bobby.

“Nothing,” Austin answered him, and then asked Eddie, “Do you have Cap’s wife’s number?”

“Uh, yeah. Why?”

“She should probably swing by the station. Quickly.”

Austin didn’t say anything more. He simply picked up his plate, took it to the sink to be rinsed off by whoever’s turn it was to do dishes, and headed down the stairs to the station’s bay. Eddie, as lost as everyone else, simply shrugged and shot Athena a text telling her to hurry to the station.

Danny’s arrival was not heralded by storm clouds or a yowling cat or flickering lights or emergency sirens. There were only briefly raised voices and footsteps that echoed through the station. And then Danny crested the top of the stairs, a worried looking Austin following close behind.

Bobby looked alarmed at the sight of him, almost leaping out of his chair and to his feet.

“Who’s that?” Bosko, sitting on Eddie’s other side, asked.

“That’s Danny,” Eddie said, getting up as well and suddenly very thankful that he’d texted Athena. Danny looked as pissed off as Eddie had ever seen him. “Buck’s brother.”

“Oh,” was Bosko’s response.

“If I had any respect for you, I’d offer to do this in your office,” Danny said, and for how pissed he looked, his tone was calm. It made Eddie even more concerned. “But you’re a pathetic sack of shit, so we’re gonna do this here, in front of God and everybody.”

“Dan-” Chimney tried, getting up as well and stepping forward.

“Shove it, Han,” Danny spat at him, without even looking his way.

“Daniel,” Bobby said. Eddie couldn’t help but notice that he kept the table between himself and Danny. “I don’t know what Buck told you-”

“He told me what you told him,” Danny said. “That you’re the reason he’s not here, right now. That you told the brass that he’s a liability, that he’s not ready.”

“Because he isn’t. I understand that after the tsunami, he might feel like he can do anything, but he’s pushing himself too far, too fast. He has been this entire summer, and neither of us should have allowed it. He’s already thrown one clot-”

“You wanna talk about the blood clots?” Danny let out a laugh that was filled with more bitterness than cheer. “Let’s talk about how he just had a doctor’s appointment to check for more clots in his leg, since you’re one of the idiots who’s keeping him at a desk job.”

Still in her seat, Hen’s eyes went wide and she covered her mouth with her hand.

“He’s nearly died three times this year, Daniel,” Bobby said. “I’d assume you’d understand my desire to keep him safe. That’s all I’m trying to do, keep him safe and out of danger.”

“You know, you sound just like our mother,” Danny said. Eddie winced, because he understood the weight of that insult. “He isn’t your kid, Nash. You should be able to recognize that, since he’s not charbroiled.”

That line landed like a punch to Bobby’s solar plexus, and it had several people at the table gasping.

“Buck is my kid,” Danny said, stalking up to the table that separated him from the captain. The people sitting there scattered from their seats to get out of his way. Austin kept close to him the entire time, and Eddie wasn’t sure if that was out of support or because the man wanted to be near enough to grab him if the need arose. “And that means I’m not going to let your stupidity make him miserable, any more than I let our actual parents do it. I’m not going to let you smother him to death anymore than I let my mother.”

“I’m not-” Bobby tried, but Danny wasn’t there for a conversation, that much was clear. 

“Here are the choices, Nash,” Danny said. “Buck is a firefighter, and he can either be a firefighter here or he can be a firefighter somewhere else.”

“What?” Eddie blurted out, and then cringed when Danny’s gaze turned on him.

“Mehta’s already got a spot at the One Thirty-three all prettied up for him,” he said, turning back to a pained looking Bobby. “If you don’t want him, fine. There’re plenty of captains who do.”

“And if I don’t approve the transfer?” Bobby asked, glaring at Danny

“I’m pretty sure you won’t get a choice,” Danny shot back. “But if you try? Then we’ll go to the union, and if they have to choose between you and him? I don’t think it’ll go well for you. I mean, he survived being at ground zero of the tsunami and then saved who knows how many people by himself, despite you and everyone else claiming he wasn’t ready. You?”

Danny placed his palms on the table and leaned forward.

“It was almost a hundred-fifty people who died in that fire, wasn’t it? That you then lied about to the department?”

“Daniel.” The suddenness of Athena’s voice had most of them startling. She was at the top of the stairs, in uniform, and she did not look happy. “That is enough.”

Danny merely glanced over his shoulder at her, almost dismissively.

“Buck is my kid,” he told Bobby, who had gone silent and whose face had gone utterly blank. “I promised him when he was a baby that I would take care of him, and I’m not going to stop now. Even if that means I have to crush a drug-addled alcoholic because he decided to stand in my way.”

“Daniel!” Athena barked.

He glared at Bobby for a moment longer, before stepping back and turning on his heel. Athena barely moved out of the way enough for him to go by, glaring daggers the entire time. Austin followed, though no one else moved. Not until Bobby did, hurrying in the direction of the captain’s office. Athena rushed after him.

There was a silence that lingered heavily over them all that remained unbroken as they stared awkwardly at one another, until Chimney said what they were all thinking:

“Holy shit.”

Chapter End Notes

So...
How are we all feelin' about Daniel Buckley now?

Chapter 12

There was, perhaps, a single good thing about more blood clots beginning to form in Buck’s leg, and that was the angry letters his hematologist immediately started drafting to send to the department. And immediately, in this case, meant that he watched the man pull out a small notebook from a pocket and start scribbling away, muttering to himself while a nurse started putting away the equipment. Buck was fairly certain the first draft was going to consist entirely of cuss words and a few dozen ways to call Buck’s bosses idiots. Just going by what was being muttered, at least.

The doctor had initially started with a lecture about how Buck needed to be following the recommended exercises, until Danny - wearing an expression that Buck recognized as barely restrained rage - informed the doctor what sort of light duties the department brass had saddled Buck with. That was when the notebook came out and the angry muttering began.

They weren’t big blood clots, thankfully, at least not yet. And they were found near where the hardware holding Buck’s leg bones together was located. So maybe there were two good things that came out of this, because maybe this meant they knew, now, what was actually causing the clots. Maybe this was proof that this wasn’t a forever kind of thing. 

Mostly, though, Buck felt frustration and, yes, a bit of that same anger his brother and the doctor were expressing.  The blood thinners were meant to prevent more blood clots from forming, and the leg exercises were supposed to help keep his circulation going. But there was only so much either could do in the face of a light duty where he spent hours in a row sitting at a desk and tackling administrative tasks.

The only thing that had been keeping any of it tolerable was that it was meant to be temporary. A short term situation that would only last until he could get back onto the field. Except, apparently, Bobby meant for it to last as long as he could make it last.

Because they weren’t big enough yet to be concerning, the doctor opted to give it time to see if the blood thinners would manage to dissolve the clots on their own or not. They scheduled a follow-up appointment in a week, where they’d check again and decide if they were going to admit him for thrombolytic therapy. Which was annoying, because they’d want to keep him monitored and that meant going through an entire day and night of prodding.

Danny drove them, which meant Buck was stuck waiting for him; as soon as the doctor dismissed them, Danny had pulled out his phone and called someone. Buck thought it might’ve been Danny’s captain, but he wasn’t certain. He just knew that he was stuck playing on his own phone while he waited for Danny to finish up.

Afterwards, Danny took Buck home, only to drop him off at the front door of their building with a claim that he had an errand to run. Buck almost questioned it, just out of curiosity and because of how agitated his brother still seemed, but he decided to let it go. He knew the conversation over the phone had been about him, just from how his name was bandied about, and he could assume that this errand was related.

His big brother said he was going to help Buck get back to being a firefighter, and Buck was going to trust him on that.

(Buck absolutely should have questioned where Danny was going, with how angry the man was about the discovery of more blood clots. But alas…)

When Buck got to their apartment and let himself in, he changed into looser clothes and pushed the coffee table out of the way so he could go about doing the leg exercises to help with his blood flow. After he got done with those, having gone through the whole list of them - the ones he did while laying down, while he was sitting, and the ones done standing - he started looking up other things he could do to help with his circulation and prevent blood clots. Because he did not want to give everyone a reason to believe Bobby when the man claimed he was a liability.

By the time Danny got back, Buck was next to the TV, laying on the floor with his legs stretched up along the wall. When his brother walked in and laid eyes on him, he paused just inside the door, head cocked to the side. Buck stared back, vision upside-down, and gave a little wave.

“What are you doing?” Danny asked, sounding much less agitated than when Buck last saw him.

“Yoga poses, to help with circulation.” Buck swung his legs over, rolling onto his stomach and then pushing himself up to his knees. He grinned at his brother. “Could’ve been more awkward, you could've walked in on me doing the downward dog.”

“Isn’t that your favorite yoga pose?”

Buck let out a laugh as he got to his feet. He looked around for his phone, and realized it was probably laying on his bed, where he’d thrown it before changing clothes.

“I talked to Mehta.” Danny tossed his keys onto the table just inside the door and toed off his shoes. They were kicked in a heap next to the trusty baseball bat. “And he’s talked to Chief Williams, who is apparently, uh. Not happy? With the fact that you’re chained to a desk, considering why you’re on light duties.”

“That’s good, right?” Buck asked, moving the coffee table back to where it was supposed to be.

“I think so,” Danny said. “Especially when your doctor sends that angry letter to the brass. But Mehta’s already said there’s a spot for you at the One Thirty-three, if nothing else.”

“Wait. Really?” Buck felt his heart pound at the words. “Why?”

“Why?” His brother looked at him like he was questioning his intelligence, which was a look he’d gotten a lot growing up. “Why not? How many people did you pull out of the water?”

“Dunno.” Buck shrugged. “I wasn’t really keeping count.”

“Chris said it was enough that the truck you were on got pretty crowded,” Danny said. “You’re a good firefighter, why wouldn’t he want you?”

Buck felt the smile stretch across his face and ducked his head to hide it, rubbing at the back of his neck.

“Well- Well hopefully it won’t come to that,” he said. Buck didn’t know how well that would work out, the two of them working at the same station. Not just the confusion from having the same name, but how Danny would handle it. “I mean, if the brass realize I’m not a liability, Bobby’ll have to let me come back. Right?”

“Right.” There was something odd about Danny’s tone that Buck couldn’t quite put his finger on. “I’m sure having the choices laid out in front of him is helpful, too.”

“Choices?”

“Yeah, I mean.” Danny shrugged and flopped onto the couch, picking up the remote. “They’ll probably give him the option of letting you transfer elsewhere.”

Buck wasn’t really certain how he felt about that. He wanted to be a firefighter again, and he wanted to be a firefighter at the 118 with Eddie and Chimney and Hen and everyone else. But if he couldn’t? If it was a choice of being a firefighter elsewhere or not at all?

Buck did not know how he felt about that.

One of Buck’s biggest flaws was that, a lot of times, “out of sight, out of mind” was horribly, horribly true for him. He could fully intend to clean something up, only to walk into the other room for some reason, get distracted, and completely forget that the mess existed. It had gotten him in trouble many times before, and undoubtedly would again.

This time, it was his phone. He’d thrown it on top of his bed when he changed into looser clothes for the stretches and the yoga, and then simply forgot it existed. It was in his bedroom, he was in the living room, and that might as well have been another reality for all it mattered. He didn’t remember it existed until it started ringing, snagging Buck’s attention from the search for ingredients to make into a quick dinner.

Buck pulled himself out of the fridge and bounded across the apartment, ignoring Danny’s off-hand joke about being a giant puppy, and scooped his phone off the bed. It was Eddie calling, and before he answered it he saw that he’d missed several texts from him and Hen.

“Hey, Eds,” he said. “Sorry, I was in the other room.”

“All day?”

“Yeah. I, um. Forgot about it.”

“Of course you did, I remember how many pencils you just left behind in class.” Eddie sounded amused and wistful. “Hey, uh. You had that doctor’s appointment today, right?”

“I did,” Buck confirmed, heading back to the kitchen. He pinned his phone between his shoulder and his ear so he had both hands free. “Is that what all the texts are about?”

Out of the corner of his eye, he saw Danny sit up from where he’d been lounging on the couch.

“Some of them. I mean, a few of mine. I think Hen was texting you, too?”

“Yeah.”

In the background, he was pretty sure he heard Hen say something unkind about him and his inability to respond to a text. It had Eddie laughing, whatever she said.

“So was there… What did the doctor say?”

Buck let out a sigh and pressed his forehead against the fridge door. He was careful not to press too hard, though, because he didn’t need a giant bruise on his forehead to match his birthmark. The blood thinners meant he had more than enough random bruises everywhere else.

“They found more clots.”

“They found- Shit.”

“Tiny ones, but still. The doctor wasn’t happy,” Buck said. “They’re small, though. I have another appointment in a week, just before Halloween, to see if they dissolve on their own before then.”

“Him being so pissed off when he got here makes sense now, shit.”

Buck frowned and stood straighter.

“When who got there?” he asked, but didn’t get to hear the response because, at that moment, their front door flew open.

“Daniel Buckley, what the fuck is wrong with you!?” Maddie shouted, stomping in and letting the door fall shut behind her.

“Eds, I’m gonna have to call you back.”

 “Hi, Mads.” Danny let out a sigh and got to his feet. “Are you joining us for dinner?”

“You called him a drug-addled alcoholic, Dan!” Maddie snapped, shoving her purse down onto the table near the door. “What the hell!?”

“Okay, stop.” Buck was still clutching at his phone, though he’d already ended the call with Eddie. “Who did Danny call a drug-addled alcoholic?”

“Bobby!”

“What the fuck, Danny!?”

“Nash is an asshole,” Danny said with a shrug, crossing the room to poke at the ingredients Buck had pulled out and piled on the kitchen table. “What are you making, anyway?”

“Stir fry,” Buck answered automatically. “When did you-? No, I bet you went to the station after you dropped me off here. Why did you?”

“It needed to be said.”

“He needed to be called a-” Buck shook his head, staring aghast at his older brother. “Seriously?”

“Okay, maybe not that part.”

“Apparently, according to Chimney,” Maddie said, arms crossed and glaring at Danny. “He just stormed into the station and started yelling at Bobby.”

“I didn’t raise my voice once,” Danny argued. He dug out a carrot and bit into it, dodging Buck’s swat. “I just made sure he knew where he stood.”

“You threw the fire in his face!”

“You…” Buck stared at his older brother, lost for words. Something ugly squirmed in his chest and his stomach curdled, and he thought he might puke. “Danny, why?”

“He needed to know where he stood.” Danny just shrugged, like- like- God, like he didn’t care at all, not really. “He threatened to try and keep you from transferring to another station, and I informed him why you going to the union wouldn’t go well for him.”

Buck pulled out a chair and collapsed into it, propping his elbows on the table and burying his face in his hands. He had to apologize to Bobby, he knew that, but he had no idea how to even start. He’d never had to apologize to someone because of something his big brother had done before. “Sorry my brother is a dick” didn’t seem quite enough to cover it.

“Jesus, Danny.”

“What, you expect me to talk nice to the asshole?” Danny scoffed. “I tolerated him after he slammed you into a wall-”

“Austin told you about that!?” Buck yelped, at the same time as Maddie demanded, “He did what!?”

“But that’s gone when his being an asshole is affecting your health, Buck.”

“So? You can’t just do that!” Buck said. “How would you like it if someone called you a wh-!?”

“Don’t,” Danny snapped.

Buck threw his hands out, his point proven. Though, when he remembered Maddie was standing there, looking between them with a frown that put a little furrow in her brow, he felt guilty for the jab. Because he wasn’t a dick, unlike a certain other Buckley.

Jesus Christ.

“How is it affecting your health?” Maddie apparently decided to focus on that, instead of Buck’s jab at their brother. Which he was very thankful for.

“They found more clots today,” Buck told her, reaching down to rub at his leg. “Not big enough to be concerning yet, they’re gonna wait and see if they dissolve on their own, but…”

“It’s because of that asshole,” Danny said, gesturing with a half-eaten carrot. “A desk job was the worst thing you could’ve been doing.”

“He’s not the one who assigned me to the job, Danny,” Buck said, rolling his eyes.

“He’s the reason you’ve been stuck there for weeks, and if he had his way, you wouldn’t leave.”

Buck shook his head at him, and then turned to Maddie.

“Like I said, I’m making stir fry. Do you want to stay for dinner?”

Maddie mulled it over, before nodding.

“Sure.”

“Awesome. I hope you’re in the mood for spicy, because that’s what it’ll be.”

Danny frowned at him.

“I don’t like it too spicy,” he reminded Buck.

“I know,” Buck said, pushing himself out of his seat to dig out the chili garlic sauce he knew they had somewhere. “But I don’t really care what you like right now.”

His brother glowered at him.

Chapter 13

Chapter Notes

Tomorrow is my birthday. I grow older yet.
(Every year, I understand Fran Fine a little bit more.)
cw for - Internalized homophobia; author typical levels of ACAB; a vague, blink-and-you-miss-it reference to SA

See the end of the chapter for more notes

It had been a couple days since the appointment where they found more clots in Buck’s leg, and since Danny marched into the station and made an ass of himself in front of the vast majority of Buck’s friends. Buck hadn’t talked to Bobby yet, having no idea how to broach that topic, but he’d heard from the others. Especially when word got around about the clots.

Hen, even before she knew about them, had sent him texts where she flagellated herself for being an idiot that missed something so obvious. She sent more, which included her unflattering opinions of whoever assigned him the desk job, when the news about the clots broke. Everyone else had sent similar messages. Even Athena sent him a text that consisted of telling him that his brother was an ass, and then another reminding him that she still had his tupperware.

There’d been nothing from Bobby, though.

There had also been nothing official from the department so far, which Buck was trying not to fret over. Bureaucracy was slow, he knew. There was every bit the likelihood that he was the topic of discussion in emails and conference calls, maybe even meetings. Danny had said that Mehta had said that Chief Williams, the battalion chief that oversaw both the 118 and the 133, was not best pleased with Buck’s current circumstances, and he doubted that angry letter from his hematologist would make it any better.

Today, though, instead of worrying about that, Buck had something to look forward to. Even if he had to suffer through Eddie being a worry-wart of a father first, because Chris was going to a birthday party and then, much to the kid’s excitement and Eddie’s anxiety, going to a sleepover.

The parents of the birthday boy, Buck was fairly certain the kid’s name was Dillon, had rented space at the Party Playroom. “Where Kids Rule!” or so said their sign. Buck went with Eddie to drop Chris off, and was trying his best not to laugh too much at his boyfriend.

“Are you sure about this?” Eddie asked Chris for what had to be the twentieth time since Buck joined them that day. “We can always pick you up after the party.”

“No, Dad, I- I wanna go.”

Thankfully. Buck adored Chris, but he absolutely did not want to pick him up after the party. There were plans, damn it, that would only work if Eddie’s house remained child-free. Sure, yes, Buck would drop everything - including Eddie, depending on what they were doing - to get the kid if he called, asking to come home. But Buck would much like it if it didn’t come to that.

“Hey, guys! You made it!” That was Tasha, Dillon’s mom. Or, Buck thought her name was Tasha. “Dillon is so excited, maybe now he’ll stop talking about it. But who am I kidding? No he won’t.”

She let out a laugh and glanced at Buck curiously, leaning against the passenger side of Eddie’s truck.

“This is Buck!” Chris cheerfully informed her.

“Hi,” Buck said with a wave.

“You got my emails, right?” Eddie asked. He was kneeling in front of Chris, a rolling suitcase that contained a change of clothes standing next to them. A wrapped birthday present was laying on it.

“I did.” Tasha nodded. “Very detailed.”

Buck tried to cover up his laughter, he did. The little scowl Eddie sent him said that he’d failed horribly.

“Alright, be good and have fun,” Eddie told Chris. “And call me if you need anything, okay?”

“Okay, Dad. Love you.”

“Love you too, now go, go, go.”

That seemed to be all the permission Chris needed, because he was soon rushing - as much as he could ever rush - for the entrance to the place, where another kid was patiently waiting. They’d parked in a handicap spot, so the entrance was right there.

Tasha grabbed the wrapped present and the handle of the suitcase, chuckling as Eddie trailed along in his son’s wake for a couple steps.

“He’s going to be okay,” she said.

“I know,” Eddie said, still watching Chris trundle off. Chris hadn’t looked back once. “The nightmares seem to have stopped, the doctor says a sleepover will be great for him. I’m not sure how good it’s gonna be for me.”

“Oh, I’m thinkin’ it’ll be really good,” Buck said, and grinned at the look Eddie shot at him.

“Listen, don’t worry,” Tasha told Eddie. “Dillon’s got this whole camping situation set up in the backyard, Ron’ll be in the tent next to them. We’ll call you if there’s any problem. Now go relax, recharge. Enjoy your night.” Tasha shot both Buck and Eddie a sly look then, though Eddie missed it. “I will return him to you in one piece tomorrow.”

When Tasha left them, to go inside with the kids, Buck finally let go of the laughter he’d been holding in.

“What?” Eddie demanded, glaring at him.

“It’s just-” Buck shook his head. “You, with the emails and the worrying. How detailed were they?”

“Enough,” his boyfriend said with an imperious sniff.

“God, I remember when I went to my first sleepover. I think Danny nearly demanded to look through their medicine cabinet to make sure they didn’t have any naproxen.” And then Buck ended up making the kid’s mom call home and have Danny come get him after a nightmare, but that wasn’t something he was going to bring up here. “Actually, I think he did that the first time I spent the night at your’s, and I was fourteen, then.”

“He’s my kid, he’s been through a lot,” Eddie said, going around the truck. “It’s his first sleepover and I’m allowed to be worried.”

Buck just laughed at him again, slipping inside while Eddie pulled open the driver’s door. He stopped with it open, not yet getting in, when a guy in a knee brace glared at them and said,

“That’s a handicapped spot!”

Eddie shared a look with Buck and replied,

“I know.”

“Oh, you know?” The stranger let out a laugh. “That’s rich. I had knee surgery and had to park three rows in, but look at you. Must be nice to own the world.”

“I’m not doing this with you, man. My kid has CP, I just dropped him off.”

“Yeah, I’m sure,” the stranger scoffed. “Like those guys that put dummies in their passenger seats so they can use the carpool lane.”

Eddie slammed the door shut and started for the guy. Buck, sensing that this was going nowhere good, scrambled out of his seat to hurry over.

“Did you just insult my kid?” Eddie demanded.

“I ain’t insulting anybody, I’m calling you out,” the stranger said. “You got something to say about that?”

Buck saw Eddie’s fist clench, saw him start to raise it, and practically leaped across the last bit of space between them. He plastered himself into his boyfriend’s side, using his own weight to pin his fist down and wrapping an arm around him as he tried to think of some way to defuse this.

“It’s because we’re gay, isn’t it?” he blurted out.

Eddie stiffened in his grip, and the stranger went wide-eyed.

“Wh- What?”

“You see two guys dropping their kid off, and you just…” Buck shook his head. “Homophobia isn’t cool, man.”

“What? No, that’s not-”

“Right, sure, whatever.” Buck used his grip on Eddie to drag him to the truck. “C'mon, honey, let’s just leave the bigot here. He’s not worth it.”

The guy stood there, silent and bewildered, as Buck managed to coax Eddie into the truck. Eddie, too, remained silent the entire drive back to the house, and radiated waves of agitation. Buck assumed it was lingering anger at the guy for, obliquely or not, insulting Chris. At least until they got into the house and Buck was slipping off his shoes, and Eddie asked,

“Why did you do that?”

Buck stopped what he was doing to frown at him.

“Do what?”

“Do… that,” Eddie said. “Tell him we were gay.”

“Because you looked like you were about to punch the guy, Eds,” Buck said with a shrug. “I didn’t think I had time to explain details to some idiot.”

“No, I didn’t mean- And you called me honey. In front of him!”

Buck frowned.

“And?”

“I just wish you hadn’t done that!”

“Oh.” Buck’s heart dropped as understanding dawned. He shook his head, and while he laughed, it held no cheer. “Oh my god, it’s… It’s high school again.”

He was an idiot. He’d noticed things before, noticed Eddie’s discomfort when Buck did certain things in front of others, but Buck had stupidly thought that after May, after Eddie’s shield ceremony, that they were past this. Sure, they hadn’t gone on that date, and nothing had really changed with what they did except that they were having sex again, but…

God, it really was high school again.

“So, what? Would you prefer that I told him we were just two bros?” Buck demanded. “Should I start tagging “no homo” to everything we do outside of a closet?”

“No, that isn’t-” Eddie was staring at him wide-eyed now. “That’s not…”

“I told you, Eddie. I told you that I wasn’t going back to that. I told you I wasn’t going to stuff myself back into the closet because you were afraid of coming out.”

“I’m not- I kissed you!” Eddie argued. “In front of everyone!”

“And then nothing happened!”

“Nothing? My dad-”

“Is an asshole!” Buck snapped. “A homophobic asshole! And I get it, okay? You grew up with him telling you it was wrong, we both remember what happened to Timothy. But we aren’t in high school anymore, Eddie! You don’t even live in the same state as your parents! Yeah, you kissed me in front of everyone, but now I can’t even call you honey in front of some jackass without you throwing a fit?”

Eddie opened his mouth, clearly about to start shouting back. But then he stopped just before the words left him and dropped his head. He went to the couch, taking a seat and burying his face in his hands.

“I’m sorry,” he said, the words muffled. “I’m sorry, I’m sorry…”

“I love you,” Buck told him, moving to kneel in front of him. “But I can’t go back to that, Eddie. I won’t. I told you that if we were going to do this, that it couldn’t be like how it was in high school again.”

“I remember.”

“There was this guy in Key West. His name was Scott.” He placed his pals over Eddie’s knees and squeezed them softly. “He’d hold my hand in public, kiss me in front of everyone, his friends and strangers. It didn’t matter where we were, he didn’t care who saw us, he didn’t care that everyone knew we were together. It was wonderful, and I felt so free, and I want to do that with you, Eds. But…”

“I’m sorry,” Eddie said again, dropping his hands. “I got… Comfortable, I guess. I don’t know. I’ll- I’ll try to do better.”

“You better,” Buck told him. “Because I was serious. If you can’t do this, do us, without hiding it, then…”

“Okay.” Eddie said the word like a sigh, leaning forward to rest his forehead on Buck’s shoulder. “Okay. I’ll do better, I’ll make it up to you, I promise.”

Buck let out a sigh of his own and brought up his arms to wrap around his boyfriend.

“You can start making it up to me now,” he said. “Because we had plans for tonight, Eds.”

Eddie’s laughter was loud in Buck’s ear.

Daniel was in the middle of trying to decide what to order for dinner - he was leaning towards a pizza, because he could lure Austin into coming over if he chose the right toppings - when there was knocking at the door. Curious, he tucked his phone into his pocket and went to see who it was. A peek through the peephole had him even more curious.

“Hi, Dan,” an upset-looking Michael Grant said when he opened the door.

“Hi.” Daniel frowned at him, and wondered if he should have the bat at the ready. “Are you here to talk to Buck?”

The other man let out a sigh.

“No. Can I come in?”

Daniel thought about it for a moment, before opening the door wider and stepping aside.

“I have a bat,” he made sure to mention. “Y’know, if you’re here about Bobby.”

“That’s not why I’m here actually,” Michael said, coming inside and looking around. “I just needed a friend to vent to, I guess.”

“A friend?” Daniel barely knew the guy. “Pretty sure I’m persona non grata at the Grant-Nash household, right now.”

Michael let out a boisterous laugh.

“Oh yeah,” he agreed. “‘Thena is pissed at you.”

Daniel shrugged, utterly unbothered by that fact. He wasn’t sorry for what he said, and he wasn’t going to apologize.

“I’ll live,” he told him. “So, not to be rude, why did you come over?”

“Because it’s been mentioned, once or twice, that you aren’t exactly a fan of my ex-wife’s profession. Though, you like her well enough.”

“She used her badge to make her daughter’s bully miserable,” Daniel said. “Which was something I could get behind. She’s still a cop, though.”

“Yeah.”

The man’s tone had Daniel interested, now. There was definitely something eating at Michael, and for some reason it had him coming here of all places. So Daniel went to the fridge and grabbed a couple beers, along with the bottle opener from the drawer.

“Okay, what’s up?” he asked, popping the tops off the bottles and sitting down at the table. 

“They pointed a gun at my son.”

“Oh.” Daniel looked at the bottle in his hand. “Yeah, I think we need something stronger than this, but we don’t have any here.”

He pulled out his phone as Michael laughed, asking Austin what he wanted on his pizza and if he wouldn’t mind hitting the liquor store on the way over. He got back a judgmental sounding “why?” followed by “the usual.”

“So what happened?” Daniel asked, pulling up the app for the pizza place they used.

“I got pulled over,” Michael answered, grabbing the drink he was offered. “I don’t even know why. I mean, they said it was because my tags were expired, but I don’t know. And it just went from bad to worse. The guy slammed me into my own car, in front of my kids, because I didn’t do what he said fast enough. May got upset, started recording it on her phone, and then the other one pointed a gun at Harry in the backseat.”

“What did Athena say?”  Daniel asked as he placed the order and then set down his phone.

Michael’s face morphed into a scowl.

“It was just a bad stop, they happen, there’ll be an investigation.”

“Cops investigating cops for being cops.” Daniel snorted. “And her excuses?”

“They were afraid,” Michael answered. “They overreacted. I shouldn’t have argued with them.”

“Arguing with a cop is a fast way to get shot,” he couldn’t help but point out. Michael glared at him a little. “They’re cops, they were on a power trip. They were the same in El Paso. There was this one, he was a real pain in the ass.”

Literally. Daniel was thankful Buck wasn’t there, because he might’ve realized how much of that wasn’t a joke.

“See, this is why I came over,” Michael said. “You get it! Not in the same way-”

“I am very white.”

“But I just wanted someone I could vent to without them making excuses for why that asshole pointed a gun at my son!” the man said, slamming his bottle onto the table. “And now I have to sit Harry down and explain to him that actually, we’ve been lying to him this entire time and the police aren’t his friends. Because he’s black.”

“Can’t help you there, man,” Daniel told him. “I’ve never had to have that talk with Buck. We’re from Pennsylvania, the only way we could get any whiter was if we were from, I don’t know. Minnesota.” That made Michael laugh. “But I know he saw how Eddie was treated, whenever they were coming back from Juarez.”

The other man nodded, and then gave Daniel a considering look.

“Why do you get it?” he asked.

“Reasons,” Daniel answered with a grin he knew looked like his brother’s. “There’s pizza on the way, and Austin’s hitting a liquor store on his way over. If you want to stick around and bitch about cops some more.”

“Sure, why not?” Michael shrugged. “Hey, where is Buck, anyway?”

“Eddie’s kid is at a sleepover.”

“Ah. So the two of them are…”

“Yes. I’m just thankful there’s no risk of me walking in on them on my couch. Again.”

Buck wasn’t really sure what he expected when Bobby finally reached out to him, but it wasn’t for it to come in the form of an invitation to a session at some rage room. Apparently, it was a team building exercise, which was… Well, the fact that Buck was still - or perhaps once again - part of the team was heartening.

He was going to drive Eddie there, but Eddie came to the apartment first. This whole thing was rather last minute and it was easier to have Danny watch Chris than try to get someone’s schedule rearranged for it.

“Alright, kiddo,” Eddie said, sitting Chris down on the couch. “You behave for Danny and Austin. Got it?”

Austin had gotten an invite, too. He’d just opted to turn it down.

“Okay, Dad,” Chris said, nodding and wiggling where he sat.

“Tell me what Nash says,” Danny said, hovering while Buck finished getting ready to head out. “And if he’s an asshole about anything, it’s a rage room. There’ll be plenty of things to smash him with.”

“Dan.” Austin was rolling his eyes, Buck could tell without even glancing in the direction the man was sitting.

“Austin,” Danny shot back, in the exact same tone.

“I’m not gonna hit my captain,” Buck told him, rolling his own eyes.

“Hey, Danny?” Eddie asked, coming over. “You’re off on Halloween, right?”

“Yeah,” Danny answered, nodding slowly. “Why?”

“Well, I work that day-”

“I know, so does Austin.”

“Yeah, right. Um. Do you mind taking Chris trick-or-treating? I managed to get the night off last year to take him, and Carla offered to do it this year, but…”

“Yeah, sure.” Danny shrugged. “It’ll be just like old times, except maybe Chris won’t sprint down the street from me.”

“It’s not my fault you always went too slow!” Buck protested. “If I didn’t get there fast enough, they’d run out of the good candy!”

“Yeah, yeah.”

Eddie gave him directions for where to go and how to get there, because apparently they’d been called to the rage room to rescue someone from a barrel. Which, honestly, Buck figured was a mark against going to the place, but whatever. He wasn’t the one paying for it, and if anything happened, there’d be at least three medics on site.

When they got to the place, Buck found an empty spot on the far end of the parking lot. Bobby’s truck was sitting right near the entrance, and if things did get awkward, he figured the distance would be good. Buck and Eddie hopped out and started inside. Or rather, Buck did; Eddie suddenly stopped.

“Eds?” Buck frowned at him. “What’s up?”

But Eddie didn’t really answer. Instead, he took a deep breath and reached out to take Buck’s hand, looking dead ahead the entire time. Buck smiled and gave him a squeeze, but he didn’t say anything as he led the way.

Bobby was just inside, talking to Michael.

“Look who it is,” Michael said, grinning at them. “Did you two enjoy your childless evening?”

How did he even know about that?

“We did,” Eddie answered, which made the other man laugh.

“Glad to hear it,” Michael said. “C’mon, we’ve gotta suit up before they’ll let us smash anything. And I think these two need to talk.”

Eddie was clearly hesitant, but he followed Michael at Buck’s urging. Which left Buck and Bobby standing there in awkward silence for a couple beats.

“Look, Bobby, I- I’m sorry for Danny…”

“It’s-” Bobby cut himself off with a sigh. “You don’t have to apologize for him.”

“Still, though.” Buck reached up to rub at his neck. “And- And thanks for inviting me.”

“You’re getting a call from the chief tomorrow,” Bobby said. Buck felt his breath catch. “You’re being reinstated to active duty. Too much of an opportunity to end up with legal headaches, apparently.”

“That’s awesome,” Buck breathed out, grinning widely.

“Maybe. They gave me the option to transfer you.” Just as Danny had said they might. “But I told them no. You’re coming back to the One Eighteen, where I can keep an eye on you.”

That statement was almost enough to dull the edges of Buck’s joy and excitement. Almost. As it was, when the captain let him go - with a reminder of “my house, my rules” - Buck was practically skipping through life. He put on the protective gear he was made to wear, even more important for him than the others, and rushed to where everyone else was, a laughing Eddie following behind.

“Guys!” He practically crashed into Chimney and Hen, the latter looking rather down about something and the former holding a bunch of tiny liquor bottles. “I’m coming back!”

The pair cheered, though Hen’s was subdued.

“Lena, this is Buck,” Eddie said, introducing him to the woman that was with Hen and Chimney. Buck recognized her, vaguely, from when he visited the station. She was the person Bobby claimed wasn’t his replacement.

“Your partner, right?”

“My, uh. My boyfriend,” Eddie corrected.
