“Partner works, too,” Buck said, wrapping his arm around Eddie’s waist. He felt his boyfriend stiffen, and then almost purposefully relax. Baby steps. “C’mon, let’s go smash things.”

Chapter End Notes

I was NOT going to let the ACAB plotline pass by without letting Daniel at it. Nu-uh.

Chapter 14

“A house full of teenagers alone on Halloween,” Bobby said, moving a folding chair so it was centered more with the screen. “That doesn’t feel like a bad idea to you?”

It felt like the setting for a horror movie, in Athena’s honest opinion. The backyard was set up like a home theater: a giant screen, rows of fold-out chairs, a snack table decorated for the holiday. The two of them were working all day tomorrow, hence why they were decorating it tonight, and May was taking advantage of the house being free of adults to have friends over. And yes, maybe Athena was a bit anxious about it, but she trusted her daughter.

Though, maybe she could ask if Michael wouldn’t mind making a surprise drop-in. Or maybe Daniel. She might not be particularly happy with the man right now, but she knew she could still trust him with her kids. Hell, the fact he was willing to do something like ambush Bobby at the station to verbally attack the man for Buck’s sake had been why she was willing to trust him with her kids in the first place.

(It didn’t mean Athena wasn’t sorely tempted to shoot Daniel when she heard the kinds of things he was saying to her husband, though.)

“It’s eight responsible young adults just watching scary movies and ordering pizza,” Athena rebutted, adjusting where a fake candelabra stood between empty snack bowls and a jack-o’-lantern. “It’ll be fine.

“I would just feel better if one of us was here for it,” Bobby said.

Athena let out a scoff as she went to help with the chairs

“Doubt that.” She caught the look Bobby shot her, and returned it with one of her own. “What? You think I haven’t noticed? Nights spent not sleeping, and seven different kinds of baked goods in the kitchen.”

“I’m just looking for something to do at three in the morning.”

“You’re worried about tomorrow,” Athena said, cutting through the bullshit. “Buck’s first day back.”

“The kid did nearly choke to death on his own blood right over there,” Bobby pointed out, jerking his chin in that direction. “Because he pushed himself too hard, too fast. He still is.”

“You still don’t think he’s ready.”

It wasn’t a question. Athena knew that her husband continued to harbor doubts about Buck’s readiness to return to full and active duty as a firefighter. She also knew the only reason he wasn’t raising more of a fuss about it was the fear that Buck really would go elsewhere, and then Bobby wouldn’t be able to keep an eye on him.

“He doesn’t think things through,” Bobby said. “He acts, reacts, and that leaves the rest of us to have to deal with the fallout.”

Athena snorted softly, barely more than a puff of air.

“That seems to be a Buckley trait,” she noted, and then eyed Bobby. “Not just a Buckley trait. You’ve been reacting and leaving others to deal with the fallout, too.”

“Athena-”

“What?” She went to a pile of folded up blankets and throws, and she started handing them to him so he could put one on each chair. Just in case it got chilly. “May and Harry get hurt, I react. Buck gets hurt, you react.”

“Buck’s not my kid.”

Oh, Daniel made that perfectly clear, Athena knew. And yet…

“That’s not stopping you from treating him like he is,” she pointed out. The words made Bobby scowl. “Look, you are allowed to be upset with what he’s doing, and worried about the decisions he’s making. But you care about the kid, regardless. Don’t let the other stuff make you lose sight of that.”

Bobby sighed and then smiled.

“Yeah, alright.”

Buck hummed and bounced in place as he checked through his work bag for the umpteenth time, making sure everything he needed was tucked inside. He’d been counting down the days ever since he was given an actual date by the chief for when he’d start back, no doubt annoying literally everybody in his life and not caring a whit. Hell, not even accidentally slicing his thumb while helping Chris carve a pumpkin and ruining a towel had been enough to sour his good mood, however much it had stung.

Honestly, he thought - and Chris agreed - that the blood splatter just added to the ambience.

“Y’know,” Danny said. “If you keep checking to make sure you haven’t forgotten anything, you’ll end up forgetting to go to work.”

Buck raised his head to stick his tongue at his brother only to freeze mid-motion. Danny was leaning against the open door to Buck’s room, coffee mug in hand. It was the one Buck had bought him one Christmas, several years ago; it had a cartoon fireman’s hat over the words “World’s Okayest Fireman.” That wasn’t what caught his attention, though.

“What’s that?” he asked, forgetting his bag and its contents in favor of stepping over discarded clothes as he made his way over..

“What’s what?” Danny asked in return, frowning at him.

“This.” Buck poked at the large bruise sticking out from beneath Danny’s shirt sleeve. “Did you get hit on a call yesterday?”

“No?” Danny pulled up his sleeve and twisted his arm a bit to look at the bruise better. It was a dark bluish-purple, stark against his pale skin. “Huh.”

“You don’t remember getting this at all?” Buck was the one on the blood thinners, he was the one who was supposed to be getting all the random bruises of unknown origins. And he definitely was, along with ones of known origins.

(At least Eddie was keeping it below the collar, now.)

“Not a bit.” Danny frowned at the mysterious bruise, before shrugging it off and letting the sleeve fall back down. “Whatever.”

Buck poked at the bruise again and then danced away when his brother smacked at him for it.

“You ready for tonight?” he asked, going back to his bag to zip it up. He shot Danny a grin. “Got your costume all laid out?”

Chris had been bummed, initially, when he was told that Eddie wouldn’t be able to take him trick-or-treating again this year. That had cleared up quickly once he was informed that Danny would be doing it, instead. And then he somehow - there was no evidence of Buck’s wrongdoing here, no one could prove anything - got it into his head that if Danny was taking him trick-or-treating, then Danny needed to be in a costume, too. Even better, a matching costume.

There’d been a feeble attempt by Danny to argue against it, but he had always been a soft touch. Especially once the puppy dog eyes came out.

“Mine and his.” Danny’s tone was mild, the man utterly unbothered by Buck’s attempt at teasing.

Halloween - today - fell on a Thursday this year. Buck wasn’t certain if Eddie simply hadn’t thought about it when he initially asked Danny or what, but there’d been a general game plan formed since then. Buck brought over Chris’ costume along with what he’d need for spending the night, Carla was going to pick the kid up from school and drop him off at the apartment, and then Danny was going to drop him off at school the next morning.

Buck couldn’t help but note that there hadn’t been any emails sent to Danny, detailing how to properly care for Eddie’s precious boy. In fact, Eddie was practically blasé about the entire thing.

“I should get going,” Buck said, checking the time on his watch. “I don’t want to be late for my first day back.”

“That would be embarrassing,” Danny agreed, stepping out of the way to allow Buck past. He followed him to the door, stopping him just before he could leave. “Tell me if Nash gives you a hard time. Or if he’s just an ass to you in general.”

“Danny-”

“I’m serious, Buck.”

“What, so you can swing by and yell at him again?” Buck rolled his eyes and yanked the door open. “It’ll be fine.”

“Right.” His brother did not agree, that much was clear. “Austin’ll tell me, anyways.”

Buck huffed and rolled his eyes and stomped out the door.

The drive to the station was comfortably familiar, and he bounced in his seat while he sang along with the radio. He was running a little late, having spent too much time making sure he had everything, but he was still arriving before his shift started. He pulled into the parking lot and an empty spot not far from Eddie’s truck and he leaped out of the jeep, grabbing his bag and bounding to the front entrance where Eddie, already in uniform, was stood waiting.

“Hi!” Buck chirped, springing forward and pecking a kiss on his lips.

“Excited?” Eddie guessed, laughing at him.

“Of course! It’s my first day back,” he said. “Plus, I wanted to kiss my boyfriend before we’re both on the clock.”

Eddie laughed and ducked his head, and then he leaned in for another kiss. This one was deeper and lasted longer, and Buck was reluctant to let him pull away when it was over. Afterwards, Eddie took his hand and led him inside, which was decorated for the holiday with streamers of bats, actual bales of hay, and pumpkins. Buck happily greeted and was greeted by everyone that they passed. They only let go when they made it to the locker room and Buck needed both hands free to change out of his civvies.

“I’m not sure this should be allowed.” Hen’s voice had Buck turning slightly on the bench he was sitting on, doing up the laces of his boots, to face her as she joined them in the locker room. She was holding a small white box. “The two of you changing in front of each other here, I mean. I don’t think any of us want to see that.”

“Haha.” Eddie, leaning against his locker, rolled his eyes. “We can control ourselves.”

“We did last year,” Buck pointed out.

“Welcome back,” Hen said, taking a seat on the bench next to him. “We debated having another party for you, with a proper cake and banner, but figured we didn’t want to jinx anything. You know, since you nearly died at your last one.”

“Yeah, I’m not sure how well Bobby would be able to handle that,” Buck agreed.

“I got you a cupcake anyway,” Hen said, handing him the box. “It’s red velvet, bleeds when you cut it.”

Buck chuckled and wrapped an arm around her for a side hug.

“I missed you guys,” he told her. “How’s the, uh, baby-making going? You and Karen knocked up yet?”

“Buck,” Eddie admonished, shaking his head. Buck just grinned at him.

“It’s… A long process, more twists and turns than you might expect,” Hen told him, quickly returning the side hug before getting up. “It’s good to have you back.”

“It is good to be back,” Buck declared, getting up as well.

The three of them filed out of the locker room, freezing when there was the sound of someone clearing their voice. Austin was standing there, phone out and looking at Buck expectantly.

“Smile,” he instructed, not even waiting for it to be followed before taking a picture of Buck. “There, that should hold your brother off for a little while, at least.”

“God knows we don’t want him storming in here again,” Hen muttered. Buck could only agree.

“Welcome back, Firefighter Buckley,” Chimney said, joining the group. Bobby trailed behind him, tablet in hand.

“Thanks, Chim.”

“Now that we’ve got that out of the way,” Bobby said, with not even a greeting in Buck’s direction. “Morning briefing-”

The alarm sounded, cutting him off. Everyone started for the trucks, Buck included, only to be halted by Bobby with,

“You’re the man behind today.”

“Uh, what? I’m not going with you guys?”

“My house, my rules, remember?” Bobby tossed at him as he left him behind. “You stay put.”

Buck was left to glare as the trucks left the station without him.

Daniel was flipping through Netflix, trying to find a good kid-friendly horror movie, when someone started knocking at the door. He got to his feet and went over to answer it, and was greeted by a brightly grinning Chris and a softly laughing Carla.

“Happy Halloween,” Daniel said, stepping aside to let them in.

“Happy Halloween!” Chris replied, quite loudly, his crutches tapping along the floor as he entered.

“I’ve been told that he hasn’t gotten into any candy yet,” Carla informed him, holding out Chris’ backpack for him to take. “But he must’ve, because he’s been a bouncy thing since I got him.”

“He’s got nothing on Buck,” Daniel told her, taking the bag. “There were times when I was stuck in a car with him and I might’ve killed the squirt if I didn’t love him so much. And that was without any sugar added.”

Carla laughed.

“He has homework, but nothing much-”

“Who gives homework to a kid on Halloween?” Daniel asked with a sneer.

“A teacher who probably doesn’t actually expect it to get done,” Carla answered with a shrug. “Where are you two going for trick-or-treating? Is there a lot of that happening in this building?”

“Athena asked if I’d check in on May at some point, apparently she’s having friends over for a movie marathon,” Daniel said. “So I’m thinking we’ll hit up that neighborhood. It seemed fancy enough to me, they probably have the good candy. And then afterwards go swing by the station, surprise Buck and them.”

Make sure, in person, that Nash was properly toeing the line.

“They’ll probably all like that,” said the woman who clearly had not heard about what Daniel had done the last time he swung by station 118. He wasn’t going to inform her, either. “Buck brought over everything Chris needs for tonight and tomorrow?”

“Yes, ma’am.”

“Then have fun you two!” she said, raising her voice a little for Chris to hear her. “And don’t forget to do your stretches in the morning!”

“Okay, Carla!” Chris called back, from where he’d already claimed a seat on the couch.

Daniel closed the door behind Carla, and then went to where Chris was sitting. He knelt down in front of him, so that they were close to eye level.

“Alright, kiddo, you’re ready for a fun Halloween?”

“Yeah!” The kid nodded like a bobble-headed, the straps on his glasses managing to keep anything from flying away.

“Then let’s go over the rules first,” Daniel said, smirking at the groan Chris let out. He was very expressive with it, dropping his shoulders and throwing his head back and everything. “C”mon, I’m sure your dad did it with you last year. What was his first one?”

“Stay where he could see me,” Chris dutifully recited. “Don’t run off. Don’t be greedy. Say thank you.”

“All very good rules.” He nodded approvingly. “Okay, so, we have time until we start looting and pillaging. I was thinking we find things to watch in our costumes until it’s time to go. That sound cool to you?”

“Yeah!” Chris slid off the couch, grabbing his crutches where they were leaning against its side.

“Do you need help getting it on?” Daniel offered.

“No, I’ve got it.”

“Alright.” He stood, gave the kid a pat on the head. “C’mon, it’s hanging up in here….”

Normally, a table filled with treats would have Buck salivating. At the moment, though, he was resisting the temptation to just flip it over and scream. It was very childish of him, he knew that, but his frustrations today were simply mounting. Because not only had he been sidelined all day, not only was he going to be stuck handing out treats while his friends actually did their jobs, but Bobby was explaining everything to him like he was an idiot.

“The teal bucket is for kids with food allergies,” Bobby was saying, pointing at the bucket in question. It had been there last year, too, and they used it for the exact same thing. And the year before that. “Everything in it is nut-free, dairy-free, egg-free, soy-free, and gluten-free.”

“And these?” Buck asked, holding up an unpackaged smoke detector. “Kids allergic to fun?”

“Those are for the parents,” Bobby said, plucking it out of his hand and putting it back in the rows of its brethren. “There should also be pamphlets about basic fire safety in the closet.”

“So how long,” Buck said, “are you going to keep punishing me for my decisions?”

“You know, some day you’re gonna figure out when to stop pushing and learn some patience,” the captain told him. “I hope we’re both alive to see it.”

“Okay, that’s funny,” Buck couldn’t help but blurt out when Bobby turned to leave. “‘Cause, being patient and not pushing almost had me ending up back in the hospital.”

Bobby didn’t say anything as he walked away, but Buck wasn’t left alone for long.

“You know,” Chimney said, coming over. “Being stuck here isn’t actually the end of the world, it just feels like it. Trust me, I know.”

Buck’s face must’ve been betraying his doubt, because the older man laughed.

“I do,” he insisted. “My first few months here, the only time I saw the outside of this building was at the start and the end of my shift. On my way in, on my way out.”

“How’d you cope with it?” Buck asked him.

“I cleaned every inch of this place. Learned where everything was, how all the equipment worked. Set a new world record for getting my turnout gear,” Chimney answered. “And then eventually, someone took pity on me and they let me outside. So basically, um, suck it up and be patient. Just like Bobby said.”

But Buck was feeling a little bit bratty, and a lot annoyed, so he bluntly told the man,

“That sounds like awful advice.”

After all, as he’d just told Bobby, that kind of thinking had him sticking with a job that was detrimental to his health. Mental and physical. He sucked it up, he was patient, he trusted his captain. It hadn’t gotten him anywhere.  At least the path he ended up taking got him back here, with everyone else, even if Bobby was keeping him back from calls at the moment. Baby steps and all.

“I’m gonna go find those pamphlets,” Buck declared, and started off in the direction of the closet.

“Have you talked to Maddie today?” Chimney asked, before he could get too far.

“No, I’m pretty sure she’s working,” Buck replied with a frown. The other man sounded kinda anxious. “Why?”

“Nothing,” was the obvious lie. “Just wondering.”

Push it or let it go? Push it or… He was gonna let it go, a decision helped by the fact that he spotted Eddie coming over from the same direction Buck needed to go.

“You doing okay?” Eddie asked when Buck reached him.

“Could be doing better,” he answered with a grin that felt more than a bit plastic. “Cap has me on smoke detector duty tonight.”

Eddie let out a sigh.

“I’m sorry,” he said. “Do you want me to say something to him?”

“Would it help?” Buck asked in return. “He didn’t want me to come back, and he’s annoyed that I am. I just hope Austin hasn’t said anything to Danny, the last thing we need is a repeat.”

Eddie grimaced because unlike Buck, he’d actually been there to witness Danny tearing into Bobby.

“Then what can I do?”

Buck thought for a moment. This time, his smile was very real.

“There are fire safety pamphlets in the closet that I need to hand out,” he said, bumping his shoulder against the other man’s. “Wanna help me find them?”

“Lead the way.”

(Buck figured they would've found the pamhplets sooner, had they not gotten a bit... Sidetracked.)

Chapter 15

Chapter Notes

cw for canon typical blood

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Buck was not having a particularly enjoyable first shift back. Sure, being stuck at the station was loads better than being stuck at a desk, because at least he was able to stand and walk around and even hit the gym equipment. But as a fire marshal, he wasn’t stuck repeatedly watching the trucks leave without him as everyone else went to do the job he fought so desperately to get back to.

It wasn’t the first time Buck had been the man behind, but unless it was being used as a punishment - like when Buck was a probie and chopped that snake’s head off - it was normally a role that rotated among those of them who weren’t necessarily needed for the emergency they were responding to. That meant the paramedics were rarely the man behind, and Austin never was. For the rest of them, though, they’d miss a call and then be back on the trucks for the next one.

Not this time. Buck had been left behind at the station for every call today. Because it was a punishment, that much was very clear, regardless of what Bobby claimed.

It did not help that manning the candy table was reminding Buck of the worst of the customer service jobs he’d done in the past. Most of the kids were fine and if he was in a better mood he would probably enjoy trying to make them laugh. However, only most of the kids were fine; some were little shits.

“Woah, woah!” He yanked the bucket of candy out of the reach of a kid dressed like an old-fashioned prisoner, complete with fake chains. “Sorry, buddy, two piece limit. Those are the rules.”

“That’s a stupid rule,” the snot-nosed brat shot back.

“Not the world’s greatest defense strategy. Explains the get-up.”

“What are you supposed to be, anyway?” the kid demanded.

“I’m a firefighter,” Buck answered, gesturing down at his uniform.

“So how come you’re not out fighting fires?” The kid asked. 

Buck scowled at him and was about to explain that, actually, they only sometimes dealt with any actual fires anymore because the push on fire safety back in the day actually worked. But before he got his chance, the sound of a horn and the engine’s brakes bringing it to a stop right outside the station bay doors cut him off.

“Look!” The kid sneered at Buck. “The real firefighters are here.”

“He is a real firefighter.” 

Danny seemed to materialize out of nowhere, which was impressive considering how tall he was. He was dressed in a cheap butler costume; beside him, Chris was dressed up like Batman, his glasses resting neatly over his mask. Danny glanced at Buck and then at the kid dressed like a prisoner, and then he bent down to whisper something into the kid’s ear.

The kid’s eyes went wide and he burst into tears, and he ran off with a cry of “Mommy!”

“What did you say to him?” Buck demanded, rounding the table and kneeling to give Chris a hug.

“Don’t worry about it,” Danny told him.

Buck gave him a squinty-eyed glare, but he decided to let it go. That kid had been a brat, after all.

“Got a good haul, Batman?” he asked Chris, instead.

“Yeah!”

The bay doors slid open, allowing the others that were actually being firefighters to come inside. Austin was having to park the truck in front of the station, because the bay was currently taken over by little monsters and their parents, along with an antique fire truck for them to clamber over and take pictures on that Buck had no clue about the origins of, it had just sorta appeared that day. When the others came in, wearing their turnouts, they all froze momentarily at the sight of Danny standing there.

Danny gave a smile and waved at a blank-faced Bobby.

“Do not,” Buck warned him as he stood back up.

“Nice work, Buckley,” Bobby called over. “Keep it up.”

“Buckley?” Danny asked under his breath.

“Do not,” Buck repeated, and then smiled at Eddie when he came over. “Hey.”

“Hey.” Eddie bent over to give Chris a hug. “Have you gotten your candy from Buck yet?”

“No.” Chris tilted his head back to look up at Danny. “Alfred?”

Danny let out a sigh and lifted up the candy bag dangling from his hand, holding it open. With a completely straight face and in what might have been the world’s worst impression of a British accent, he said,

“Of course, Master Bruce.”

“Trick-or-treat!” Chris dutifully exclaimed.

Buck plucked a couple pieces of candy from the table to toss into the bag his brother was holding. Then, with a glance around to make sure none of the other kids were watching, threw in a couple more.

“Don’t tell anyone,” Buck said, and Chris giggled and nodded. Buck reached into his pocket, pulling out a handful of fun sized Skittle bags which he handed to Danny. “To keep you from rifling through the kid’s bag.”

“I would never,” Danny denied, tucking his Skittles away.

“Liar.”

Buck had lost far too much of his candy to Danny as “tax” to ever trust his brother on that.

“Where did you go tonight?” Eddie asked, running his hand through Chris’ hair. The Batman mask was a plastic thing that only covered part of his face. “My neighborhood, or elsewhere?”

“Athena wanted me to check on May and her friends,” Danny told him. “So we hit up her neighborhood. One of the houses was giving out full-sized candy bars.”

As a unit, Buck and Eddie leaned forward to peer into Chris’ bag of candy, eyebrows rising at the sight of a king sized Hershey bar sticking up amongst everything.

“I’ll remember that for next year,” Eddie remarked.

“How’s it been here?” Danny asked, dropping the bag back down to his side. “Gone on any fun calls today?”

Austin, who’d been walking over to join them after depositing his turnouts, froze at the question. Danny’s back was to him, so he didn’t see the man take a breath and let it out, head dropping down a bit before he continued over.

“Well.” Buck couldn’t help the way his eyes darted in the direction he knew Bobby had gone off in. “It- It’s been-”

Beside him, Eddie let out a sigh and crossed his arms.

“Bobby hasn’t been letting him out on calls today,” he tattled. “We’re pretty sure he’s punishing him.”

“He’s what?” Danny demanded, voice dropping dangerously. “Austin was supposed to-”

“I didn’t want you storming in here and, I don’t know, actually-” Austin glanced down at Christopher, who was looking between them curiously. “I didn’t want you doing anything stupid.”

Danny glared; Austin met his gaze and didn’t flinch.

“Well, I’m here now,” Danny said. He shoved the bag of candy into Austin’s chest, who took it with a roll of his eyes. “I’ll go have another talk with Captain Nash and-”

“You will not.” Buck’s hand struck out before his brother could move, grabbing him by the arm. “Tia Alma and Isabel both said they want pictures of you and Chris. Go stand next to the truck.”

Danny glare turned on him for a moment, before his eyes darted down to Chris. He sighed and tugged his arm out of Buck’s grip.

“C’mon, kiddo,” he said, heading to the antique fire truck, its bed full of hay bales and pumpkins. “Your dad’ll help you onto the back.”

“Oh I will, will I?” Eddie grumbled, already bending down to sweep Chris up and carry him over.

“Send me a couple,” Austin told Buck. “Mom’ll like ‘em, probably.”

“You got it,” Buck agreed. He pulled out his phone as he waited for Danny and Chris to get into position. “Alright, on the count of three…”

The TV was showing the news when Bobby walked by, the anchor reporting on the call they’d come back from not too long ago. A pair of parents who kept their children chained up in the basement, all of them dehydrated and malnourished and sick, and then had the audacity to claim they were keeping them safe when they were caught. Hen was lounging on one of the couches, arm propped up over her head.

“You know,” she started, eyes fixed on the screen. Bobby stopped in his tracks to hear what she had to say. “Karen and I make jokes about locking Denny up. For his protection. But the truth is, protecting him like that, it wouldn’t be for him. It’d be for us. We’d be robbing him to give ourselves some peace of mind.”

Bobby took a seat on the coffee table with a sigh. Hen’s eyes finally moved from the news to him.

“That’s not very subtle,” he told her.

“It’s really late,” she said. “And I’m really tired.”

“You think I’m being too hard on him?”

Hen sat forward, dropping her arm as she faced him properly..

“I think Buck makes everything hard on Buck,” she said. “The boy has two settings: zero and shut up before I smack you.”

“I can see that he’s trying,” Bobby admitted to her. “It just still seems like he doesn’t get it, like this is all a game to him.”

Hen looked at him as if he was stupid, head tilted slightly and an eyebrow raised.

“I got him to talk about Texas a bit, after I figured out about Eddie,” she told him. “He’s watched Dan do this job for years, Bobby. He knew what he was getting himself into. If he were looking for a game, there’s a hundred different other things he could be out there doing right now. But he chose this job.”

“So I should let him do it.”

“Or let him go somewhere else that will.”

Those were the options that Daniel had given him, when the man came in and yelled at him. They weren’t good options then, they weren’t good options now. Because how the hell was Bobby supposed to trust that Buck wouldn’t go out there and do his usual risky stunts and get hurt again? And as long as Buck was here, and not at another station, Bobby could make sure and see for himself that he was safe.

Though, maybe Hen was right with her extremely unsubtle jab. Maybe he should stop robbing Buck for his own peace of mind.

The man in question was downstairs, talking and laughing with Eddie as they cleaned up from the day. Each of them was carrying a box filled with decorations, bouncing off each other’s shoulders as they walked. Bobby jogged down the steps, calling out “Buckley!” as he went.

Buck and Eddie paused where they were, and Bobby watched Eddie send the other a questioning look. Buck shrugged and, with a frown and a nod, Eddie continued on to put up the box he was carrying.

“Candy detail’s all wrapped up, Cap,” Buck reported when Bobby reached him, setting his box down on the pingpong table. “And I gave away all the smoke detectors.”

“Good, that’s… That’s good,” Bobby told him. “Listen, Buck, I’ve been thinking…”

He looked at Buck’s face, expression growing hopeful. He sucked in a breath, ready to say the words. Then he remembered, for the briefest of moments, the way Buck looked under the truck and as he choked on his own blood and as he collapsed at the VA hospital.

“Why don’t you go home?” was what left Bobby’s mouth, instead.

Buck’s face dropped like an ice shelf into the sea.

“Home?” The kid shook his head, like the words he was hearing weren’t making sense. “It- It’s the middle of my shift…”

“I don’t wanna overtax you when you’ve just gotten back.”

“I stood behind a table,” Buck argued. “And got bullied by children.”

“So you earned the time off,” Bobby said. He gave Buck a pat on the arm, ignoring the kicked puppy expression the kid was wearing. “Good work today.”

And then he walked away, leaving Buck there.

Sometimes, Buck wished he was more like his brother. Throughout his life, he’d watched Danny stand up for him, against their parents, against teachers, against the world. And Buck spent much of his time hiding behind him and letting him do it, grateful for him to do it, even. Danny was the type to face conflict head on, usually with that baseball bat in hand. Buck did everything he could to avoid it.

Eddie was upset on Buck’s behalf, when he told him that he was being sent home early. He’d even offered to go yell at Bobby for him. But Buck, always trying to avoid conflict, talked him down from it. He’d joked, even, about how this meant he’d be able to hit up the discount candy bins first thing tomorrow.

On the way to the apartment, Buck stopped at a Shell station for candy of his own and maybe something non-child appropriate, to wash down the bitterness he was tasting. He’d just parked and was getting out when he spotted a woman exiting the store. She walked oddly across the parking lot, limping almost.

The car she went to, sitting in front of a gas pump, had what must’ve been one of the most realistic Halloween decorations he’d ever seen. It probably wasn’t appropriate, and he wondered how many calls to 9-1-1 it had garnered from concerned bystanders. Buck appreciated the effort must’ve gone into it nonetheless.

He was about to head inside when, just as the lady started her car, the man’s foot jerked.

Buck’s first instinct, as the car began to drive away, was to chase after it as he shouted for the woman to stop. He was ignored, though, and he scrambled back to his jeep. The tires screeched as he peeled out, foot pressing on the gas. He called 9-1-1 as he went.

“Nine-One-One, what’s your emergency?” a male dispatcher asked.

“This is LAFD firefighter Evan Buckley,” he said, eyes fixed on his target. “I’m going south down Vermont following a gold, four-door sedan. There is a man embedded in the windshield.”

“Sorry, did you say in the windshield? We had a report about that yesterday, is that real?”

Is that real? Seriously? Well, it was Halloween, they’d probably gotten tons of reports like it.

“Yeah, it’s definitely real, and I think he’s alive,” Buck said, crossing over into the next lane. “I’m gonna approach the vehicle right now.”

He pressed down a bit more on the gas, bringing the jeep even with the driver. The passenger side window was already rolled down, and he leaned over a bit as he shouted at the woman in the car. From this angle, he could see what looked like a huge knot on her forehead.

“Ma’am!” he tried. “Ma’am, pull over!”

She didn’t even look his way. Which meant it was plan B. Which was a shame, because this jeep was practically brand new.

Buck sped up even more, overtaking the sedan, and then he cut it off. Thankfully the woman braked, coming to a stop as he did. He grabbed a pen light from the compartment between the seats and got out as the other woman did as well, looking irate.

“You are a horrible driver,” she complained, one of her eye surrounded by a day old bruise. “And you are right in my way.”

“Ma’am, I’m a firefighter-”

“Well your costume sucks.”

“Okay, I’m just gonna take a look…” Buck flashed the penlight in her eyes, not surprised at all when one of her pupils - the one on her injured side - remained fixed. “Did you hurt yourself?”

“What?” The woman sounded confused. She probably didn’t even remember whatever happened.  “What do you mean?”

The man in the windshield let out a pitiful sounding groan.

“You, uh, you stay right there,” Buck told the woman, tucking the light into his pants and climbing into the sedan. He felt around to check the guy out, even though that meant reaching through shards of plexiglass. “Sir? Sir, I’m LAFD. Help is on its way.”

It wasn’t the 118 that responded, for which Buck was both thankful and disappointed. He climbed out of the sedan when the 121 arrived, telling the paramedics what he knew of the current status of the patients and then getting out of their way. By that time, his hands and one of the sleeves of his shirt - which had fallen down from where they’d been rolled up - were covered in blood.

It was enough that one of the 121’s paramedics, when both the man and the woman were being loaded, stopped to ask him,

“Want us to check you out?”

“Oh, nah, it’s not mine,” Buck assured him, though the paramedic reached for his arm anyway, pushing the sleeve up a little. “I have another shirt in my-”

He stopped and stared at his forearm. That was an awfully long cut there, one he hadn’t even noticed. One that was still bleeding.

“Oh,” Buck breathed out.  How much of the blood he was covered in was from the guy in the windshield, and how much was his own? “You, uh. You should know that I’m on blood thinners.”

Daniel was sadly very used to being woken up in the middle of the night by his brother for various reasons. Because he’d had a nightmare, because he wanted to be picked up early from a sleepover, because he was stumbling home drunk and was even more incapable of existing at a reasonable volume. Daniel was not, however, used to being woken up by his brother calling him to say he was in the hospital.

He barely let Buck finish the sentence before demanding to know which hospital, and then hanging up to leap out of bed and get dressed. The presence of Chris, sleeping soundly on the couch with a mound of pillows, was only a minor obstacle. Daniel woke him up enough to tell him they were going to pick up Buck and then carried him out to his car.

The entire drive there, Daniel tried to calm himself down. He rationalized that if it had been as bad as he feared, then Buck wouldn’t have been the one who called him. The hospital would have, or Nash, or - far more likely - Eddie or Austin. But it had been Buck who called him, which meant Buck was fine.

Though, that still begged the question of what happened. When Daniel swung by the station with Chris, he’d been told that Nash was keeping him back from calls. Which meant that either he’d changed his mind and let him out, or that something had happened at the station.

After finding a spot to park at the hospital, Daniel picked Chris up again, doing his best not to wake him, and carried him inside. He rushed, as much as he could rush with an eight year old in his arms, up to the nurse’s station.

“I’m looking for a patient,” he told the nurse. “Evan Buckley?”

“Danny?”

Daniel turned and spotted Buck a few feet away. He went over, the nurse no longer important to him, to check on his baby brother. He looked okay, mostly, with the exception of one forearm being bandaged and the opposite hand having a large band-aid stuck to the side of it.

He also wasn’t in his uniform.

“What happened?” Daniel demanded to know, gently setting Chris down into a nearby chair. He slipped out of his jacket and laid it over the kid as a make-shift blanket, remembering all the times he’d done the exact same thing for Buck.

“Well maybe if you hadn’t hung up on me,” Buck started to say, only to cut himself off with a roll of his eyes at Daniel’s glare. “There was this lady at the gas station on the way home, she had some guy sticking out of her windshield. She drove off, but I managed to get her to pull over.”

“Okay.” Daniel gingerly grabbed Buck by the wrist, tugging at his arm for a better look. “And?”

“And I cut myself on the windshield when I checked on the guy. The paramedics sealed the wounds and they brought me here to be checked out just in case,” his brother said, not fighting the inspection at all. “I just finished giving the police my statement. Apparently she hit him a couple days ago, and hit her head pretty bad at the same time. The doctors found a brain bleed, probably why she was so confused.”

“What about the guy she hit?” Daniel asked, dropping the arm to check the other hand.

“He’s in surgery,” Buck answered, because of course he cared enough to find out. “Docs say he’s got a fair chance.”

“Because you were there,” Daniel told him, finally letting go now that he knew for sure everything was okay. “Why were you there? I thought it might’ve been because Nash finally got his head out of his a-” He glanced at Chris, still snoozing away. “Out of his butt, but you’re not in uniform.”

“Yeah, no, uh.” Buck reached up and rubbed at his neck. “Bobby sent me home early.”

“Why?”

His brother dropped his head and shrugged.

“He said he didn’t want to, uh. Over tax me when I’ve just gotten back.” Buck raised his head, looking far more upset than he had when Daniel first showed up. “This is how it’s going to be, isn’t it? He’ll keep me back at the station however much he can, he’ll send me home early. Everything he can to keep me tucked away and safe.”

He spat the word out like bile.

“I don’t know,” Daniel told him. “Probably.”

Daniel, as biased as he was, was not the best person to be asking anything when it came to Nash.

Buck swallowed, throat bobbing as he nodded. Once, twice.

“Okay,” he said. “Is, uh. Is that spot at the One Thirty-three still open?”

Daniel let out a sigh and reached up to squeeze Buck’s shoulder.

“Are you sure?” he asked him. “Eddie’s at the One Eighteen, your friends are there. And… And I can’t promise I won’t freak out on you if I actually see you do the things I know Austin doesn’t tell me about.”

“I trust you,” Buck said with a shrug. “You’ve never held me back before, except for like, twice, even when you really wanted to. It’s not like I’ll never see Eddie or Chimney or the others ever again, and… And I remember how miserable I was in Hershey, Danny, with Mom refusing to let me do anything because she wanted me to be safe. I- I can’t do that again.”

Daniel let out a sigh, hand moving from Buck’s shoulder to the back of his head as he pressed their foreheads together.

“Alright, squirt. I’ll talk to Mehta when I go in tomo- Well, this afternoon, I guess.”

“Thank you.”

Chapter End Notes

The most painful part of writing this chapter was that I oh-so-desperately wanted to slip in DCAU references and couldn't. Damn the Buckleys for being so pop culturally ignorant.
(And I guess I should probably add another tag, huh?)

Chapter 16

Chapter Notes

Nobody look at the chapter dates!
(Remember when this series was updated almost daily?)

The decision to transfer out of the 118 was, actually, a rather easy one to make. Sure, he would miss the people he worked with, but he was guaranteed to see Chimney and Austin and Eddie. He would have to make a point of keeping in touch with Hen, though. A better job at it than when he was out with first his leg and then the blood clots, but that shouldn’t be all that hard. But Bobby, well…

Buck wasn’t sure what he was going to do when it came to Bobby after this. Especially since the biggest reason that transferring was such an easy decision to make was because Buck simply didn’t trust Bobby any longer. Being able to trust the people that had your back, being able to trust your captain, was one of the most important aspects of being a firefighter. If Buck couldn’t trust Bobby, then he couldn’t stay there, simple as that.

The decision to transfer out was an easy one to make, but Buck still had to follow through with it and make it happen. As much as he might like to simply show up at the 133 on his next shift and pretend like he was meant to be there until everyone accepted it, he couldn’t actually do that. Or, well, he could, but it wouldn’t work.

Probably.

(Would it?)

There were several steps to be taken before a transfer could happen, one of the first being making sure there was somewhere for him to transfer to. Danny was taking care of that step for him, or at least he was supposed to be; he’d promised to talk to Mehta about whether or not that spot that had been offered to him before was still there. There’d probably be questions, considering Buck returned to the 118 and almost immediately decided to leave it. He just hoped he wouldn’t be causing more grief for anyone than he truly had to.

Buck was upset with Bobby, yeah, but that didn’t mean he wanted his choices and actions to potentially harm the captain’s career. Buck didn’t want to hurt Bobby, he just wanted to be able to do his job, and he wanted to be useful as he did it. He’d screwed up badly enough that he could longer do the one thing was born for, but he could at least keep being a firefighter.

Anyways, making sure he actually had a place to transfer to was the first step. Talking to Bobby, telling him what he was doing, that was something he couldn’t avoid, something he shouldn’t avoid, but it came later. But in between the two was another, equally important step. There were other people in his life, important people, that this would affect, after all.

Which meant Buck would need to talk to Eddie.

On the morning after Halloween, the first day of November, Buck went with Danny to drop Chris off at school. The kid was a bit grumpy about everything, he’d only mostly slept through Danny rushing out of the apartment with him in the middle of the night and to the hospital, but he was easily appeased with the promise of a looming weekend and his stash of candy waiting for him at home. Buck wasn’t sure how much Chris remembered, but he made sure to wear a long-sleeved shirt to cover up the cut on his arm all the same.

Afterwards, Buck and his brother hit up a store for the discount candy that was left over, making off like bandits. Sure, they were twenty-seven and thirty-four respectively, but everyone was a child at heart when it came to certain things. ‘Sides, it meant that Chris’ candy was safe from Danny’s thievery disguised as “taxation.”

Danny’s shift was set to begin in the afternoon, so the time before then was spent on the couch watching kid unfriendly horror movies and gorging themselves on the discount candy. Danny also forced Buck to let him take a look at the cuts on his arm and his hand. They had scabbed over finally, and normally that would be enough for either of them. But the last thing either of them wanted was for Buck to accidentally reopen either wound and start bleeding again. Safer, then, to put fresh bandages on that would hopefully lessen that chance. 

“Are you sure you still want me to talk to Mehta?” Danny asked him, right on the cusp of heading into work. “I was serious before, Buck. I can’t make any promises about…”

“I told you, I trust you,” Buck told him, shrugging. “‘Sides, it’s not like you’re the captain. You can yell all you want, I’ll just ignore you.”

Danny let out a sigh and shook his head.

“I feel like we’re all going to regret that Mehta ever made the offer,” he said as he opened the door and stepped out

“You know you love me!” Buck called after his brother.

The only response was a grunt before the door closed behind Danny. Buck chose to assume that said grunt translated into something like, “Yes, Buck, you are the best brother in the world and I absolutely adore you.” Though it could just as well have been a request to have spaghetti for dinner tomorrow night, the two grunts sounded alike.

With Danny gone, Buck didn’t have any excuse to dither in an empty apartment. He grabbed the bag of candy that Chris left behind that morning, along with some of the candy they bought and his own overnight bag, locked the apartment behind him, and went to his jeep, which they’d stopped to get on the way back from the hospital last night-slash-very early this morning.

Eddie was home when Buck pulled into the drive, but he still didn’t bother knocking. He simply used his key, stepping inside to find Chris sitting at the dining table with his homework. The boy looked up and let out a sound of joy that could’ve been mistaken as being directed at Buck himself, until Chris rushed to grab his candy out of Buck’s hands. Then Buck was forced to realize that, on a list of Chris’ priorities, he fell somewhere below individually packaged sweets.

“Thank you, Buck!” Chris called out, already on his way back to the dining table with his haul.

“He was complaining about leaving his candy at your’s the entire ride home,” Eddie said, having stepped out of the kitchen when he heard Buck come in. He was frowning and making no effort to hide the way he looked Buck up and down. Normally, Buck would be flattered by that, but he had the suspicion that- “He also mentioned something about Danny waking him up to go somewhere, last night? To pick you up?”

“Oh, uh. Yeah.” Buck took a moment to leave his shoes by Eddie and Chris', just inside the door, and cross the space to Eddie. He kissed him hello, glanced at Chris - whose attention was fully focused on his homework once more - and led his boyfriend into the kitchen. “I needed Danny to pick me up at the hospital.”

“At the- What?”

“Yeah, uh…” 

Buck tossed the bag of discount candy onto the kitchen table, which currently had a spread of take-out menus covering it, Eddie’s attempt at “making dinner.” Unfortunately, he used the hand that was cut to do this, which meant his boyfriend got a good look at the bandage on it. Much like Danny had at the hospital, Eddie immediately grabbed him by the wrist and pulled his hand closer for a better look at it.

“Buck, what happened?”

“Okay, so, I was on my way home, right?” he started, and waited for the other man to nod. “And I stopped at a gas station, where there was this car with a guy sticking out of the windshield.” Eddie’s eyebrows skyrocketed. “I thought it was a Halloween thing, y’know? Until I saw the guy’s leg move. Anyways, long story short, apparently the driver hit him a couple days ago but also hit her head, so she was going around with a brain bleed that no one caught. Luckily they’re both going to be okay.”

“Okay, but how did…?” Eddie let the question trail off as he dropped Buck’s hand, having apparently decided everything was in one piece.

“I wasn’t paying attention,” Buck admitted. He pulled up the sleeve covering his other arm, letting Eddie see the bandages there. “Cut myself on the windshield when I checked the guy. I’m okay, though.”

Eddie was shaking his head, looking increasingly unhappy.

“That shouldn’t have happened,” he said.

“Eds-”

“No, Buck. That’s the entire reason we wear our turnouts on those kinds of calls, to keep ourselves from being cut like this,” Eddie said. “If Bobby wasn’t so busy being a dumbass, you wouldn’t have gotten hurt!”

“I could help them both because I was there, though.”

“Buck.” Eddie sighed. “She was driving around with someone in her windshield. Someone would have called it in.”

“It did get called in,” Buck pointed out. “They just thought it was fake or something.”

That definitely sounded like something he should raise with someone, now that he thought about it. The fact that someone had called it in the day before and, evidently, no one had looked into it. Or Buck didn’t think they had, because it was the sort of thing that stuck out on the road.

“Still feel like I need to yell at Bobby for this,” Eddie said, going over to poke through the candy Buck had brought. “If we rub it in his face, maybe he’ll stop being an idiot.”

“Yeah, about that.” Buck let his shirt sleeve drop back down, and he could feel the gauze beneath the fabric when he anxiously rubbed at it. “Uh, so- so, um. That actually isn’t- I mean-”

Buck could feel himself stumbling over the words, eventually just blurting out with,

“I love you.”

Eddie eyed him, warmth mixed with concern on his face.

“I love you, too,” he said. “What’s going on?”

Buck took a breath and then out from his mouth spilled,

“I asked Danny to ask Mehta if the spot he offered was still open.”

Eddie froze, hand still buried in the bag of candy. Slowly, carefully, he slid it out and crossed his arms, leaning against the table. His face was blank in a way it had never been when they were kids, before Eddie had to grow up far faster and rougher than Buck.

“You want to transfer.”

“Yeah,” Buck said, the word coming out with a sigh.

“I thought everything these past few months was to come back to the One Eighteen.”

“It was to get back to firefighting, Eddie,” he said. “Did I do any firefighting yesterday, when I was stuck behind a table and being bullied by children?”

“No,” was given grudgingly. “I’ve hated being there without you.”

“I’m sorry,” Buck said, even though hearing that made something warm bloom in his chest. “But I- I don’t trust Bobby. He spent weeks lying to me, and then he punished me because he didn’t like my choices. I mean, he- he’s always treated me a little different from the rest of you, I’ve even joked about it, but this…”

“What do you mean?” Eddie asked.

“You remember hearing about Chimney’s accident, right? Rebar through the brain, and he was back a month later. No hoops, no questions about him being a liability, no questions about whether or not he was ready to be back,” Buck said. “And remember that day the CAD system was out? When you did your Spider-Man routine to get to that kid?”

“Yeah.”

“If I’d done that, Bobby would still be yelling at me for it,” he said. “I even said that to him, and he never denied it. Because it’s true.”

“Maybe he just doesn’t care about me as much as you,” Eddie joked.

“But that’s the problem, isn’t it?” Buck pointed out. “He cares about me like- like Mom cared about me.”

Eddie, having met Margaret Buckley in person and having heard all about her from both Buckley brothers, winced like he just saw someone take a fastball to the nuts.

“And I don’t know why,” Buck continued. “He doesn’t treat Hen and Chimney like this. He doesn’t treat you like this, and we’re the exact same age!”

“When Danny came to the station, he made a point of telling Bobby that you weren’t his kid. Bobby’s kid, I mean.”

Buck’s nose scrunched up.

“But Danny knows I took him on a date once,” he said. Eddie, who’d unwrapped a piece of hard candy and had just popped it into his mouth, started choking on it. Buck slapped him on the back. “I mean, he didn’t know it was a date then...”

When Eddie managed to cough up the candy, sending it bouncing onto the floor to be picked up later, he practically screeched out the question of,

“You did what!?”

“I took him to a Springsteen concert,” Buck said, grabbing a paper towel to go hunt down the erstwhile choking hazard. “I’d been at the One Eighteen for a couple months then.”

“And you just… Took your captain on a date.” Eddie shook his head, and then started frowning at nothing.

Buck picked up the candy and tossed it into the trash with the paper towel, and then he poked at Eddie.

“What?”

“We haven’t gone on a date, yet,” Eddie said, still frowning. “I promised you something public and fancy, and then Shannon happened, and then your leg, and I… I got comfortable, and just…”

He grabbed Buck by the wrist once more and when he gently tugged, Buck allowed Eddie to pull him closer.

“Go on a date with me.”

“Right now?” Buck gestured with his thumb at the kitchen door. “I think you’re forgetting about Chris.”

Eddie rolled his eyes.

“No, pendejo-”

“Hey!”

“Not now, but soon,” he said, and then screwed up his face. “You’ll be on a different schedule from me, we’ll have to figure that out.”

“Look at the bright side,” Buck said. “There’ll be days you’re working and I’m not-”

“How is that a bright side?” Eddie interrupted to ask, making Buck roll his eyes.

“Because then you won’t have to depend on Carla or your family to watch Chris,” Buck pointed out.

“I guess,” his boyfriend muttered, sounding so very much like the moody teenager he used to be. “Still rather have you watching my back.”

“Yeah, I know.” Buck sighed, pressed his forehead against Eddie’s. “You know Bobby picked you out specifically for me. He thought we’d be a good fit.”

Eddie let out a laugh.

“Little did he know.” He pulled away with a groan, twisting around to grab a couple of the takeout menus laying on the table behind him. “Help me decide what to make for dinner.”

Buck rolled his eyes even as he tapped at the pizza menu in Eddie’s hand.

Buck ended up spending the night at the Diazes, to the shock of absolutely no one. It was a Saturday, so that meant Eddie got to sleep-in while Buck, ever the early riser, joined Chris for his morning stretches and then made them both breakfast. It also meant that he had to explain to a worried Chris what happened to his arm, since he came out of the bedroom in one of Eddie’s short-sleeved shirts.

(Everyone had gotten used to the random bruises showing up on Buck, enough so that they mostly didn’t comment on the ones Eddie left as long as they weren’t so obviously toothy.)

Eddie, ever the grumpy bear when it came to mornings, didn’t rouse until some hours later. He came stumbling out, rubbing at his eyes and yawning, to find Buck and Christopher sitting on the couch with a bag of candy between them as they watched cartoons. The man blinked at them for a moment before joining them.

Their lazy Saturday morning stretched into a lazy Saturday afternoon, but Buck needed to head back to the apartment at some point. Not only had he left his uniform there, something he would need for their shift tomorrow, but he needed to talk to Danny, too. His brother had texted to tell him that Mehta was going to send him home with the transfer request to be filled out and handed in. Eddie had taken the news grimly, but he didn’t try to get Buck to change his mind.

“Maybe I could convince him to trade someone for you,” Buck joked.

Eddie looked for a moment like he might actually take him up on that, before he shook his head.

“I don’t think my nerves can handle working with Danny when he has access to an ax,” he said.

“Wuss.”

When evening rolled around, Buck bid farewell to Eddie and a disappointed Chris and went home. Danny was on the couch when he got in, his dinner - a bowl of cereal - in hand. Buck paused where he stood to frown at him, trying to decide if Danny was actually looking paler than normal or if it was just the light.

“There’s the paperwork,” he said, pointing at it on the coffee table with his spoon. “Fill it in, give it to Nash.”

Buck walked over, tossing his overnight bag out of the way, and picked the paperwork up. There wasn’t a lot to it when it came to switching between stations. There was a spot to put in which station he was moving to, and then space at the bottom for both himself and his current captain to sign and date.

“If he gives you any trouble about it,” Danny continued, “Mehta said to go over his head and talk to Chief Williams.”

“Wouldn’t that get Bobby in trouble?”

“Probably.” His brother shrugged. “It would be his own fault.”

Yeah, there was no point for Buck to expect Danny to express any sympathy for Bobby. Once Danny decided he didn’t like someone, nothing would ever change his mind, and that ship had sailed once he learned about Bobby laying hands on him. Eddie was the only exception that Buck knew about so far.

(Their parents were something else, entirely.)

The next morning, Buck was a bundle of nerves when he walked into the station. Eddie served as an anchoring presence as he smiled and nodded to everyone, trying to act natural even as he felt like he was about to puke at any moment. They changed into their uniforms, and then hunted Bobby down, paperwork in hand.

They found the captain upstairs in the loft, looking through the fridge.

“Uh, Bobby?” Buck said, after clearing his throat.

“Yeah?”

Buck let out a shaking breath as he held the paperwork out.

“This is for you,” he said.

Bobby pulled his head out of the fridge to look, letting the door close. He took the paperwork from Buck’s hand, his entire face turning into a frown as he saw what it was.

“Buck, what…?”

“I’m requesting to transfer to the One Thirty-three,” Buck said. Behind him, where the others had gathered, conversation stopped and he felt, more than saw, the way all eyes were suddenly on him.

Bobby seemed to have noticed their audience, because he gestured for Buck to follow him to his office.

“Is this because I had you as the man behind when you came back?” the captain asked when they got there, the door closing behind them.

“Yes,” Buck said, wishing Eddie was there. “But it’s mostly because I don’t trust you.”

“Buck-”

“No, Bobby.” He shook his head. “You lied to me for weeks, kept me from my job, and then you punished me for coming back anyway. How am I supposed to trust you?”

“I was just trying to-” Bobby cut himself off, tossing the paperwork onto his desk and dragging his hand down his face. “You nearly died three times!”

“Is it because they happened so close together?” Buck asked him. “If they were spread out like Chimney’s, would that be better?”

“No, Buck!”

“Danny and I, we left Hershey and our parents because Mom wanted me to be tucked away and safe,” Buck told Bobby. “She didn’t want me getting hurt, so she tried to wrap me up in cotton and keep me in a glass case like fine china. And I was miserable. Just like I was miserable when you did it to me.”

Bobby was propping himself on the desk with his fists, shaking his head down at the transfer request.

“You just needed to be patient, a tantrum isn’t-”

“This isn’t a tantrum!” Buck snapped. “I don’t trust you anymore, Bobby!”

A knife to the gut might’ve been less painful, judging from the expression on Bobby’s face.

“You really want to leave,” the captain whispered, but it was quiet enough in the office for Buck to hear.

“I want to be useful and do my job,” Buck told him. “You have spent weeks, months even, keeping me from that. Maybe I don’t know Mehta, but I know he’ll actually let me be a firefighter again.”

Bobby was quiet, staring at him with an expression that Buck couldn’t quite decipher. Then he let his head fall forward and, slowly, he picked up a pen.

Chapter 17

Chapter Notes

cw: Daniel makes references to and jokes about his past sex work.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

The shift that followed was tense, the atmosphere in the station feeling much like growing up at home in Hershey after an argument between his siblings and his parents. Unlike the shift before, Buck wasn’t stuck as the man behind and was actually allowed to join the others on calls. Maybe it was an attempt to get him to try and change his mind, Bobby giving him what he wanted in the hopes that he would decide to stay at the last moment. But Buck hadn’t been lying when he said he no longer trusted Bobby, not about this.

There was no guarantee, after all, that the captain wouldn’t just arbitrarily change his mind and decide to keep him back again for his own safety.

Working with the others again, working with Eddie again, was nice, however. Buck slotted back into place like he’d never left, him and Eddie working as an efficient pair wherever they were needed. They had always been on the same wavelength, even when everything between was fraught and on the verge of erupting, and it might have been even more true now. It was just a shame that there was a countdown to it all, now.

The others were all curious about whatever happened between him and Bobby, Buck could tell that much. It was obvious with the way that all eyes followed him when he came back from the captain’s office, and with the way the Chimney kept opening his mouth in the engine only to shut it with a click, and with the way conversations cut off when Buck got too close. He was simply thankful he wasn’t being ambushed for an interrogation. Not yet, anyways, because he knew it was inevitable.

It was a very long twenty-four hours, and Buck was exhausted by the end of it. He was looking forward to leaving and hanging out with Eddie, maybe he’d check in with Danny and tell him that Bobby hadn’t put up much of a fuss at all. But just as he was done getting his shoes on, sitting on the bench in the locker room and slumped into Eddie’s side, Hen said,

“Let’s get breakfast.”

Buck might’ve turned it down, except his stomach chose that moment to throw in its two-cents. The meals that Bobby had made them that shift weren’t quite as filling or as appetizing as they usually were, though that might have been because of the way the table kept being choked with awkward small talk. And he hadn’t eaten yet that morning, anyways, so breakfast did sound good. Even if it came with a side of piping hot inquisition.

After a reluctant agreement and a short argument about where to go, Buck and Eddie split apart and made for the parking lot. The place they’d decided on was an all-day breakfast place, just outside of downtown. It wasn’t that far away, though, if one wasn’t dragging their feet and wasn’t stupid enough to try the Freeway.

Hen and Chimney beat them both there, because Buck did drag his feet a bit, regardless of how hungry he was, and that meant Eddie did too. The two paramedics were waiting inside, having already claimed a table. Buck and Eddie threw themselves into the seats opposite them, smiling at the waitress when she came over to get their order of coffee.

“So,” Hen said, once the waitress was gone. “Transferring?”

“Yup.” Buck, without much thought, threw his arm around the back of Eddie’s seat. He felt his boyfriend stiffen for a moment, glance around them, let out a breath, and then almost pointedly relax more against his arm. “Mehta offered me a spot at the One Thirty-three, so I’m taking it.”

“C’mon, Buck,” Chimney groaned. “I told you, you just needed to suck it up and be patient. It wasn’t going to be forever.”

“Sucking it up and being patient had me sent home early,” Buck pointed out with a humorless smile. “Because he didn’t want to overtax me too much.”

“And then you went and got yourself hurt saving people,” Eddie grumbled, scowling at the laminated menu in his hand. “Again.”

Buck made a face at him, because that had the paramedics sitting up in their seats with expressions of concern. He’d done a good job hiding the healing cut on his arm from everyone at the station until now, but the cat was very much out of the bag. With a sigh, he removed his arm from the back of Eddie’s seat, pulled the sleeve up, and laid it down on the table for his friends to see for themselves.

“I just cut myself on some safety glass,” he assured them. It was one long, scabbed over line now; he hadn’t bothered putting a new bandage on it this time around.

“What happened?” Hen asked. She was peering down at it from behind her glasses, her and Chimney both having leaned in to frown at it closer.

“A woman with a brain bleed and a guy stuck in her windshield,” Buck explained. The pair raised their heads to fix him with the exact same look, because they spent far too much time with each other. “They were both going to be fine, last I heard.”

“He’d be fine if he’d been wearing his gear,” Eddie complained. “But no, we can’t let him go out on calls with us. He might get hurt.”

“Sometimes it is very obvious that you hung around Danny a lot as a teenager,” Buck remarked, resting his cheek on his fist to look at him. His boyfriend preened a little at the words.

“I told Bobby to stop being an idiot and let you do your job.” Hen sighed and shook her head. “Or to let you go somewhere that would.”

“He signed the papers,” Buck pointed out. “I’m gonna hand them in myself after this.”

Part of Buck didn’t trust that Bobby wouldn’t just “forget” to file the request if it was left to him. After all, the captain hadn’t seen any problem with taking it upon himself to decide whether or not Buck was ready to come back to active duty. He would just much rather know that it was done, instead of risking the chance of things being drawn out over drama that benefited no one and hurt everyone.

“You’re still doing it?” Chimney asked him, sounding dismayed. “You went on every call with us yesterday.”

“Which wouldn’t have happened if I wasn’t transferring. I don’t have reason to trust he won’t suddenly change his mind again.”

Chimney grimaced, but he didn’t try to argue the point.

The waitress arrived with their coffees, finally. Buck hurried to pick something to eat, because unlike the other three, he’d been wasting his time yapping away instead of figuring out what he was going to order. He still wasn’t certain what to get when it was his turn, not until Eddie just reached over and tapped at an option, which was what he went with. After this long, Eddie knew well enough what Buck liked.

“Remember when you made a big deal out of me making your sister hot mustard,” Chimney said, once the waitress was gone with their orders.

“I remember using it to point out that you and Maddie were dating,” Buck shot back. “Okay, Hen, we need to make a plan.”

“Oh, we do, do we?” Hen sounded unimpressed as she leaned back, arms crossed over her chest.

“Yeah! ‘Cause I’m guaranteed to see these two idiots,” he said, finger moving between Chimney and Eddie. Both of them rolled their eyes at him, which was rude. “But you and I, we have to make a point of it.”

Hen let out a hum, fingers tapping on the table top, and nodded.

“You’re right,” she said. “I dropped the ball with that before, and I did promise not to do that again.”

“That’s the spirit.”

Austin’s apartment wasn’t too terribly different from Daniel’s own. It was smaller, a single bedroom for a single person, and there were bits of lived-in clutter everywhere. Little figurines in front of books on shelves; a couple crayon drawings stuck to the fridge with magnets, alongside a grocery list and the menu for the closest Chinese restaurant that delivered; a throw pillow on the couch striped in black and gray and white and purple; mismatched picture frames on the walls that weren’t quite even with one another

There were very few pictures of Austin himself hanging up, a grand total of three. There was one, which must have been taken when he graduated high school, with a teenaged version of the man in a red cap and gown with his parents. Another, opposite the previous, showed him fresh out of the firefighter’s academy, in dress uniform and posing with his brother and sister. The third was of him holding a red-faced newborn, the little bundle wrapped in a blue-striped blanket and in the middle of screaming their head off. That picture was Daniel’s favorite, if only for the wide-eyed, horrified expression that was captured on Austin’s face.

Austin himself was currently at the stove, stirring a pot of something and looking cozy in a pair of sweats. He’d been in the middle of cooking dinner when Daniel came over, and had only rolled his eyes at the man letting himself. Daniel tossed his jacket onto the back of a chair, grabbed a beer from the fridge, and perched himself on the edge of the kitchen island.

“What’re ya makin’?” he asked, twisting off the bottle cap and tossing it into the nearby trash can.

“Stew.”

“Smells nice. How was your shift yesterday?”

Austin turned his head to eye him over his shoulder. Then, with a frown, he set aside the spoon he was using to stir with, put a lid over the pot, and turned to look at Daniel fully.

“Are you feeling okay?” he asked.

“Yeah.” No. “Why do you ask?”

“You’re looking a bit…” Austin’s frown grew heavier as he decided on the word. “A bit worn around the edges.”

Daniel was feeling a bit worn around the edges. A bit achy in places, a lot tired all the time. There was a slight, persistent chill that clung to him no matter what he did, and he’d had the worst headache for the past couple of days. But none of that mattered, because,

“I’m fine.”

Doubt was clear on Austin’s face as the man kept looking him over. Just to mess with him a little, Daniel stretched out a bit against the island, shirt pulling up a little, as he put himself on display like he used to back in the day. It was normally enough to have Austin laughing at him and calling him an idiot. Instead, the man reached out and tugged at his shirt, lifting it up.

“You have another bruise,” he said as he poked at a spot on Daniel’s hip.

“It’s probably nothing,” Daniel said, waving it away. “Or, hey! With my work history, maybe it’s AIDS.”

“Dan.”

“I’m joking, I always made sure they used a condom,” he said, and then, with his brightest smile, “Unless they paid extra.”

Austin was very much not amused, that much was obvious as the man shook his head at him and went back to the stove, lifting the lid to check on the food. With him no longer looking, Daniel poked at the bruise for himself. It was small and fresh, still in the process of blooming. He remembered hitting a cabinet door with his hip to close it earlier in the day, remembered feeling the corner dig in a bit, but he hadn’t thought he hit it hard enough to leave a bruise like this.

“I got tested religiously, it isn’t AIDS.” Daniel wasn’t certain if he was reassuring Austin or himself. Not that either one of them needed to be reassured about anything, because everything was perfectly fine. “You never said how your shift was, though.”

“Are you asking because you care, or because you want to know how Cap treated your precious child?”

“Oh, come on.” Daniel set his drink down and crossed the space between them, draping himself over Austin’s back. “I’m a multitasker, it can be both. ‘Sides, Buck already told me that Nash didn’t fight very hard about the paperwork.”

“He came out of the office looking like someone ran over his puppy,” Austin said, and then side-eyed Daniel. “Or like you came in and threw his family’s deaths in his face again.”

Daniel let out a groan, dropping his head against the other man’s shoulder.

“Yes, yes, I’m a giant bag of dicks,” he said, pulling away and going back to where his drink was waiting. “That was ages ago, we’ve moved on.”

“Sure.” Austin could’ve at least tried to be convincing. “He spent the rest of the shift just kinda moping about the station. Even his food tasted sad.”

“Are you trying to get me to feel bad for him? ‘Cause it ain’t gonna happen.”

“Honestly, I’m just thankful you haven’t killed him.” Austin took the spoon he’d been using to stir with and took a taste of the stew, letting out a pleased little hum. “Get bowls down.”

“You know I like it when you’re bossy,” Daniel joked, laughing at the man’s look of distaste as he did as he was told. “Hey, how good are you at poker?”

Austin stared at him as one might a venomous snake that was rearing up to strike.

“Why?”

“Apparently Mehta goes to a game on the regular with some other people from the department,” Daniel explained, setting the bowls down on the counter next to Austin. He went to the drawer with the silverware to get them both forks. “I’m not that good at it, and the only times I’ve been to actual poker games, I was just there as somebody’s arm candy.”

“Did you tell him you sucked?” Austin asked and then the moment Daniel opened his mouth, he added, “You know what I meant, you pervert.”

“Calling me a pervert, but you’re the one who thought it,” Daniel complained, watching as the other man filled both their bowls. “I did, in fact, tell him I was bad at poker. Pretty sure that’s the reason he invited me.”

“So he thinks you’re an easy mark.” Austin handed Daniel his bowl and then grabbed a bag of Hawaiian rolls from a cabinet. He carried both it and his own bowl of stew to the couch, Daniel following behind him. “And now you want me to go with you to, what? Watch your embarrassment in person?”

“I mean I’d much prefer it if you turned out to be some sort of savant and embarrassed them,” Daniel said, kicking his feet up on the coffee table. “Dunno, guess it sounded fun.”

“It sounded fun,” Austin repeated. Daniel just shrugged. “Sure, fine, whatever.”

The pair settled in, with Austin flipping through channels on the TV until he landed on something they both agreed on. Daniel ate only half of his serving, finding he wasn’t as hungry as he thought he was, and set his bowl aside. His appetite had started taking its leave around the time he noticed the aches.

“How are you most scared to die?” he asked, mostly out of the blue.

Austin turned his head to stare at him.

“What?”

“How are you most scared to die?” Daniel repeated. “And don’t say “in a fire,” that one’s too obvious.”

They’d both seen enough of those types of deaths to have it stuck in a permanent first place on this particular list. If fate was kind, the smoke would smother a person before the flames reached them. If fate was particularly cruel, a person could just fall directly into a fire, like that probie the 133 had years before Daniel transferred from El Paso.

“Why the hell are you asking that?”

“Dunno.” Daniel shrugged. “Just… Popped in my head.”

“I thought I was friends with the normal brother,” Austin said, shaking his head as he turned back to the television. “Falling, probably.”

“That makes sense.”

Austin was the slightest bit scared of heights, he knew. Not bad enough to be a phobia, not quite, and it thankfully didn’t come up all that often when Austin worked since he was attached to the engine. But it was there all the same.

“There was a Sylvester Stallone movie I watched forever ago,” Austin said, and then interrupted himself to ask, “You do know who Sylvester Stallone is, right?”

“Sure, he was in that Spy Kids movie I took Buck to see, and the Expendables.”

“Good enough. Anyways, the movie opens with them trying to rescue a couple from a mountain, and the woman’s harness fails. She screams the entire way down. I had the same nightmare about it for a week straight.”

Daniel cringed at the imagery his mind conjured up.

“Gee, thanks,” he said. “You know I’m gonna have to watch Buck do rope rescues, right?”

“I know.” What an asshole. “What about you, Mr. Big Thoughts?”

That was easy.

“Being stuck in a hospital bed and wasting away,” was Daniel’s immediate answer.

This time it was Austin’s turn to cringe.

“Yeah,” he said. “I should’ve guessed that one.”

Chapter End Notes

(I am not a good person.)

Chapter 18

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

It took about a week after Buck put in the transfer request for it to go through and for everything to be arranged on the administrative side. There were questions, of course, especially since he handed the request in personally rather than going through his (former) captain. He was asked to come in on his day off and while what followed wasn’t officially an interrogation, the presence of a battalion chief, as well as someone from the department’s HR department and the union, made him feel like it was.

Considering the context of his return to work, and the timing of his transfer request, it only made sense that the department would have questions. Buck did his best to answer them without throwing anyone, without throwing Bobby, under the bus. Mostly he just told them that he realized it would be better for himself and for others if he moved to another station, and no, he wasn’t forced out, and no, he didn’t see the need in making any formal complaints against anyone at the 118.

Buck wasn’t certain if he was successful in avoiding Bobby getting penalized in any way, he never considered himself the best with his words or at explaining himself, but he hoped he managed it.

The shifts preceding him leaving the 118 were bittersweet, in a way. Because he was going to miss working with them all, even if he would still see at least some of them. It was almost enough to have him regretting and rethinking his decision. But only almost. Because however much he would miss having Eddie’s back on a call, however much he would miss hanging out with Hen and Chimney at the station in their downtime, it didn’t do a thing to change how his trust in Bobby as his captain was, perhaps irreparably, damaged.

It still sucked how the man seemed to carry a constant air of hurt whenever Buck saw him.

“I don’t think he did it out of a sense of malice,” Hen said at one point, while Buck was helping her go through the ambulance’s inventory. “He did it because he cares about you and he was scared.”

“If he’s that scared about me doing my job, then maybe this is a good thing for him, too,” Buck pointed out.

Hen hadn’t agreed with him, but she also hadn’t disagreed, either.

On Buck’s last day at the 118, they held a farewell party for him. There was a banner, there were balloons, there was a cake. And unlike the last party that was thrown for him, he didn’t cough up blood all over the place. Afterwards, at the end of his shift, he went through the chore of clearing out his locker and carting out his turnout gear. It was his gear, after all, and there was no way they’d have spare bunker pants at the 133 that would fit his long legs.

On the morning of his first day at his new station, Buck woke up feeling not quite as nervous as he did on his first day at the 118, but close to it. It was that “starting a new school” anxiety, like when he started school in El Paso. For all of his extroverted ways, he’d never quite figured out how to comfortably fit into a new group of people right away. Even when he made the football team in high school, even when he was allowed on the field and helped them win more than once, he was always a bit too different to be popular. A bit too loud, a bit too bouncy, a bit too weird.

It helped, though, that he’d grown into his skin a bit more since those days. Plus, it wasn’t like he was going into this new station completely blind. Sure, he didn’t know these people well, he certainly wasn’t as familiar with them as he was with the people Danny worked with in El Paso, but he’d met some of them before, at least in passing. A couple times, they’d hit the bar Buck was working at for after shift drinks. Plus, while it didn’t happen often, the 118 worked scenes with the 133 sometimes.

Buck woke up before his brother, and he worked off some of the anxious energy after he showered by making them both breakfast. By the time Danny came lumbering out, covering up a yawn as he did so, Buck was setting a plate of pancakes with sausage and eggs onto the table for him, along with coffee.
