Danny blearily eyed it, and then him, before sitting down and digging in.

Afterwards, while Danny took his turn in the shower, Buck cleared the table and made sure his work bag was all set. His brother came out to find him going through it, shook his head, and simply went to his room to grab his own.

“C’mon, squirt,” he said, scooping up his keys. “Let’s go.”

They were carpooling, since they were going to the same place, and they were taking Danny’s car by writ of Danny being older. It left Buck feeling like he was a kid again, especially when he reached over to change the channel on the radio and got his hand smacked away.

“We are not gonna listen to that pop shit you like,” Danny told him, finger wagging in his direction while the man kept his eyes on the road in front of them. When Buck opened his mouth to argue, he cut him off with, “Or NPR, you weirdo.”

“Do we really have to listen to mullet rock, though?”

“Yes,” Danny said. “I’m the driver, I get to pick the music. Bratty kid brothers get to shut their mouths.”

Buck was left to scowl at his brother and stew in his seat as he wondered if this was truly worth saving on gas.

Station 133 was a bit to the east and north of the 118, not really that far away, and closer to where they lived. While the 118 looked almost like a warehouse from the outside, the 133 was two-stories tall and vaguely Spanish Colonial in design, red clay roof tiles along with stucco and brick walls. It sat at an intersection, making a vague L-shape, with a main entrance on the corner and a “wing” that had the bay doors.

Danny parked them in the lot attached to the station, marked from the outside world by a brick-topped stucco wall. Between the two of them, they managed to carry both their bags along with Buck’s gear inside, Danny greeting people as they went. The 133’s engine, ladder, and ambulances were lined up in the bay, where the cages for their turnout gear lined one of the long walls.

“This is you,” Danny said, unceremoniously dumping Buck’s turnout coat in front of an empty cage. A helmet with 133 was resting on top of it, and the name-plate was covered by a piece of tape with “E. Buckley” scrawled on it in sharpie. “We had a probie, but they called it quits when they were barely into their second month.”

“What did you do, try to cook for them?” Buck teased, setting down his work bag to get his gear in order. “Where’re you?”

“Over there.” Danny pointed down the row.

“Buckley and Buckley,” a voice said. Buck turned to greet his new captain, or so he assumed: the man’s name tag said Mehta. Mehta eyed the coat Buck had just finished hanging up. “Yeah, that’ll do for now, but I’ve already ordered you a new coat.”

“A new…” Buck trailed off, glancing at the piece of tape again. “Come on, really? Why do I have to be E. Buckley? Can’t he be D. Buckley?”

“I was here first, squirt,” Danny told him, throwing an arm around his shoulders and pulling him down to knuckle his head. Buck managed to wiggle out from under his arm and shove him away.

“Huh.” Mehta looked vaguely unnerved about something. “Right, anyway. Buckley, Daniel, show him to the locker room and then give him the tour.”

“Got it, Cap,” Danny said, and then slapped Buck on the shoulder as he started off. “C’mon, it’s this way.”

Buck grabbed his bag off the ground and hurried after him. There was a hall that led from the bay to the rest of the station. Just inside, across from each other, were two doorways. When Buck poked his head into the one on his right, he saw a long room that appeared to be the station’s rec area. There was a foosball table and a pinball machine on the end of the room nearest to him; on the other end was another doorway to the hall and a large television on the wall, with couches and comfy chairs arranged in front of it.

Danny went through the door on the other side of the hall. Buck followed him, and then clutched at the door frame.

“Oh, thank God,” he said, doing his best to literally hug the room. “Walls!”

“What’s up with him?” The person asking was vaguely familiar to Buck, enough that he recognized him as one of the ones that came in for drinks. He was an inch or two shorter than both brothers, with coiffed dark hair.

“The locker room at my last station has glass walls,” Buck answered, giving the solid, non-see through wall a grateful pat.

“Seriously?” The guy made a face as Danny nodded to him.

“It's true, just ask Austin,” he confirmed, and pointed at a locker with a Post-It note stuck to it. “That one’s yours.”

“Made it really awkward when we ran a blood drive last year, and there were all these people coming in,” Buck added, going to his new locker. He sat his bag down on the bench and started dragging out his uniform to change. He took off his shirt, and heard the other man let out a whistle.

“Mendoza, stop eying up my baby brother,” Danny said, heading to his own locker. “He’s taken.”

“Taken by a vampire, apparently,” Mendoza said with a laugh. Buck ignored the comment even as he felt himself flush, because he’d already heard it all from Hen and the others. Still, he hoped he wouldn’t have to be on the blood thinners for much longer. “Mostly I’m just waitin’ to see if that leg is real.”

“Mendoza!”

“It’s real,” Buck assured, patting his still covered thigh before bending down to untie his shoes. “They stuck a rod in, though.”

“That’s all? Shit. The way it looked on TV, I thought for sure…” Mendoza sat on the bench next to him. “Jon Mendoza, one of the paramedics. I’d better do the introductions, I don’t think your brother even remembers half of our names.”

“That’s not true!” Danny argued, voice muffled by his shirt as he pulled it over his head.

“Oh yeah?” Mendoza asked. “What’s Flowers’ actual name, then?”

“You mean Florian?” Danny shot back. “You should call him Flowers when we introduce you, Buck. It'll be hilarious.”

“He hates it,” Mendoza cheerfully said, and then leaned in closer to Buck. “Hey, since we’ve got you here now, maybe you can tell us a few things.”

“Like what?” Buck asked him, feeling his own smile grow. The opportunity to embarrass his older brother in front of his brother’s friends? Hell, yes, count him in.

“Like, for instance…” Mendoza let out a hum in thought. “How did Dan get that cigarette burn on his ass?”

“Cigarette…” Buck twisted his head to look at Danny. His older brother was resolutely looking straight ahead. “Danny, what?”

“Oh, you didn’t know about it? Damn.” Mendoza sounded disappointed. “There has to be a hilarious story behind it, right?”

“Yeah…” Buck was still staring at his brother, who seemed to be doing his best to will either himself or them out of existence. There were little bruises littering his skin here and there, some freshly blue and purple, others aged green and yellow. And the fluorescent lights made him look washed out. “So, uh. You know Austin?”

“Sure do, same class at the academy. I came here, he went to the One Twenty-two.”

“The One Twenty-two?” Buck scrunched up his face in confusion.

“He moved stations when the One Eighteen needed a new driver-engineer,” Danny said, because of course he knew the answer to Buck’s unspoken question.

“Okay, so, has your brother told you anyone’s names?” Mendoza asked.

“Uh.” Buck stood up to change pants and gave the man a shrug. “No?”

“Seriously, Buckley?” Mendoza glared at Danny. “Do we not matter to you at all?”

“Nope,” was Danny’s response.

“Wow.” Mendoza shook his head, dramatically clutching at his chest. “I’m wounded.”

Buck, pulling on his uniform, snickered to himself.

“Alright.” The paramedic pointed a stern finger at Buck.  “Listen up, Baby Buckley, ‘cause there’ll be a pop quiz at the end and if you get any of them wrong, you’re cleaning the rigs with a toothbrush.”

Danny shook his head at Buck, when he looked at his brother to check if the man was being serious or not.

“Okay, cool kids first, the paramedics-”

“Aw, you think I’m cool, Mendoza?” Danny was grinning again. “Thanks.”

“You spend all your time driving the rig, you don’t count,” Mendoza said, which made Danny laugh. “Anyways, the other paramedics…”

“How do I look?”

Daniel lifted his head up, ignoring how stiff his neck felt and adjusting the hood of the sweater he was wearing so it wasn’t bundled up uncomfortably, to peer over at his baby brother. Buck was standing in the bathroom doorway, hair freshly combed and wearing a light blue button down with black dress pants, holding his arms out to his side for better viewing. He was backlit, though, which meant Daniel had to squint a little.

“Like I need to call up Alma so we can get drunk together again,” he remarked. His brother huffed and rolled his eyes. “Oh, c’mon, he’s already seen you naked and he named his kid after you, he’s not gonna care how you look.”

“Well, maybe I care,” Buck complained, going back to the bathroom to continue preening in front of the mirror. “Eddie’s actually taking me out somewhere on a date. And it’s not a Denny’s! I need to look good.”

“It only took him forever to get around to it,” Daniel grumbled.

He swung his legs off the couch and pushed himself to his feet, padding over to his bedroom. His closet door was already open, so it was just a matter of selecting what he wanted and reaching in to grab it. Buck was still in the bathroom when Daniel came out, fussing with his hair, and he startled when a tie was sent flying at his face.

“What the hell!?” he yelped, fumbling to catch the bright red piece of cloth. “Danny!”

“Wear that,” was all Daniel said, before heading back to the couch.

“Thanks!”

He was about to sit down again when there was knocking at the apartment door. Something was knocked down in the bathroom, Daniel heard the clatter and the cursing, and then Buck was a blur of motion as he ran past him, still doing up the tie. He practically leaped the remaining space to answer the door.

“Hey! You’re- Oh.” The disappointment was audible in his voice.

“Wow,” Austin said, stepping past Buck to come inside. “You really know how to make a guy feel special, Buck. Nice clothes.”

“Eddie’s picking him up for a date,” Daniel said. 

Austin looked at him, his entire face turning into one big frown. He opened his mouth, glanced at Buck, and then shook his head.

“Haven’t they been together since May?” was what he ended up going with.

“Eddie’s a dipshit,” was Daniel’s answer.

“Hey!” Buck was puffed up adorably, offended on behalf of his boyfriend. “You like Eddie again, remember?”

“I never agreed to that,” Daniel pointed out.

“You’re a jerk.”

“You’ve got a pimple on your forehead.”

Buck’s eyes went wide and he sprinted for the bathroom, skidding to a stop in front of the mirror once more. Daniel watched as he leaned as close to the glass as he could manage while still seeing, checking his face from every angle.

“You asshole!” Buck shouted at him. “I do not!”

“All of this seems like far too much effort,” Austin commented, taking a seat at the dining table.

“See, that’s what I was saying,” Daniel, going to the fridge to get drinks for the both of them. “Different reasons, obviously.”

“You both suck,” Buck said, once more anxiously fretting over his appearance.

“I do not,” Austin retorted. “Your brother, on the other hand…”

“I tried to tell Hen and Chimney that you made jokes, but they wouldn’t believe me.”

“You look fine, Buck!” Daniel set Austin’s drink in front of him, the glass of the bottle clinking against the wood. “I’ve seen him go on actual first dates without fretting this much.”

“Shut up,” Buck said, the words lacking any heat. “You-”

Once more, there was knocking at the door. Once more, Buck nearly killed himself rushing out of the bathroom. Daniel beat him to the door, though, opening it to see an equally dressed up and nervous looking Eddie standing in the hallway, a bouquet of flowers in his hand.

“Oh, Danny, uh…”

“Eds!” Buck practically shoved Daniel out of the way. “Hi.”

“Hi.” There was a moment where the two just smiled at each other, Daniel rolling his eyes at the both of them, and then Eddie was thrusting the flowers at Buck. “Here, for you.”

Buck ducked his head with a blush as he took them.

“Thanks,” he said, and then sniffed the air. “You put on the good cologne for me.”

“Yeah,” Eddie said, smiling at him.

“Jesus Christ,” Daniel grumbled. “Just go away, the both of you.”

“Sorry,” Buck said, not sounding very sorry at all. “I know you’ve apparently planned something with your not-boyfriend.”

“Austin’s here?” Eddie leaned around Buck to look, and then waved. “Oh, uh. Hi.”

Austin, turned around in his chair, waved back.

“Alright, I’m off,” Buck said, patting one-handedly at his pockets to make sure he had everything he needed and then grabbing a jacket off the nearby rack. “Bye, Danny!”

“Don’t choke on anything this time,” Daniel told him.

He recognized the impish expression Buck was wearing when he turned around. The moment his brother opened his mouth, Daniel slammed the door in his face.

“Rude!” Buck shouted through the door. In response, Daniel locked it. The deadbolt made a delightfully, and pointedly, loud click as he turned it.

“I really hope his kid is having a sleepover,” Daniel told Austin, joining him at the table where his own drink was waiting. “The last thing I want is my wine drunk baby brother waking me up.”

“How do you know he’s going to get wine drunk?”

“Because he has trouble with moderation, and I know the place they’re going to has a wine menu,” he answered. Austin raised an eyebrow, and Daniel rolled his eyes. “I helped Eddie pick the place.”

“Oh, yeah, you really hate the guy.”

“It was for my baby brother.”

“Right, sure.”

They fell into comfortable silence, neither of them in any rush to break it. Daniel wasn’t, anyway. He was actually contemplating whether they should order something - he was in the mood for Chinese - or if he should break out leftovers from the dinner Buck made them the other day, when Austin spoke up.

“Are you feeling okay?”

“Seriously?” Daniel rolled his eyes. “Austin, we’ve been-”

“You look like shit, Dan,” Austin said, cutting him off. He reached over to lay a hand on Daniel’s forehead, which he batted away. “And you’re hot-”

“Aw, really?” Daniel grinned at him. “It’s nice to know I’m everyone’s type.”

Austin glared at him.

“You’re running a fever,” he said, refusing to rise to the bait, much to Daniel’s disappointment. “You’ve been running a fever, and you’re paler than you were a month ago.”

“It is November, it’s getting colder. Doesn’t make sense to keep up my summer tan.”

“It was seventy degrees today.”

“I’m fine,” Daniel insisted.

“Yeah, right. And I want to have sex with you.”

“It’s a bit sudden, but sure, take off your-”

“Dan,” Austin snapped, making him flinch. “Go to the doctor, seriously.”

“For what?” Daniel demanded, throwing his arms out and his head back. He winced, because the movement aggravated the headache that wouldn’t go away. “A little cold? I told you, I’m fine.”

“You’re not fine,” Austin argued. “Something’s wrong and I’m worried.”

“Don’t be, it’s just a cold,” he told him, getting up to grab his phone off the coffee table. “It’ll pass. What do you want from that Chinese place?”

“Dan-”

“I’m fine!” Daniel nearly shouted. Austin reeled back in his seat, surprised by the outburst. At a calmer tone, Daniel said, “It’s just a cold, I’m fine. Now what do you want to order?”

After a moment, Austin answered with,

“The usual.”

“Sweet and sour chicken it is,” Daniel said. He gave Austin’s shoulder a pat as he went past, and he ignored the way he could feel the other man’s eyes boring into his back.

Chapter End Notes

I literally gave everyone on Daniel's shift at the 133 a name, and then didn't use a single one of them in this chapter.
(Mendoza doesn't count - he's from way back in Spare Parts, though he does have a first name now.)

Chapter 19

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

The click of the deadbolt was loud in the hallway, and it sent Buck and Eddie both into fits of giggles as they made their way to the elevator. Back in El Paso, Buck and Danny lived in a building that wasn’t really the nicest. The elevator there would be broken more often than not, and when it worked, it rattled and groaned whenever it was in use. Every time someone rode it, it seemed like they were taking their lives into their own hands. It wasn’t unknown for people to get trapped in it, either.

Somehow, that hadn’t ever happened to Buck. It happened to Danny, though. He came back from an exhausting shift, only to find himself stuck in a metal box and in need of rescue. He had not been the happiest of campers that day.

Eddie’s truck was parked in one of the guest spots, and he and Buck goofed off the entire way to it, bumping into each other and generally trying to knock the other down. When they got close to the truck, Eddie suddenly sprinted forward and Buck watched, amused, as he opened the passenger door for him.

“Seriously?” They were both very lucky that the apartment didn’t have a window that faced them, Danny would never let either of them live this down if he saw it.

“I want to do this right,” Eddie mumbled, not looking at him.

Buck rolled his eyes even as he smiled and leaned in to kiss Eddie. Then he climbed into the truck, careful not to crush the bouquet of flowers that he still clutched to his chest. He watched Eddie jog around to the other side and climb into the driver’s seat.

“It’s a good thing it’s not too hot tonight,” Eddie commented as he backed them out of the parking spot. He threw his arm onto the back of Buck’s seat as he did, twisting around to look behind them. “The AC crapped out again. I bought Chris a fan until I can afford to fix it.”

Buck twisted in his seat to look, spotting a small electric fan attached to one of the oh-shit handles in the back.

“I feel like you just went through this a few months ago.”

“Yup. I need to break down and get a new truck,” Eddie said. “I wish I knew how Danny managed to make things work back then, he made it look easy.”

“Oh, um.” Buck nervously glanced out his window, watching the traffic keeping pace with them. “Yeah, he… He’s just awesome that way. Hey, uh, who has Chris right now?”

“Abuela. Carla’s gonna pick him up in the morning and take him to school for me.”

“Oh, you are very confident in how this night will end, aren’t you?.”

“Please,” Eddie said, smirking at him. “We both know it’s a sure thing.”

Buck threw his head back and laughed.

The place Eddie was taking them was downtown, some rooftop restaurant-slash-bar on top of a hotel. He watched as Eddie, after they parked, drew in a large breath and straightened his shoulders before getting out. Before Buck could follow suit, Eddie was on his side and opening his door again.

“Okay, seriously,” Buck said, even as he stepped out, leaving the bouquet of flowers in his seat. “The gentleman thing is very attractive, but you know you don’t have to act like you did with Shannon, right?”

“I’m not,” Eddie told him. He eyed Buck’s hand and, after a moment where he visibly steeled himself, reached out and took it in his own. “I never really took Shannon on a proper date, either. There wasn’t time in between, you know. Everything.”

“Oh.”

Still holding his hand, and very clearly doing his best not to pay attention to anyone they passed, Eddie led Buck through the hotel lobby and to the elevator that would take them to the restaurant. It had a nice view of the city when they reached it, and a host led them to an empty table near the wall that lined the edge. Buck managed to get to his chair and sit down before Eddie could do something silly like try to pull it out for him. His boyfriend rolled his eyes at his antics, as if he was any better, and sat down across the small table from him, immediately tangling their legs together.

A waiter materialized by the table within moments, and Buck watched in bemusement as Eddie ordered a basket of chips to share and water for them both, along with a bottle of wine with a name that he was fairly certain was French. Buck wasn’t really all that picky when it came to wines; there was red and there was white, and he just knew he preferred the red varieties.

“You didn’t even look at the menu,” he teased, laughing a little at the way his boyfriend flushed.

“I looked at it when I was picking the place out,” Eddie said, finally picking the menu up. “You know, to make sure there wasn’t any bread for you to choke on before we actually ordered.”

“Okay, I don’t know who told you the story-”

“It was Danny.”

“Of course it was fucking Danny.” Buck rolled his eyes and huffed. He picked up his own menu to look through it, glancing over at the view of the city as he did. “Well, it ain’t a Denny’s, that’s for sure. Oh, wait, they have chicken tenders. Maybe it is.”

“I’m saving that for the second date,” Eddie told him. “So how has it been at the One Thirty-three?”

Buck scrunched up his mouth as he thought about the best way to answer the question.

“Different,” was what he decided to go with. “It’s different. Like, Bobby has the whole family meals thing going on right?” He waited for Eddie to nod. “They don’t do that. I mean, obviously there’s a cooking rotation - Danny stuck me on the list without even telling me about it - but half the guys either bring in their own food or order something. And there’s no sitting at the dining table together to eat, either. Everyone splits off into their own groups. Kinda like lunch back at school.”

“And Mehta?” Eddie asked, following it with, “Oh, this steak sounds good.”

“I’m eyin’ the burger.”

“‘Course you are.”

The waiter showed back up while Buck was trying to find something to throw at Eddie’s face. Glasses were set in front of them, along with the basket of chips that came with salsa and guacamole, and wine was poured. The opened bottle was left with them after the waiter took off with their orders, something that Buck knew he was going to end up regretting.

It was probably a good thing there was water and actual food already there.

“Anyways, Mehta’s definitely no Bobby,” he said, answering Eddie’s earlier question. “I don’t think I’ve heard anyone at the station call him by his first name. He’s not a hardass or anything, he’s just, y’know. The boss.”

“And everyone else?”

Buck scrunched his face up as he took a swallow of wine.

“It’s like I’m a kid tagging along with my big brother and his friends again.” The words made Eddie laugh. “I’m serious, that’s how they’re treating me! It’s all the teasing from Hen and Chimney but amped up. Nothing mean, just…”

“How you treated Sophia and Adriana.”

“Exactly! They even found a news clip from my probie year, the one I embarrassed myself-”

“Just the one?” Eddie teased.

“Shut up.” Buck threw a napkin at him. It was probably bad form to mention how that interview got him a girlfriend. “How’s the station without me?”

“The same as it was when you were stuck as a fire marshall,” Eddie answered. “Which means it sucks.”

The words had Buck wincing.

“Sorry,” he said.

“Shut up,” Eddie told him. “This is Bobby’s fault, not yours.”

“How, uh. How is he?”

“He’s fine,” was the immediate answer.

(That was a lie. Eddie caught Bobby staring at the empty cage that had once been Buck’s, morose expression on his face. But he wasn’t about to tell his boyfriend that, because he knew better.)

“Speaking of Bobby,” Eddie hurriedly said, leaning forward. “We got called to Hansel and Gretel on ice when it became a bloodbath. Hansel got Gretel’s blade to the chest, and someone had four of their fingers sliced off. We had to go searchin’ for ‘em.”

“Gross,” Buck said with a grin, leaning in as well. “How did that happen?”

“Someone’s skate hit a sequin.”

“Wait, a sequin?” Buck tilted his head as he took another swig of wine. “One of those shiny little things they put on clothes? Someone running over a sequin sent two people to the hospital?”

“Three, Gretel injured her shoulder,” Eddie said. “But yeah, according to Cap, wardrobe malfunctions are the most dangerous part of figure skating.”

“How would he know?”

“Because.” Eddie grinned and wiggled his shoulders a little in excitement. “He used to figure skate. Twin Cities Junior Pairs Champion with someone named Heidi Shatsky.”

Buck’s jaw dropped and he immediately dug out his phone. He was halfway through typing “Twin Cities Junior Pairs Champion” into the search bar before he realized it. Feeling rather sheepish, he set his phone down on the table and picked his wine back up.

“Sorry,” he said.

“It’s fine.” Eddie was smiling softly at him, eyes warm. He reached across the table and grabbed Buck’s hand. For once, it didn’t seem like Eddie was obviously - painfully - aware of where they were as he did it. “Google away. Maybe you’ll have better luck at finding pictures than we did.”

“Well you said her name was Heidi Shatsky,” Buck said, putting down the wine and picking his phone up again. He felt Eddie’s thumb rubbing circles on the back of his hand. “There can’t be too many of those around…”

Eddie was extremely lucky that Los Angeles in November was not as scorching hot as it would have been earlier in the year. Hell, if the AC in his truck had decided to crap out for the umpteenth time just a couple weeks ago, when it was in the nineties, being stuck in traffic would have had him melting into his seat. As it was, he still cussed out the drivers in front of him everytime he came to a standstill and he lost the breeze through his open window. 

He needed to get a new truck, that much was becoming very obvious to him. Unfortunately, there wasn’t really room in his budget for that big of a purchase at the moment. Once again, he really wished he knew how Danny managed all of this on his own back in El Paso.

Eddie parked in his usual spot, just outside of the station, and hopped out. He grabbed his work bag from the backseat, along with what he and Buck had codenamed the Package. He tucked it under his arm and jogged inside. Austin was the only one in the locker room when he got there, using his shoulder to keep his phone pressed to his ear as he pulled on his boots.

Usually, the man was calm and stoic in the face of anything and everything. Not to say he was an emotionless robot, but in the year and change that Eddie had known him, Austin always managed to keep his cool. This morning, though, he was clearly getting irritated with whoever he was on the phone with.

“No, I’m not going to let this go,” Austin snapped. Eddie threw him a wide-eyed look as he set the Package against the wall and started changing into his uniform. “It is not just a cold. Stop being a dumbass and-”

Austin cut himself off. Eddie looked over and saw that he now had his phone in hand and was glaring at it. Whoever he was talking to must’ve hung up on him.

“Is everything okay?”

“Oh, apparently everything’s fine.” Austin spat the word out with more vitriol than Eddie had ever heard from him. He watched the man shake his head and push himself to his feet, stomping out of the locker room without even once glancing Eddie’s way.

Buck, if he were here, would probably hunt the man down and badger him until he told him what was going on. Eddie, however, simply shrugged and continued on with his life. Because he was curious, but he wasn’t comfortable enough with the man to try and draw out his personal drama.

Once he was in his uniform, and his bag was safely packed away in his locker, he grabbed the Package from where it was leaning and carried it up the stairs to the station’s loft. Chimney was already there, warily eying a still fuming Austin, though his attention was drawn to Eddie and the Package.

“What’s that?” the paramedic asked him.

“A gift from Buck,” Eddie answered with a grin. He opened the cardboard up and saw Chimney’s own smile grow. “He found Heidi Shatsky.”

Getting it set up was easy. Finding a way to hide it so the surprise wasn’t ruined was a bit trickier. They eventually decided on just having it turned so it faced the kitchen island while Eddie used his own body to block it from view. Chimney was basically vibrating with excitement as they waited for Bobby to join them.

The captain finally climbed up the stairs at the other end of the loft shortly after their shift officially started. Everyone else had gradually gravitated their way, both in preparation for the morning brief and because they wanted to see his reaction.

“Morning, folks,” was how Bobby started, before checking the clipboard in his hand. “First up, the water heater went out last night, but they are sending someone to fix it. Until then, you might wanna brace yourselves when you hit the showers. We’re also changing the lunch schedule.”

Nothing important then, awesome. That meant Eddie was free to interrupt by dragging the cardboard cutout of Bobby Nash, junior pairs champion, out from behind him to be presented with appropriate fanfare. The man’s surprised face was comical.

“Captain Robert Nash!” Chimney announced, using his phone to record Bobby’s reaction for posterity. And also hopefully for Buck, since he wasn’t here to see it. “This is your life!”

“Oh, my gosh.” Bobby was grinning as he stared at the cardboard cutout of himself. “Where did you get that?”

“Heidi says hi,” Eddie said. “Buck said she’ll email you later, apparently she didn’t want to ruin the surprise.”

Bobby blinked at the mention of Buck’s name, his smile growing softer.

“I think we all had a little surprise this morning,” Chimney said. “Not sure what I was expecting, but it certainly wasn’t that hair.”

“Yeah, Cap, what is this look?” Eddie asked, gesturing at the glorious mullet that the kid version of Bobby sported. “Is it some kind of Travolta thing?”

“Not Travolta, Bowie,” Bobby argued. They stared at him, and then at the cardboard cutout. The captain looked vaguely insulted. “Ziggy Stardust?”

“This feels a little more like Siegfried and Roy,” Chimney told him.

Eddie kinda had to agree. The get up very much came across as less glam rock and more “watch as I dress up flamboyantly and make large predators do tricks for your amusement.” Honestly, it made him wonder about Bobby’s parents, because he could already confidently guess that they were a bit more open about things than his own.

His father would have had a coronary if Eddie even joked about wearing a shirt like the one the younger Bobby wore.

“Sorry I’m late.” Hen was rushing up the stairs to join them. “I had to take Denny to…” She came to a stop, jerking her head back as she spotted the cardboard cutout. “School. What did I miss?”

Bobby rolled his eyes.

“Okay, for everyone who isn’t fired.” The captain sent Eddie and Chimney both a mocking scowl. They smiled serenely back at him. “I will not be cooking today, we’re having a special guest chef at lunch.”

The special guest chef turned out to be Athena. Eddie wasn’t entirely certain on the details, but he knew she was suspended from duty after she went a little off the rails in the effort to solve the decades old cold case of her fiance’s death. Athena was evidently going a bit stir crazy at home, hence why she came over to cook for them.

“I still think it’s ridiculous that you got suspended for solving a murder,” Chimney said, after the food was cooked and they’d all been served, the whole bunch of them sitting around the dining table or one of the smaller ones nearby. “But I might get over that if you keep bringing us food.”

“She’s been cooking at home, too,” Bobby said, sending Athena a smile. “Every meal’s been a feast.”

“Have you ever considered being suspended forever?” Eddie asked the sergeant. Bobby gave him a flat look, making everyone laugh. “Not that your cooking isn’t good, Cap.”

“Smooth, Diaz,” Hen taunted from farther down the table.

“How’s Buckaroo settling in?” Athena asked, and then gestured over her shoulder at the kitchen, where the cardboard cutout of Bobby was watching them. “It’s nice to know he’s still bothering everyone from afar.”

“Fine. He said it’s different from here.” Eddie tried not to look at Bobby as he answered her. A difficult feat, considering the two where right next to each other. “They’re teasing him, apparently. Someone pulled up an old news clip to embarrass him with, something about Twitter questions?”

That had most of the others laughing.

“I bet I know which one,” Athena said, shaking her head as she chuckled. “Something about people being turned on by the uniform and how they wouldn’t feel that way if they saw him out of it.”

Eddie thought about how Buck looked in the uniform, and then how looked without it, and then… Well, he bit his tongue to keep from voicing his particular, rather strong, opinions on whether or not someone would be turned on by it. His thought clearly showed on his face, though, because Athena threw her head back to laugh again.

Down at the other end of the table, Hen pulled out her phone with a frown, answering it with,

“Cheryl?” A beat as she listened to whoever was on the other end, and then she dropped her fork and got up from the table, walking away from them. “No, I’m at work… I’m so sorry, I thought Karen was gonna call you…”

The others were left to look at each other, good mood having been sunk, and wonder what was going on.

Chapter End Notes

It was actually very important to me that I had Buck still be the one to mastermind Bobby's public embarrassment.

Chapter 20

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Buck knew that he’d needed to make concrete plans with Hen to see each other, now that he wasn’t at the 118. That was because, outside of work, there was nothing that really guaranteed that they’d spend time together and hang out. This wasn’t the case with Chimney. With Chimney, all Buck had to do was invite his sister and half the time she’d bring him with her.

The fact that the invitation was for dinner probably had much to do with it, because none of them were in the habit of passing up an offer of food.

“I think I’m gonna have to come over more just for your cooking, Buckaroo,” Chimney said, after the food was eaten and they were clearing the table. “It might not be as good as Bobby’s, or Athena’s, but…”

“Gee, thanks.” Buck rolled his eyes at him, though he smiled when Danny bumped against him on the way to the sink.

“Where’s Eddie?” Maddie asked, refilling her glass of wine before taking her plate to the sink. “I figured he and Austin would be here.”

“Austin’s got his own family buggin’ him to be social.” Danny turned the sink on and started the fun job of rinsing off the plates as he was handed them, while Buck was packing away the leftovers. “He’s at Cynthia’s, dealing with her pregnancy hormones.”

A confused looking Chimney shot Buck a look, an unspoken question on his face.

“His sister,” he told him, watching the other man nod. “And Eddie said that he didn’t want to crash dinner with my sister.”

“His loss,” Chimney said.

Buck rolled his eyes again.

“How’s working with Dan?” Maddie asked him.

“Not too bad. It’s fun to be the annoying younger brother again.”

“Like you ever stopped being that,” Danny shot over his shoulder.

“You’re just upset that your work friends like me more.” Buck stuck his tongue out at him, which went unnoticed. “How’s the station been without me?”

“Uh.” Chimney went wide-eyed a bit, and then looked to Maddie for help. “I don’t really know how to answer that, would you prefer I said everything was fine or if I told you it’s awful?”

“Eddie’s already told me it sucks, but that’s just because he misses me-”

“You’re constantly texting each other,” Danny said. “When does he have the time to miss you?”

“Shut up, you’re just jealous.” Buck’s phone chose that moment to buzz with a text on the coffee table. He refused to even glance Danny’s way, because they both knew who it was. “But how’s everyone else? Bobby, Hen?”

“Bobby’s fine.” Chimney said it all in one breath, like he was trying to sprint past talking about Bobby. “Hen’s… I dunno, something’s bothering her.”

“Like what?” Maddie asked.

“I don’t know, is the thing. Someone that Karen was supposed to call ended up calling her during lunch. And she was late to shift, said that she had to drop Denny off at school. Normally Karen does that. Whatever’s going on, it’s got her in a bit of a mood.” Chimney carried some plates over and sat them down at Danny’s elbow, before going back to the table. “I really wish I could help her, but she’s not talking to me about it. I’m not sure she’s talking to anybody.”

“Yeah, she’s been kinda distant whenever I talk to her, too. There’s definitely something going on with her,” Buck agreed. He glanced over at Danny, who’d barely rolled up his sleeves to wash the dishes, apparently not caring about getting them wet. And he hadn’t eaten much, especially compared to the way he was usually as much of a bottomless pit as Buck himself. So it wasn’t just Hen that was acting odd. “Hey, d’you guys think it’s the Santa Anas? They make people act weird, right?”

“They do,” Danny confirmed.

Maddie laughed at them as she picked her glass up.

“I definitely don’t think it’s the wind,” she said, earning a contemptuous snort from Danny. “Wait, you know what? Maybe it’s something personal she doesn’t feel like sharing.”

“Well, historically speaking,” Chimney said, “Hen and I tell eachother everything.”

“Wait, everything?” Maddie sounded worried. Buck looked over and met Danny’s eyes, both of them frowning. They frowned harder when she lowered her voice to ask, “Did you tell her about me?”

“No,” Chimney quickly answered. It was ruined, somewhat, by the way he followed it up with, “I mean, I would have if I felt like she was listening.”

“Oh.”

“Maddie, she’s my best friend. I need to talk to someone.”

“I- I know,” she said. “It’s just, you tell her, she tells Buck or Eddie-”

“Austin overhears,” Danny chimed in, making no effort to hide the fact that he and Buck were both eavesdropping on whatever conversation they were having.

“Maybe,” Maddie continued, ignoring him, “everything doesn’t need to be shared with your friends and coworkers.”

“But if we don’t tell each other everything,” Buck said, “what else would we do on a twenty-four hour shift?”

“Yeah.” Maddie let out a sigh. “Okay.”

“Hey, you don’t have to,” Chimney told her, but she shook her head.

“No, it’s fine. Uh, I am back in therapy,” she said. “Mandated by my boss this time.”

“Did something happen at work?” Buck worriedly asked.

“A woman filed a complaint against me, said I was stalking her,” Maddie said, and then, under her breath. “I think stalking is an overstatement.”

“But it’s true?”

“This woman called Nine-One-One, and Maddie thought she was in trouble,” Chimney said, rushing to her defense. “So she just tried to help her.”

“Yeah, but she didn’t want my help, because she thinks I’m a crazy person who invaded her privacy,” she said. “Maybe there’s a lesson there.”

“Hold on.” Danny shut off the faucet, with perhaps a bit more force than necessary, and turned to face Maddie. “This wouldn’t happen to be the same woman you told me about last month, would it? The call you thought was a DV case?”

Maddie didn’t answer, which was itself an answer. It also, Buck thought, explained some things.

“Mads.” Danny shook his head at her. “When I tried to force help on you, you told me to stay out of your marriage and then cut me off for a year. Why did you think she’d react any differently?”

“Because I thought I could get her out before it was too late!” Maddie snapped, slamming her glass down on the dining table. “I know that I messed up, okay!? I don’t need you rubbing my face in it!”

Buck, stuck standing between them, met Chimney’s gaze. The man looked about as awkward as Buck felt.

“I’m not…” Danny sighed and dragged his hand down his face. He looked tired, all of a sudden. “That’s not what I’m trying to do.”

“Whatever,” their sister huffed.

“So!” Chimney said, a bit louder than necessary in the strained silence. “Buck! How’s the One Thirty-Three?”

It wasn’t the smoothest topic change, but Buck gladly grabbed it with both hands.

“Are you okay?” Buck blurted out, an hour into their shift.

Danny paused what he was doing, looking through a drawer for a pen, and lifted his head to stare at him.

“What?”

“Are you okay?” he repeated.

He’d noticed more and more than Danny was looking off. He was still pale, with new bruises popping up. None of them were big, nor were they concerning except in the way that they shouldn’t have been appearing like they were. Buck was the one on the blood thinners, it made sense why knocking his hip against a table got him a fresh new bruise. There was no reason for Danny to end up the same way when he did it.

Plus, Buck caught him tossing back a concerning amount of tylenol that morning.

“I’m fine,” was Danny’s gruff answer. “C’mon, you’re helping me today.”

Danny pulled out a pen from the drawer he was digging through and then grabbed a clipboard, which already had a checklist stuck to it, from a wall file and headed across the bay to the engine. Buck hurried to catch up with him.

“Helping you with what?”

“This,” his brother answered, pressing the clipboard into his chest along with the pen. He grabbed one of the tool boxes as they passed it, rolling it with them. “You’ve seen Austin do the truck inspection and maintenance, right?”

“Yeah. He normally does it with-”

“With the guy who drives the ladder, I know.” Danny came to a stop on the driver’s side of the engine, just by the cab. “I normally do it with Flowers, and then I’ll help him do the ladder. But the ladder already got its weekly check-up last shift.”

“Why didn’t the engine?”

“Because Dixon’s a lazy dick,” was Danny’s immediate response. “And ever since he announced he was retiring at the start of the year, he’s gotten worse. The weekly maintenance is scheduled for the same day every week, but the last few times it was C-shift’s turn, Dixon just didn’t do it. Remember, squirt, never trust that the people before you did their jobs.”

“I feel like this is something you should tell the captain about,” Buck said, looking over the weekly checklist. Sure enough, there was a point where his brother’s initials started doubling up and one of the others - a LD - simply disappeared.

“Why?” Danny asked with a scoff. “What’s Mehta gonna do about it? Dixon’s got one foot out the door already, remember?”

“I guess…”

“We’ll do the pre-trip first, and then I’ll have Mehta shut us down so we can take her top off.”

Buck made a face at the wording. His brother could be so weird.

They walked around the engine, Danny explaining to Buck what he was doing as he did so. It reminded Buck a little of when he was a little kid and tagging along with his brother, always curious to know what he was doing. He watched as Danny yanked on the doors and slammed them closed, as he ran his hand over the tires and used a gauge to check their air, as he crouched down and stuck his head under the truck itself, as he opened every compartment and made sure the gear inside was stowed away properly. Danny told him what he was checking, what he was looking for, what to look out for. And when he deemed it fine, he told Buck to check it off.

After they made their circuit around the engine, and Danny decided everything from the tire lugnuts to the windshield wipers were acceptable, they climbed into the cab for the systems checks. Danny clambered into the driver’s seat and Buck jogged around to the other side, throwing himself into what was usually Mehta’s spot. And that was, perhaps, a mistake.

It was not the same vehicle, it was not the same cab. They looked similar, superficially, but Buck could normally point out the small, insignificant differences between the inside of the 133’s engine and that of the 118’s second ladder truck, the one they got as a replacement. The one that exploded beneath him. Buck could normally, if he was in his usual seat in the back, be able to point out the differences.

But at the moment, they might as well have been mirror images.

Danny was talking, Buck knew that, but his voice seemed as if it was at a distance, and his words weren’t quite registering in Buck’s brain. What Buck’s brain did register was the way his grip tightened on the clipboard to the point that, if he had the mental space to process it, he’d worry that he was about to break the wood. What Buck’s brain did register was the tightening in his chest as he fought to get enough air.

Any moment now, he knew, Bobby’s voice would come over the radio, and there would be heat and light, and then-

The passenger door was flung open and Buck found himself being yanked out.

“Buck!” Danny snapped, gripping at his arms.

Buck got the impression that his brother had been calling his name a lot before then.

“I’m-” His voice came out in a croak. He cleared his throat, tried again. “I’m fine.”

He saw Danny’s lips flatten into a thin line. Danny glared, clearly not believing him, and then shifted his gaze over his shoulder, through the open door and into the engine’s cab.

“Was that your first time sitting up front since…?”

Buck swallowed and nodded.

“Sorry,” he said, and drew his brother’s glare again. “It just caught me by surprise. I- We can-”

“We can take a break,” Danny said, tugging the clipboard and the pen out of his hands. Buck fought to relax his grip enough to let him do it. “I have to get Mehta to take us offline, anyway.”

“Wait, no, don’t we need to check the, uh, the sirens and the lights?”

“Eh, I know they’re working.” Danny was already marking off everything that Buck had missed. “Seriously, Buck, go get a drink or something.”

“No, no, I’m fi-”

The station alarms interrupted Buck’s lies, the voice of a dispatcher announcing details of a MVA. Danny tossed clipboard and pen on top of the tool box before he and Buck dashed for their turnouts, along with everybody else. Buck hesitated for the briefest of moments before jumping into the cab with the others, and he saw Danny’s look of concern in the rearview mirror as they pulled out.

“This is going to be a mess,” Mehta said, voice so quiet that Buck wasn’t sure it was meant to be heard over their headsets. Louder, he told Danny, “We’re heading for West Olympic and Main. One of ours struck a car. They were transporting a patient in critical condition. Buckley - Daniel - you’re gonna help transfer him.”

Their lights and sirens were indeed working, parting traffic before them like the Red Sea. When they reached the intersection, Buck found himself wishing they’d taken longer to get there. It was also the first time he wasn’t happy to see his friends.

The front of the 118’s ambulance was smashed into the driver’s side of a little blue car, its passenger side door open and pieces of paper - Buck saw it was sheet music when the wind blew one into his foot - scattered across the pavement. Beside the car, he saw Eddie and Chimney crouched down over a young woman with Bobby and Austin standing over them. In front of the 118’s engine, he saw Athena, dressed in civvies, consoling Hen, who seemed on the verge of hyperventilating.

Daniel ran past the group for the back of the 118’s ambulance, its doors already wide open, with another of the 133’s own paramedics close on his heels. They got to it just as Eddie, who’d been doing compressions on the driver, stopped his motions and shook his head at Chimney.

“What happened?” Buck asked when he reached them.

Eddie heaved out a sigh, watching Chimney make his way to Athena and a sobbing Hen, and ran his hand over his hair.

“I don’t know,” he said. “I… I don’t know.”

It was with much relief that they reached the end of their shift the following morning. After they got the original patient, with two major arterial bleeds, transferred to their own bus and rushed off to the hospital, the 133 had very little to do. The second patient, the driver of the car that the ambulance rammed into, was declared shortly after they showed up, and the investigation meant they weren’t even responsible for any of the clean-up.

One of the chiefs showed up not long after they did, along with several other of the administration types, and took control of the scene. The 118 was taken offline for pretty much the rest of the day, since they were giving statements to the brass and the police, both. Which meant that the surrounding stations, the 133 included, ended up picking up the slack.

Daniel never did get around to doing the weekly maintenance check. There just wasn’t time to go offline so he could flush the water pumps or lift the cab up to check the engine’s engine. His counterpart on the incoming shift was going to throw a fit over it, undoubtedly, but there wasn’t much that Daniel could do about that. Really, it was all Dixon’s fault.

More annoying was the way the others kept coming to him and Buck for information, because they all knew they had connections to the 118. Not that either of them had much information to share, even with the way Buck was attached to his phone at every available minute, talking to Eddie and to Chimney, trying to check in on Hen.

He’d asked for the number of Daniel’s lawyer ex, Attica Rose, at one point, to give to Chimney so he could pass it on to Hen. Daniel hadn’t the heart to tell him that the department, and the union, would no doubt be fielding a lawyer of their own for her.

Now, though, they were at the end of their shift, the morning after an exhausting night and a long day. The locker was filled with those coming in and changing into their uniforms, and those about to leave who were changing into their civvies. Daniel had just pulled his uniform shirt over his head, bundling it up without much care to stuff into his work bag, when he heard Buck behind him draw in a sharp breath and ask,

“What is that?”

“What’s what?” Daniel asked, turning around slightly. 

Buck was only half-dressed himself, his own t-shirt dangling from his hand. With his other hand, he reached out and poked at Daniel’s side.

“That,” he said.

Daniel looked down, and felt his own breath catch in his throat. All over his torso and down his arms were little red dots. Hauntingly, horrifyingly familiar red dots.

“I- I don’t know,” he told Buck, hurriedly pulling out his own t-shirt and tugging it on. “A rash, it’s just a rash.”

He ignored the look his baby brother gave him, and the way the others around them glanced at him curiously, and hurried to finish changing. It was on his legs, too, when he changed pants. Daniel was torn between the inability to look away from them and the desire to look anywhere else.

The drive home was hell on Daniel’s nerves, and he was pretty sure he frightened his baby brother a little with the way he screamed at a particularly boneheaded driver. He felt bad for it, because he never wanted to scare Buck, but he was trying very hard not to have a full blown freak-out where he could see it.

“I’m going to go lay down,” Daniel said when they got to the apartment, and he was already closing his bedroom door behind him by the time he heard Buck acknowledge it. His work bag was dropped to the floor and he sat down on his bed in the dark, not even bothering to turn on a light.

The headache, the fever, the chill, the aches, the bruises, all of that Daniel could - and happily did - ignore. All of that was fine, it was nothing, it meant nothing. A cold, nothing to be worried about. But this? He remembered this. Not on himself, but he’d seen it on others.

There really was a point where he simply couldn’t keep lying to himself, wasn’t there?

Pulling out his phone, and looking up the number he needed, he made the first call, sitting through the hold music and doing his best not to snap at some poor lady as she scheduled him in. The second call was, in a way, much harder.

“Hey, Maddie, could you um… Are you free, Tuesday morning? I really need you to go somewhere with me.”

Chapter End Notes

This seems like a good point in the fic to tap the sign and remind everyone that I Do Not have a medical degree.

Chapter 21

Chapter Notes

I enjoyed writing this one. :)

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Waiting rooms all looked the same, Daniel decided. It didn’t matter which hospital or which ward or which department, they all seemed to have the exact same uncomfortable chairs and the exact same cheap looking tables and the exact same months out-of-date magazines. Stick him in a waiting room at Los Angeles General or at the Boston Medical Center, he wouldn’t know the difference.

Hell was probably a hospital waiting room.

For all that he liked to tease and joke about Buck’s inability to stay still and be quiet, even to save his own life, Daniel knew he wasn’t much better. Oh sure, he didn’t have that constant thrumming need beneath his skin to move, to go and keep going, that Buck seemed to have, but patience had never been a strength of his. It was the cause of so many of his mistakes: the inability to hold himself back, to make himself wait just a little bit longer. The need to do something then and there. He and Buck were men of action, being forced to stand by and do nothing was anathema to them both.

Austin wasn’t like that. Austin was perfectly content with quietly sitting beside him, to watch, to observe. A statue in a chair, practically part of the scenery. Daniel wished he could have an ounce of that control. Instead, he was no doubt driving both him and Maddie spare by the way he was constantly bouncing his leg. It was better than giving in to the urge to get up and pace.

What he needed was a distraction. Maybe he could start an argument with his sister, poke her into furious indignation. Maybe he could goad Austin into ranting to him about whatever piece of drivel he was reading in that gossip rag that had his eyebrow twitching. Maybe-

His phone chimed and he hurried to dig it out of his pocket. When he saw what it was, he found himself managing an actual smile.

“What?” Maddie, just as curious as her brothers, leaned over to stick her nose in his business. “Did Buck send you something?”

“Why do you think it’s from him?” Daniel asked her, opening up the file he was just sent.

“Because Austin and I are sitting right here. And as far as I know, you don’t have any other friends.”

Austin didn’t even bother hiding the snort that comment inspired, and then he was setting down the magazine he was reading and nudging Daniel in the side.

“I’m heading to the bathroom,” he stated, getting to his feet. “I’ll be quick.”

“It was nice of him to come with you,” Maddie stated as they watched him go.

“Yeah,” Daniel agreed. “I didn’t even ask him to, he just… Did it.”

And he didn’t once hold it over Daniel’s head after he told him about the doctor’s appointment. Just asked for the when and the where, and then promised to be there.

Daniel could feel his sister’s gaze on him. When he looked, her expression was soft. Too soft to be directed his way in this context. He truly did not want to know what she was thinking in that moment, and he needed to cut her off before she could say anything to ruin his blissful ignorance.

“Michael sent me May’s college essay,” he told her, angling his phone so she could see the screen. “It’s titled, “Living With the Enemy.” It’s about Athena.”

“Wait.” That soft expression was replaced with scrunched-up confusion. “Michael Grant?”

“Yeah.”

“Why is Michael sending you May’s college essay?”

“‘Cause we bonded over how all cops are bastards.”

“But… What?” Maddie shook her head, looking remarkably like Buck when the two of them were teaching him about change. They were both about ready to slam their heads into a table by the time they managed to get the kid to understand that nickels were not worth more than dimes. “I thought you were friends with Athena. Or, friendly.”

“She abused her authority to get revenge on her daughter’s bully,” Daniel pointed out. “It’s a good example of how cops suck, but it’s also why I like her. Ironic.”

“Why don’t you like cops?”

“Reasons.”

That answer was very obviously not good enough for Daniel’s sister, but it was also the best she was gonna get from him. He refocused his attention to May’s essay, smirking at some of what she had to say. He did like Athena, but she was still a cop. And there was absolutely no way she’d like what her daughter had to write about her..

Unfortunately, the essay was short - only six-hundred words, or thereabouts - which meant it didn’t take very long for Daniel to read it. It was a nice distraction from the reason for the appointment, but he was soon left right back where he was before he got the email. His foot started bouncing again almost as soon as he dropped his phone in his lap.

“Hey.” Maddie reached over and placed her hand on her leg. “Dan, it’ll be okay.”

Daniel swallowed.

“You don’t know that,” he said.

“Yes, I do. Even if… Even if the cancer-” The way she spoke it, the word might as well have been barbed. It certainly felt that way to Daniel. “-is back, it doesn’t mean… Buck can just fix it, right? Like when we were kids. It’ll be fine.”

Oh, God. She didn’t know. She didn’t know.

“Mads,” he said, turning his head to look at her. “Buck can’t donate.”

“The blood thinners, I know. But they can just take him off them for a week and-”

“No, Maddie, he…” Daniel shook his head. “The embolism, remember? He had a DVT, and they still aren’t certain what caused it. He can’t, Maddie.”

It was like watching glass crack in slow motion. Comprehension spread across Maddie’s face, followed by horror. Her eyes went wide, her hand went to her mouth. He turned in the uncomfortable seat as much as he could to face her fully.

“You can’t tell Buck about this,” Daniel said. Begged, really. “You can’t tell him I had a doctor’s appointment, or why. Please.”

“What?” Maddie’s horror was forgotten in favor of confused outrage. “Dan, you can’t hide cancer. He’s going to notice.”

“We don’t know if it’s cancer yet.” He was grasping that little straw of hope with all his might. “He was a mess after the embolism, Maddie. It destroyed him when he realized he couldn’t donate anymore. I don’t know what he’ll do when he finds out that I might be sick again.”

Maybe it should have, Buck had said, when Eddie tried to tell him how lucky he was that the embolism hadn’t killed him. Those quiet, half-whispered words spoken so many weeks ago still haunted Daniel. A nice new link in the long chain of moments where he was faced with the irrefutable evidence of how he failed his baby brother, time and time again. Yet another line filled in on the laundry list of reasons for why their parents - their mother - would only get near Buck again over Daniel’s dead body.

(Maybe that chance would come sooner than they all feared.)

“Dan, we can’t just not tell him,” Maddie argued. “You live with him, you work with him. He’ll notice when you start treatments. We can’t hide this from him.”

“I told you, we don’t know if it’s cancer yet. That’s why I’m here, right? So they can do all the tests and I can wait forever to find out.” As much as Daniel would like for them to be able to just input information into the computer and have the answer pop up immediately after, he knew that wasn’t how it worked. Not even after a couple decades of advances. “If we told him now, he’ll be worrying for nothing.”

“And if the tests come back and…” Maddie trailed off, unable to voice the possibility. “What then, Dan?”

“If it is cancer, then- then we tell him. But not a moment before.”

If it was cancer, he told himself. If, if, if. He was clinging to that if with all that he had, because the alternative was just…

“Maddie.” Daniel’s voice cracked. “I’m scared.”

His sister didn’t say anything as she wrapped her arms around him and pulled him close, or as close as she could with the armrest between them that pressed into his ribs. Daniel let his head fall onto her shoulder and he closed his eyes, trying to keep his breathing even and not fall into the panic that had threatened to consume him ever since he saw the little red dots all over his skin. Austin, when he came back, was just as silent as ever, his return announced by his hand falling to rest between Daniel’s shoulder blades.

“Buckley, Daniel.”

The sound of his name had Daniel jolting in his seat. There was a nurse standing by the door leading to the exam rooms, in pink scrubs and already looking exhausted despite the early hour. Or perhaps because of the early hour.

He drew in a trembling breath, let it out, and got to his feet.

When Buck was dating Abby, when they’d moved past conversations on the phone to him actually spending the night in her bed, it hadn’t taken long for him to practically move into the place. His spare uniforms ended up in her closet, his hair products in her medicine cabinet. Buck hadn’t meant for it to happen, it just sorta had. It had happened at Maddie’s, too, during that short period of time he stayed with her after everything with Doug, when he was avoiding Danny because he was still so angry with his big brother for keeping him in the dark. A few days on her couch, and suddenly he was spreading out in all directions like an ooze.

He hadn’t done that yet with Eddie. He had a toothbrush over there, and some of his clothes had inevitably ended up in the laundry, but he still mostly left with as much as he came with. If he thought about it too hard, he’d probably come up with an uncomfortable reason for why he wasn’t taking over his boyfriend’s home the way he did his ex’s and his sister’s. 

Buck didn’t want to think about it too hard.

Anyways, the point was that he wasn’t living at Eddie’s, even if he did spend quite a few nights there. Which meant the other nights he didn’t work, like this night, he came back to the apartment. A decision motivated by the fact that Danny had gone radio silent. All of Buck’s texts since some time that morning had gone unanswered, which was… Well, it was very damn concerning, that’s what it was.

It was very dark, and very quiet, when he opened the door. Danny was home, Buck knew that much - his car was in the designated spot. Maybe he was in bed? A bit early for him, but the way Danny was looking more and more worn down was not lost on Buck. His brother claimed he was fine when he asked, but he wasn’t certain if he believed him.

He turned on the light, and was startled when he saw Danny sitting on the couch.

“Jesus.” Buck was tempted to throw his shoe at him. “Why were you sitting in the dark?”

“Didn’t feel like getting up,” was his brother’s quiet answer. “I didn’t even realize it was so late.”

“Where’s your phone?” was Buck’s next question as he walked over. “I texted, you never replied.”

“Sorry, it’s…” Danny flapped a hand in the direction of the coffee table. “It’s over there.”

Buck looked and spotted the phone sitting right there within reach. The screen, when he turned it on, showed that it was nearly dead along with all the notifications for unread texts. Most of them were from him, but a couple were from Austin and there was one from Maddie.

“So you just ignored me.”

“Sorry. Didn’t mean to.”

Alright, now Buck’s worry went up a notch. Several notches, actually. Danny didn’t just ignore him, not for any reason. In the past, several of Danny’s dates had been ruined because of that fact.

“Danny, what’s going on?” Buck asked him, kneeling in front of him and handing him his phone.

His brother shrugged, dropping his phone into his lap.

“Like I said, I lost track of time.” Danny let out a sigh and then reached up to cup Buck’s face. “You’re a good kid, y’know that?”

“Um.” Buck didn’t really know how to respond to that. “Thanks?”

“I’m serious,” his brother continued. “You were the best kid in the world.”

“You have to say that.”

“No, I really don’t,” Danny told him. He moved his hand up, his thumb moving to stroke at Buck’s birthmark. “I thought this was the coolest thing, the first time I saw you. Mom always said they’d probably fade away, but I’d always hoped they wouldn’t.”

Sometimes, when he was growing up, Buck wished that they had. They were another mark of how he was different from all the other kids, in ways both tangible and unquantifiable. Even if it wasn’t a reason for kids to bully him over, before Danny descended upon them like a wrathful angel, it still earned him questions and comments from people whose assumptions were never the correct answer. He’d stopped keeping track of how many times someone thought it was a bruise. Or worse, some sort of eye herpes, just like that brat from school.

“Y’know, I have made so many mistakes,” Danny continued, dropping his hand to Buck’s shoulder. “But none of them were a waste. Every choice I made was worth it, because you are right here. And I would do it all over again if I had to.”

“Danny?”

“Or- Or maybe not all of it. I’d do some of them better. I hope I’d do them better,” his brother said. “You weren’t- You weren’t born to be useful. You don’t have to be useful, you don’t have to be anything except yourself. But that’s enough, okay? Being you is enough, you’re enough. I love you for you, and I don’t need you to be anything else. Okay?”

“O- Okay.” Buck was so very confused, and so very worried, and maybe a little bit frightened now. “Danny, what’s going on?”

He watched Danny’s throat bob as he swallowed, and then his brother opened his mouth like he was about to say something. Then, instead, he just shook his head. Before Buck had the chance to press, to demand that he tell him what was going on, Danny’s phone rang in his lap. Maddie’s face and name flashed on the screen.

“Yeah?” was how Danny answered it, voice coming out hoarse. Every bit of earlier vulnerability slid away, his face turning serious at whatever their sister was telling him.  “We’ll be right there.”

“Right there” turned out to be at an ER waiting room. Buck caught Danny making a face as they walked in, before they both spotted their sister, standing in the middle of everything, and rushed over. She seemed okay, except for how upset she was. That, and the bits of blood on the cuffs of her jacket.

“What happened?” Buck asked, because Danny continued to prove himself a less than reliable source of information on the drive over.

“Tara- The, uh, the woman that… The woman who called Nine-One-One, the one that accused me of stalking her.” Maddie didn’t meet either of their eyes as she spoke. “She called me, asked me for help. I went over there to find that she’d shot her husband.”

“Because he was…” Danny trailed off, but Maddie nodded all the same.

“That’s what she said. That they were fighting, he was angry, he went after her. She’s gonna end up with this massive black eye.” She took a breath, let it out. “I got there to find that she’d shot him. He was just laying there, bleeding out. I had to stick my finger into his chest to keep his lung from collapsing.”

She stared at her hand, at her fingers on that hand, before she grimaced and curled them into a fist. Buck glanced at his brother, and then he went to a nearby coffee machine to get her a cup while Danny didn’t quite manhandle her into sitting down.

“A finger thoracostomy is kinda badass,” Buck told her when he came back. She took the drink, but she didn’t say anything. “C’mon, you saved a guy’s life, someone who seriously did not deserve it.”

“No,” she snapped. “Nobody deserves to die.”

Judging from the expression on Danny’s face, their brother did not quite agree with that sentiment. Buck didn’t think he did, either. Not entirely. He was all for dancing on Doug’s grave, because that bastard absolutely deserved it for what he did to his sister. Hell, he deserved worse.

“Ms. Kendall.” That had all three of them grimacing, even as Maddie stood up once more to face the cop that was heading their way. His tag said his name was Williams, but Buck didn’t recognize him right off. “I’m sorry to keep you here so long, but you can head home now.”

“Wait,” Maddie said. “Don’t you want to take my statement?”

“Detectives talked to Mr. d’Agostino before he went into surgery,” Officer Williams said. “He told them he shot himself by accident.”

“By accident?”

“Yeah, that’s what I thought, too.” The officer’s tone made it clear he didn’t believe the statement for a second. “You folks have a good night.”

He left them all feeling flabbergasted. Or maybe that was just Buck, because this evening had just been one confusing thing after another.

“I don’t get it,” he said. “She shot him. Why would he lie for her?”

“Maddie!” A woman came rushing over from the entrance to the ER proper. She had a busted lip, a bruise around her eye, a gauze stuck to her temple. There was blood staining her sweater. “I’m so glad you’re still here, I wanted to thank you. Thank you so much.”

“Tara-” Buck had already guessed that, but it was nice to hear Maddie confirm it. “-what’s going on? The officer just said-”

“He said he could forgive me if I would forgive him,” Tara said, a statement so monumentally absurd sounding to Buck that he wasn’t certain he actually understood a word of it. “If- If we could make things work.”

“What?” Maddie was shaking her head in denial. “No. Tara, no.”

“It can be different now,” Tara said. “We can be different, he promised.”

She gave Maddie, as well as Buck and Danny, a shaky smile. Then she was leaving them, returning to the man who left those bruises on her and who she had shot in return. This all seemed kind of… Empty, to Buck.

“Do… Do you want to stick around?” he asked Maddie, though he was still focused on the door that Tara just went through. “In case she needs you?”

“No,” she said. “I can’t help her anymore.”

“I don’t understand,” he admitted. 

Why would she go back to the guy? (Why would Maddie stay with Doug for so long?) Why wouldn’t she take Maddie’s help? (Why didn’t Maddie take Danny’s?)

“That’s a good thing,” Danny told him. 

When Buck tore his gaze away from the ER entrance and looked at them, he saw that his brother had drawn a devastated looking Maddie into his arms, her head tucked under his chin. Danny didn’t seem surprised, though, or confused about what just happened. Just maybe quietly disappointed.

Buck got the impression that he hadn’t really expected it to go any other way.

Chapter End Notes

I procrastinated and put together a pair of playlists for Daniel and Austin with songs that they would listen to here.

(Sometimes I post things on Tumblr under the "series: born for adversity" tag that don't make it over here, but which you might be interested in.)

Chapter 22

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“So she shoots him and then takes him back?” Eddie asked, making no effort to conceal his low opinion of that. He and Chris had come over for pizza and video games, and now he was dumping plates into the sink. “And I thought my relationships were complicated.”

Buck could only agree with him. He was still trying to wrap his head around what happened yesterday. All of what happened yesterday, the whole general weirdness of events that followed each other one after another.

“How’s Maddie taking it?”

“Uh, it’s kind of rough on her,” Buck told her, pulling out a pair of beers from the fridge . After a thought, he grabbed one for Danny, too, even though his brother hadn’t asked for one. “I think she thought she could save Tara from Vincent, but, y’know, she’s realizing you can’t save someone from themselves. Not if they don’t want it.”

“Ain’t that the truth?” Eddie shook his head, picking up the drink that Buck opened up and sat down on the kitchen table for him.

It was a lesson that Buck learned his first year as a firefighter, with Devon and the rollercoaster. It was a lesson that Danny learned in this exact context years ago, not that Buck knew about it until relatively recently. When he first realized that his brother knew about Doug before Maddie came back into their lives, that he’d known for years and hadn’t told him about it, Buck had been livid. They’d talked it out, though, and time did the rest. He had a bit of a better understanding, now, of just how much of a bad spot Danny was in.

Buck still wished that he’d told him about it earlier, though.

Currently, Danny was sitting on the couch with Chris, the pair of them jostling each other as they played something delightfully violent. Buck was fairly certain that Danny was actually letting the kid win, which was just offensive. Danny never once simply let him win anything. All of Buck’s video game victories against his brother, few as they were, were hard-earned.

“I’m worried about Danny,” Buck said in a hush, rolling his (still unopened) bottle between his hands.

“Yeah, he is looking a bit…” Eddie glanced over at the pair on the couch. “Worn.”

“No. I mean, yes. I mean-” Buck cut himself off with a sigh. “It's not just how bad he’s been looking. He was acting weird yesterday, before Maddie called us.”

“Weird, how?”

“Weird like…” He chewed on his lip. “You remember how I complained that he wasn’t answering his phone? I found him sitting in the dark, Eds. His phone was on the table, he just… Didn’t pay attention to it for some reason.”

“Maybe he was just tired?”

“Maybe.” Buck let out a sigh and set his drink on the table beside the one for Danny, ran a hand down his face. “But he was kinda talking weird, too. The things he said, it was kinda like…”

“Like?” Eddie prompted when he trailed off.

Like Danny was saying the things he wanted to say before he couldn’t anymore.

“Just, weird,” Buck said instead. It was probably time to change the topic. “Any luck with finding a new me, yet?”

Eddie made a face.

“There’s not going to be a new you,” he said with a shake of his head. “I mean, it was already an adjustment over the summer. Everyone’s fine, the floaters and all, it just isn’t as easy to mesh with them as it was with you.”

“Aw, are you saying I’m special?” Buck teased.

“You are very special,” Eddie agreed with a laugh, leaning in.

He tasted like the beer he’d been drinking, sharp and bitter, and like the pizza they’d eaten. The world narrowed until it was only the two of them, Eddie’s free hand clutching at his hip and both of Buck’s gripping Eddie’s shirt. It was easy to forget that anything else existed.

At least until their peaceful little bubble was ruthlessly popped.

“Really?” Danny said, his voice tearing them apart as easily as if he’d crossed the room to do so. “There are innocent eyes here.”

Buck looked over and saw that his brother had a hand over Chris’ eyes, the kid giggling like a loon. He made a face at them both, though only Danny got to see it, and picked up the two unopened bottles from the kitchen table.

“What about my innocent eyes, huh?” Buck asked, handing Danny his drink. “I had to watch you and Austin make-out, remember that?”

“We didn’t make out,” Danny said with a roll of his eyes, uncovering Chris’ so he could open his drink. “Chris, wanna see me beat your dad at this?”

“I dunno,” the kid said, frowning at him. “You kinda suck.”

Danny’s insulted expression was comical.

Maybe, Buck thought on reflection, Danny wasn’t simply letting Chris win. Not entirely, at least. Because he distinctly remembered his brother being much better at this game, and yet neither he nor Eddie were getting their asses handed to them as one-sidedly as he would expect. In fact, Buck actually witnessed his boyfriend win multiple rounds in a row, something that felt like a herald of the apocalypse.

It was another thing to be worried about, maybe. Another piece of evidence, to go along with the bruises, the lack of appetite, the concerning dosage of tylenol, the general unwell appearance, and the weird - upsetting, frightening - conversation from the day before. Buck felt like he was putting together a murderboard in his head, and he didn’t think he was going to like whatever conclusion they led him to.

Much to Chris’s disappointment and displeasure, tomorrow was a school day and he hadn’t brought anything he’d need in the morning, so neither Diaz could stick around for much longer. Buck walked them down to Eddie’s truck, with its still busted AC, the kid complaining the entire time. The goodbyes lasted a bit longer than it absolutely needed, which prompted yet more complaining from the child who did not appreciate being stuck in the car while his father and his father’s boyfriend made out.

Honestly, Buck couldn’t really blame him for it.

When he got back up to the apartment, Danny wasn’t in the living room. The empty beer bottles had been tossed into the recycling, and both the television and the game system were turned off. It wasn’t that difficult to hunt him down, though, since the only two options were the bathroom or his bedroom, and the bathroom door was wide open with nobody inside.

The door to Danny’s room also wasn’t closed, though it was only ajar. Still, Buck took this as, if not quite an invitation, then an excuse to get away with not knocking. He pushed it open enough to stick his head in, spotting Danny sprawled out on his back over the covers. The light was on, and he hadn't changed out of what he’d been wearing.

He looked exhausted, too. Far more exhausted than he should have for just a few rounds of pixelated violence. Yet another thing to add to the mental murderboard.

“Danny?”

Danny let out a groan but he didn’t sit up. He did turn his head a little and open an eye to look at him.

“What’s up, squirt? Finished saying goodbye to Eddie and the kid?”

“Yeah,” Buck said, and then, “Are you okay?”

His brother let out a snort and then covered his face with his hands.

“I’m… I’ll be fine,” he claimed, voice muffled.

Buck scowled at him, at his choice of wording, at the obvious way he was toeing the line of lying.

“I thought you promised that you’d do better with the whole telling me about things thing, Danny.”

“Buck,” his brother groaned, dropping his arms to his sides. “I- I will, I promise. Just… Not now, okay?”

Danny was looking rather upset, Buck noticed, and kinda pathetic in the way people did when they were particularly unwell. It drained whatever anger he felt in the moment, and he gave his brother a nod before stepping out. Something was wrong, that much was very obvious, and there wasn’t much he could do if Danny wasn’t going to tell him what was going on.

Whenever Danny got sick as a kid, their mother turned into a monster, hovering and fretting and playing nursemaid. Chicken noodle soup, medicine, trips to the doctor over the simplest of sniffles. By the time Buck was old enough to actually remember, Danny’s patience for it was practically nonexistent and he was constantly annoyed with her. It got worse when they moved to El Paso, where Danny turned into the type to power through an illness.

He’d come down with the flu once, during Buck’s senior year, and it was a group effort to bully him into bed rest. Danny bitched and complained the entire time, even as he dutifully ate Tia Alma’s sopa de fideo. At the moment, though, Buck wasn’t certain how well those two options would work here, not when he didn’t actually know what was going on. He still wanted - needed - to do something to make his brother better.

Danny hadn’t moved much when Buck crept back into the bedroom, except he was now on his side. He barely reacted when Buck eased himself onto the bed beside him, sitting up against the headboard and opening up the well-worn book.

“Alice was beginning to get very tired-” he started, only to be interrupted by his brother bursting out with laughter.

“Seriously?” Danny asked, rolling over to face him.

“Shut up, I’m reading,” Buck sniped. He cleared his throat and started again. “Alice was beginning to get very tired of sitting by her sister on the bank, and of having nothing to do…”

Eddie hadn’t been lying to Buck when he complained about finding someone new to watch his six.
