In their line of work it was a necessity, but sometimes a bad partner was more dangerous than no partner at all. Worse, the station was pulled a bit thin since Hen, despite being cleared of fault in her accident, still hadn’t come back. She and Karen were away at a retreat, so they were two people down for the moment.

Luckily, this shift had been a rather slow one for most of the day. Unluckily, it seemed that was about to change.

In the early evening, they were called out to a traffic accident involving two trucks colliding into each other in the Second Street tunnel. Already a bad spot, it was inevitably bound to get worse. Especially with the pinched, worried expression that grew on Bobby’s face the entire ride there as he got more information from dispatch.

Austin parked them just short of the tunnel entrance, giving them more than enough room to maneuver and set up triage if need be. The tunnel was filled with smoke, which made it difficult to see much of anything from the outside. Eddie assumed, though, that most of the vehicles that weren’t involved in the accident either drove on through to the other side or turned around. 

“Dispatch said two victims and a hazardous materials recycling truck,” Bobby briefed them after they climbed out of the trucks. Eddie’s stomach clench at the news, because there was no telling what the truck was carrying. “I want you to grab the CO2 detection equipment, and I want everybody in full turnouts and BAs.”

They all did as they were ordered, and soon they were marching as a group into the tunnel. The two trucks weren’t that deep inside, the front end of one sprouting flames. The impact of it rear ending the hazardous materials recycling truck had peeled away the siding of the trailer, revealing a fire that burn such a bright white that Eddie couldn’t stare directly at it.

“Cap, that’s magnesium, bet my money on it,” he advised, voice slightly muffled by his mask.

“Alright. Chim, you’re on him.” Bobby pointed at one victim, dressed in tan overalls, laying on the pavement. “Eddie, check the driver.”

Eddie nodded and jogged to the cab of the big rig while Bobby ordered others to keep people out of the tunnel. He pulled open the driver’s door, revealing an unconscious and bloodied man dressed in the same overalls as the other victim.

“Cap, he’s pinned,” he told Bobby. The crash had driven the steering wheel into the man’s legs, leaving no wiggle wound. “I gotta go to the other side.”

He was already jogging around to the passenger door when Bobby acknowledged him before requesting a liquid nitrogen truck from dispatch and then for Austin to bring a saw. Through the still open door he could see Chimney and the others get the second victim loaded up on a gurney and wheeled away. 

The driver would not be moving anytime soon, at least not without effort.

“What are we looking at?” Bobby asked.

“He’s not impaled,” Eddie reported, which was the end of the good news. “We’re gonna need a jackhammer.”

“This magnesium’s starting to burn real hot.” The captain stepped out of Austin’s way when he arrived with the saw. “We’ve gotta get him out of here soon.”

“The saw’s not gonna cut it,” Eddie argued, giving the steering wheel a tug as Austin set the blade to the truck.

“Ah, God!” the driver cried out, waking up. “Oh, my God.”

“Sir, try to remain calm,” Bobby urged. “Tell me your name.”

“Ernest,” the driver said, and then, “Oh, the magnesium’s burning.”

“Sir, it’s okay. We’re working to get you out now.”

“No, no, that’s not the only thing in there.”

“What else is back there, Ernest?”

“Cobalt-60.”

The saw stopped as Austin whipped his head up to stare at the driver. His eyes were wide behind his mask.

“You were transporting radioactive waste through the middle of downtown Los Angeles?” Bobby asked, tone a mixture of incredulous and angry.

“Company’s cutting corners,” was the driver’s weak reply.

Bobby grabbed his radio and thumbed it on. It crackled to life.

“This is Captain Nash. Be advised, this truck contains cobalt-60. Start evacuating this area in a three-block radius and order all nonessential personnel outside the perimeter now.”

“That magnesium’s gonna burn through those drums,” Eddie told him, heart racing in his chest. “Once that happens, this entire tunnel turns into Chernobyl.”

“Hazmat and nitrogen will be here any minute,” Bobby replied. “I need both of you out there when they land.”

“Copy that,” Austin said, already hauling ass to the tunnel entrance.

“I’ll stay here with Ernest,” Bobby told Eddie, who hadn’t quite moved as quickly as the other. “Get him out.”

“Fine,” Eddie said, a bit reluctantly.

He crawled backwards out of the truck cab and jogged out of the tunnel. He found Chimney standing by the other victim, another paramedic doing compressions.

“Any word on the hazmat and nitro?” Eddie asked him, tugging off his mask.

“Delayed,” Chimney answered. Eddie grimaced at the news. “Too many cars trying to evacuate downtown.”

With an agitated sigh, Eddie thumbed on his radio.

“Cap, hazmat and nitro are delayed. What’s your status?”

There was a beat of silence and then,

“The cobalt’s ignited,” Bobby said. “We’re at gamma radiation level, hold your positions.”

There wasn’t much Eddie could do from out here, except be thankful that Buck wasn’t with them. His boyfriend would, doubtlessly, rush in regardless of orders. Eddie wasn’t about to, not with the way the army had drilled the dangers of radioactive fallout into his head. 

Thankfully, hazmat and the nitrogen truck arrived after not much longer. Some of them rushed in to put the magnesium out, others started setting up a decontamination tent similar to the ones they broke out during the bank robbery, back in the spring. The driver, Ernest, was soon being wheeled out with Bobby not far behind.

“Smoke’s clear,” Eddie told Chimney. The paramedic was perched on the side of the engine, the first vic loaded up in the bus and ready to be taken away. “Fallout’s gonna be minimal.”

“Maybe not for all of us,” Chimney said, with a significant glance at where Bobby had just been hurried into the tent. He slapped his finger onto his nose. “Not it for telling Buck.”

Eddie let out an aggrieved sigh.

“So one of the firefighters that responded to the Chernobyl fire,” Buck said, looking at his phone as he dropped his work bag just inside the door. “The radiation caused his eyes to actually change color, from brown to blue.”

“You’re not leaving that there,” Danny told him on his way past.

Buck stuck his tongue out at him and moved the bag farther against the wall with his foot.

“And!” he continued, undeterred,. “You can actually book a trip there. They even offer day trips to Prypiat, which is still covered in plutonium.”

“Isn’t there a horror movie about that? Came out a few years ago?”

“Um, yeah. I couldn’t really get through it.”

“Why?” Danny grinned at him, wan looking though it was. “Too scary for you?”

“No,” Buck scoffed. “One of the actresses reminded me too much of Shannon.”

“Ah.” His brother nodded in understanding. “So, any other fun radiation related facts?”

It was what Buck had been doing ever since Eddie told him what happened, after the brief flare of panic for Bobby. He knew he’d annoyed some of the others at the 133 with his reciting of trivia as he read it, there was no way he hadn’t, but none of them said anything about it. Flowers - Florian - who drove the ladder truck even shared some of what he knew from his grandfather, who apparently used to work at a gaseous diffusion plant in Ohio.

Eddie at the moment was suffering in the same way Danny used to, the text thread between them a repository for the knowledge that Buck decided was too cool or interesting or scary to not share. Like the bit about the eye color changing, that had gotten sent to him with an additional, “holy shit???” tacked onto it.

“Lessee,” Buck said, scrolling through the page. He hopped on one foot to undo the laces of his shoe, kicking it off and doing the same with the other. “The other three reactors were kept in use for thirteen years afterwards.”

“What I’m hearing is that they didn’t have a union.”

“Or OSHA,” he said. “Apparently there were a couple incidents before the big one, and then a fire in the nineties.”

A thought struck Buck and he finally raised his head to look at his brother, who was in the kitchen rummaging through their fridge.

“Hey, do you remember the Chernobyl disaster?”

Danny slowly pulled his head out to fix him with an incredulous stare.

“I was a year old.”

“Oh, right.” Woops. “Maybe Bobby does, I can ask him.”

Buck already sent multiple texts to him and Athena to check in on his former captain. They’d mostly gone unreplied to, except for Athena’s brief mention of the tests looking good and there being more tests in a week. 

“I don’t think Nash would be in the mood to go down that particular memory lane, squirt,” Danny pointed out, closing the fridge door without pulling anything out. “Not at the moment, anyways.”

“Right, right. You’re right.” Buck nodded, slipping his phone into his pocket. “Hey, I can-”

He was about to offer to make them both breakfast, since he was positive Danny hadn’t eaten anything since the night before. And that wasn’t much. His brother’s phone ringing interrupted that plan, however. He watched Danny pull it out, saw his eyes go wide and what looked like (but certainly could not have been) fear flash across his face.

Danny turned around, back to Buck, and answered it with a gruff,

“Hello?... Yeah, this is him.”

The phone call was mostly one-sided. Buck crept closer in the hopes to hear something beyond his brother’s repeated “yeah” and “okay.” It ended with a rough sounding “thank you” and then Danny sat the phone down on the counter.

And then his shoulders began to shake.

“Danny?” Buck gave up the creeping and rushed to him. “Danny, what’s wrong!? What’s- Are you laughing?”

“Yeah, I- Sorry,” Danny said between laughs and gasps for breath. He was laughing so hard that he was crying with it. ‘“I’m fine, I’m fine, I’m-”

The laughter overtook him again and he slid down the counter to sit on the floor. Buck knelt beside him, completely lost as to what was so funny.

“Who was that?” he asked.

“That was-That was my doctor,” Danny managed to answer, wiping at his face. “Everything’s fine, I just have mono.”

Buck couldn’t help the way he scooted away from him, even as his brother laughed louder and harder.

Chapter End Notes

Here's a fun piece of medical literature that I've had open in my tabs for uh. More than two months now:
"Epstein–Barr Virus Infection Masquerading as Acute Leukemia: A Report of Two Cases and Review of Literature"

Chapter 23

For once in his life, Daniel was not feeling miserable in a doctor’s office. Or- Alright, so he was feeling very damn miserable, physically speaking. But he’d also been feeling rather buoyant since the phone call the other morning, since the revelation that he wasn’t stuck in one of his worst nightmares.

He knew he’d frightened Buck with his reaction, but he couldn’t have helped it if he tried. It was a moment where he could laugh, or he could cry, and he ended up doing both. It was just so ridiculous. There he was, preparing himself for the worst news, only to hear that he had mono of all things. He’d joked about it being AIDS before, though Austin hadn’t found it at all funny, and apparently he was closer to the diagnosis than he knew.

Buck was not finding the humor in this situation, Daniel knew. He really had been frightened by Daniel’s reaction to the news, enough so that he called their sister and had her rush over. Maddie was, obviously, just as relieved to hear about the mono diagnosis as Daniel was, much to poor Buck’s continuing confusion.

The both of them had come with him to the doctor’s appointment that afternoon, squeezing into a convenient opening in the scheduling. Daniel had gone alone into the doctor’s office this time, instead of having anyone come in with him for support, leaving the two of them in the waiting room. That was maybe not a good thing for Daniel, because Maddie was undoubtedly using the opportunity to tell Buck all about the cancer scare, but that was fine.

He could weather his kid being upset with him.

“So, Mr. Buckley.” The doctor, some old guy named Griffiths, looked up from Daniel’s charts. “Your tests all came back.”

“That’s what the lady on the phone said,” Daniel told him, slouching where he sat on the exam bed. “She told me I had mono. Which, I gotta say, Doc, I’m thankful for it. But I don’t understand how I could’ve gotten it.”

“It is called the kissing disease,” the doctor joked with a chuckle.

It irritated Daniel. His demeanor reminded him far too much of the doctors he regularly saw in El Paso, to make sure he wasn’t walking away from johns - and janes - with anything more than money and bruises. He knew exactly what they thought of him and he couldn’t even say they were wrong, not really.

“Yeah, but I haven’t been kissing anyone in months,” he said. Griffiths opened his mouth and Daniel cut him off with, “I haven’t fucked anyone in months. I doubt I got it from my hand. Or the internet, that’s what the antivirus is for.”

If the doctor was bothered by Daniel being crude and blunt, he didn’t show it.

“Mononucleosis is usually caused by the Epstein-Barr virus,” Griffiths said instead. “It’s so common that it doesn’t even get screened for when testing for STIs. You could have gotten it years ago. Was there any time you remember being sick like this before? Maybe a doctor diagnosed you with strep?”

“Maybe?” Daniel shrugged. “So how did I get sick this time?”

“When you get it, it sticks around in an inactive state. It doesn’t normally act up again, but…” Griffiths glanced through the charts once more and gave Daniel a considering look. “You’re a firefighter, that’s a stressful job. Stress can weaken your immune system, enough that it can’t continue suppressing the virus.”

“I’ve been a firefighter for nearly thirteen years, Doc,” Daniel pointed out. Then, after a thought, he added, “My brother nearly died three times this year.”

“That’ll do it.”

“I really did think it was the cancer again.” The words came out of Daniel’s mouth in a hush, but the doctor’s office was silent and he heard it all the same.

“They can present so similarly that a misdiagnosis of leukemia isn’t unheard of,” Griffiths told him, voice sympathetic. Daniel had to fight the reflex to sneer. “That's why I ordered the spot test. Lift up your shirt for me, please. I want to check your spleen again, see if it’s gone down any since last time.”

Daniel dutifully did as he requested as the doctor pulled on a pair of disposable gloves. The enlarged spleen was why he was bruising so easily. Probably also wasn’t helping his appetite any, to be completely honest. 

He stared at the wall as the doctor groped at his abdomen, barely wincing when his fingers pressed in at spots. There was a buzz in the back of his head that got steadily louder until the doctor stepped back and told him he could drop his shirt again. It took him a moment to blink back into awareness and recognize the words for what they were.

“It’s still rather enlarged,” Griffiths was saying when he tuned back in. There was the snap of elastic as the doctor tugged the gloves off and tossed them into the waste bin. “But it feels like it’s shrunk some.”

“Okay, well. We know what’s wrong with me,” Daniel said. “How do we fix it?”

“Fluids and bed rest.”

“Seriously?” he snapped, glowering at the doctor.

“It’s a virus, so antibiotics are out,” Griffiths said with a shrug. “Rest up, drink plenty of water. Take some acetaminophen or ibuprofen for the pain and fever. That’s about all I can give you.”

“So, what I’ve been doing, basically.”

“Is that what you’ve been doing?” The doctor had no business sounding so skeptical. “The impression I got from your friend last time was that you’ve been ignoring your symptoms for weeks.”

Austin was getting so much coal for Christmas.

“I’ll make sure your captain gets a note saying you shouldn’t be doing anything labor intensive,” Griffiths said, pulling a pen from his coat pocket and writing something down on his clipboard.

“Gee, thanks,” Daniel groused. “I drive the engine. Can I still do that, or is that too much?”

“Eh, it’ll probably be fine,” the doctor said without a single glance his way. “Now, there really is a reason it’s called the kissing disease. It spreads through saliva, so you don’t need to be quarantined. Just be careful with anything that involves swapping spit, like, well, like kissing or sharing drinks.”

“For how long?”

Another shrug.

“You could be infectious for upwards of eighteen months,” Griffiths said. Daniel stared at him in open disbelief. “I hope your computer’s antivirus is really good.”

“I don’t think I like you as my doctor,” Daniel bluntly told the man.

“And yet, I’ll still happily accept your insurance.”

The doctor let him go shortly afterwards, following a reiteration of bed rest and fluids. The moment he stepped back in the waiting room, which was still its own circle of hell, he was being accosted by his siblings. He didn’t even get the chance to tell them anything before Buck, angry as expected, was making his feelings clear.

“Were you ever going to tell me that you thought you had cancer?” Buck spat, hounding his steps as he went for the exit.

“Eventually,” was Daniel’s easy answer. It did not make things better, apparently.

“You said you would do better about this! What the hell, Danny!?”

“Don’t look at me,” Maddie said when he turned to her for help. “I told you to tell him days ago.”

Traitor.

“I was waiting until I knew for sure,” Daniel said. “And look, now I do. I don’t have cancer.”

“Because you have mono.” Buck made a face as he said it, suddenly remembering to keep his distance. “Who did you get mono from? Austin hasn’t been looking sick.”

“You know that Austin and I don’t really do the whole kissing thing, right?” Daniel asked him. “Not like you and Eddie, making out in front of my pizza and an innocent child.”

“So then who have you been kissing to get it?” his brother asked, ignoring the bait.

“Wouldn’t you like to know,” he taunted. Mostly because he didn’t want to tell Buck that this was, apparently, a stress induced illness. His brother would almost certainly draw the right connections and start blaming himself, because that was the kind of stupid thing he did.

“What did the doctor say?” Maddie asked, putting a stop to their bickering. “Do we need to swing by the pharmacy?”

“That I’ll be perfectly fine and I can go on with my life.” Daniel’s answer got him a pair of incredulous stares. He let out a sigh. “He just prescribed fluids and bed rest.”

Buck let out a bark of laughter. Daniel stopped and turned to glare at him.

“Good luck with that. He had the flu one year in El Paso, looked like the walking dead. He still tried to go in for his shift. His captain ended up sending him home.” Buck tilted his head, a considering look crossing his face. That, and a smile that Daniel did not like. “I’m gonna call Mehta, make sure he knows.”

“You little shit.”

Like Buck had a leg to stand on when it came being stubborn about going into work. The hypocritical brat.

When he told the others that he was transferring stations, Buck badgered Hen into promising that they’d make plans to see each other regardless. Unfortunately, shit happens, and so none of those plans had yet to come to fruition. Which is why Buck was standing in front of the Wilsons’ front door, waiting for someone to answer it. Hen was home, he gathered that from her car being parked in the drive. And also from the way he could hear her telling their little dog, Paisley, to calm down.

“Buck!” She sounded surprised when she opened the door. “What are you doing here?”

“I heard you came back from your, uh. Retreat?” he hedged as she let him inside. He wasn’t really sure what else to call what she and Karen did. “And I haven’t really seen you since…”

Technically, he’d seen her on the day of the accident, but realistically? Buck hadn’t really seen her since his last day at the 118. Hence what prompted this surprise visit to her house.

“You didn’t bring any sopapillas with you this time,” Hen remarked, closing the door.

“The last couple of days have been a thing. Have you heard about Bobby?” Buck asked, kneeling down to pet Paisley. Or attempt to, anyway. The yappy thing growled and ran off, only to start barking at him some more from a safer distance. “Y’know, our neighbors growing up had a little yap dog. Pretty sure it hated everybody.”

“We always know when the mail’s here,” Hen joked. “Yeah, Athena and Chimney both told me. He also told me about Maddie.”

“It’s been a trip,” Buck told her. “Apparently Danny had a cancer scare and didn’t even tell me about it!”

“Oh.” Hen’s eyebrows went sky high. “Is he okay?”

“Apparently it’s just mono,” he said. “How are you and Karen?”

“We’re better,” she said. “The time away was just what we both needed, I think.”

“Good, I’m glad. Hey, I know I haven’t asked in a while, but how’s the baby-making?” Buck watched Hen’s face drop at the question, and he immediately felt guilty. “Or- Or- Y’know what? That’s none of my business, forget I asked.”

Hen let out a sigh and headed for her kitchen.

“It’s fine,” she told him over her shoulder. “But it’s a wine conversation, at least.”

“Red wine?”

“I have a merlot.”

“...Is that red?”

Hen let out a laugh and shook her head. Buck took a seat at the dining table, Paisley coming over to cautiously sniff at him. When Hen came back, she brought with her two wine glasses that she sat down on the table and a half-empty bottle of wine.

“So none of what we did worked,” was Hen’s blunt answer to Buck’s earlier question. The merlot was more purple than red as she filled both of their glasses. “At all. None of the embryos were good.”

“Oh man, that’s…” He trailed off, not certain of what to say. Literally everything he could think of seemed too small. “I’m sorry.”

“Karen took it harder than me,” Hen said, pausing to take a drink of the wine. “I mean, I was upset, but Karen? She just… She was devastated.”

“No doubt,” Buck said, reaching down to grip his leg under the table. He remembered how he felt when he first heard the phrase “pulmonary embolism” leave the ER doctor’s mouth.

“Yeah.” She let out a sigh. “So, Dan has mono?”

“Yeah!” Buck exclaimed. “Like, I knew something was wrong, y’know? And he kept saying everything was fine, except he went to the doctor the other day to get checked for cancer. And he didn’t even tell me about it!”

“But it’s just mono.”

“Mono! I don’t even know where he got it from. It wasn’t Austin.”

“Of course it wasn’t him,” Hen said with a scoff.

“Yeah, I know, he hasn’t been sick. That and Danny said they don’t do the whole “kissing thing,” whatever that means,” Buck said. “I don’t understand what those two are doing. Like, we spent last Christmas with Austin’s family, that’s how I found out about it. And he says they aren’t dating, but whenever he’s with Austin, they’re all over each other. Like conjoined twins in the comfy chair.”

“It’s not that confusing.”

“To you, maybe.”

Hen let out a sigh, put down her glass, and leaned forward.

“Alright,” she said. “Have you ever heard the phrase, queerplatonic?”

Ever since the second doctor’s appointment, Daniel hadn’t really known a moment of peace. Buck was a doer, a fixer, and however annoyed he was with Daniel for not telling him about things ahead of time, all the energy got diverted into motherhenning him the moment they got home. Doctor Griffiths had prescribed rest and fluids, and Buck was ensuring that Daniel was following that.

Daniel was practically trapped on the couch, bundled up in blankets because Buck kept shoving more onto him, along with a glass of water that was never allowed to be empty and which Buck made displeased noises about if Daniel went two minutes without drinking from it. And that was another problem, because Daniel was also barely allowed to leave the couch - “The doctor said rest, Danny!” - but all that water had to go somewhere. Thank God that Buck followed him into firefighting instead of Maddie and nursing, or else he’d probably get murdered by a patient.

Alma even got in on it, all the way from El Paso. She called him up, presumably after Buck informed her of everything, to threaten him into behaving or else she’d come up to make sure of it in person. Despite only being a few - very few - years older than him, she really was a nagging aunt at times.

Freedom came in the form of Buck declaring he was going to go bother someone else. Daniel assumed it would’ve been Eddie, except then he realized that Eddie would’ve just come over with Chris. The three of them would probably join forces to continue making him miserable, so thankfully that wasn’t happening.

The moment he was out the door, Daniel was clawing his way out of his prison of blankets. His first destination was to the bathroom, and then he was heading for the fridge to drink something that wasn’t water. Beer was a fluid, so he was technically following the doctor’s orders. He deserved it after his suffering.

That logic didn’t mean, however, that he didn’t try hiding it like a naughty teenager the moment he heard the apartment door open again.

“Oh,” he said, relaxing when it was just Austin that came in, a pair of grocery bags dangling from his hand. “It’s just you.”

“Just me.” Austin rolled his eyes, and then pointed at the bottle Daniel pulled from behind his back. “I don’t think Buck would approve of that.”

“Oh my God,” Daniel said in disbelief. “Did that brat send you over to babysit me?”

“It only seemed fair,” Austin said. He sat the bags down on the kitchen table and started taking out their contents. “I spy on him for you, I come over and make sure you’re actually resting for him.”

“I’m breaking up with you,” Daniel said without any heat. “What’d you bring?”

“I told Mom you were sick and she sent me her chicken noodle soup recipe,” he answered. “But if you’re breaking up with me…”

“On second thought, maybe I’ll keep being your fake boyfriend.”

“That’s what I thought,” Austin said. “Where does your brother keep everything?”

“Why do you ask that like this is his kitchen?”

“We’ve known each other too long for you to even pretend like that wasn’t a stupid question.”

“Hey, I have Buck and you, and places that deliver.” Daniel pulled out a chair and sat down. “It’s not like I’m gonna starve.”

He watched Austin work, pointing out where everything was when he was asked. When he drained his drink, he got up for another, grabbing one for Austin as well. And because he was just that nice, Daniel even popped it open for him.

“How are you feeling?” Austin asked him, turning down the heat on the burner he was using.

“I’m fine,” was Daniel’s immediate answer. It earned him a flat look of disbelief. “I am!”

“You thought it was cancer,” Austin pointed out, grabbing his drink from where Daniel sat it down next to the stove. He turned around fully and leaned back against the counter. “And you look like crap.”

“It’s a good thing you don’t actually date, ‘cause you’re shit at flirting.”

“Dan,” Austin sighed. Daniel hunched in on himself at the tone, looking away. “I came back from the bathroom to find you breaking down in the waiting room.”

“That is a gross exaggeration,” he protested.

“You’re not okay,” Austin said. Daniel took a long draw from his drink to keep from cursing him out for it, which was a poorly timed idea since the next words from Austin were, “I think you should see a therapist.”

“You think-” Daniel tried to say between coughs, since he nearly choked on his beer. “A therapist? No, fuck you.”

“What would you say if it was Buck?”

Daniel glared at him for the question.

“This isn’t about Buck, this is about me,” he said. “And I’m perfectly fine. Also, fuck you.”

“You are not fine,” Austin argued.

“Austin.” Daniel got up and swaggered over, as best he could with how he was feeling. He slipped his arms around Austin’s neck, their chests almost touching. “I am fine.”

The other man scowled at him.

“Dan.” Austin pushed him back. Daniel went easily and looked away from him, unable to meet his eyes as he felt a little bubble of shame well up in his stomach. “I’m worried about you.”

“Don’t be,” he said with a scoff, turning his back on Austin. “I told you, I keep telling you, I’m fi-”

“No, you’re not,” Austin said, cutting him off. “You are just as not fine now as when you were pretending like you weren’t sick and scared.”

“Y’know what?” Daniel snapped, stomping away. “I’m gonna curl up on the couch, rest like the doc ordered. Tell me when the food’s done.”

Austin sighed behind him, but he refused to look. Instead, Daniel retook his spot on the couch, wrapping himself in one of the many blankets that his brother forced upon him earlier before grabbing the remote and finding something to watch. When the soup was done, after half of some rerun was over, Austin simply brought him a bowl of it before sitting down on the couch beside him, pressed as close as they usually were.

Neither of them said anything, and Daniel was quite content with that.

Chapter 24

Chapter Notes

Christmas chapters in the month of Halloween. I feel kinda dirty.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

The store was, as to be expected this time of year, somewhat crowded. Daniel probably should not have put this off for so long, but he’d wasted time arguing with himself, debating whether it was something he should even do or not. In the end, he’d come down on the side of yes, and so here he was.

“Why are we here?” Buck asked, looking around them with interest.

“Looking for a present for Austin,” Daniel said. Normally they just exchanged something simple, like candy. This year, he was going for something more.

“I didn’t realize he was such a nerd,” his brat of a brother commented, looking around at the games and figurines on display as they went into one of the cramped spaces between shelves..

“He is.” Daniel’s tone might’ve been a bit too fond, judging from the look that Buck shot him. “He doesn’t really get to play it, though. Weird schedule and all.”

“Why don’t you play it with him?” Buck asked, peering at the boxed models. Daniel watched his eyes skim over the price tag before letting out a low whistle.

“Kinda need more than two people.”

Not to mention that this was not one of the times where their interests overlapped. Austin enjoyed it, and that was all good for him, but Daniel didn’t really understand the appeal. If he wanted a fantasy story, he’d read a book or watch a movie. If he wanted to violently slay monsters, there were video games for that.

But if Austin asked Daniel to play, he probably would. And then they’d both undoubtedly end up regretting it.

“So what are we looking for?” Buck asked him.

“I dunno, something cool looking,” Daniel answered, shrugging when his brother turned to stare at him incredulously. “I don’t play the damn game, I just know he collects the shit. He’s got a bunch on his shelves. Little guys with axes, some weird monster things, y’know.”

“Little guys with axes, weird monster things, gotcha.” Buck nodded to himself, trailing his finger along one of the shelves as he walked. He stopped and grabbed a package, this one holding an assortment of little figures. “What about this?”

“It’s something,” Daniel said, taking it from him. 

Buck rolled his eyes at him, and then paused to reach up on one of the higher shelves. He brought down a box that depicted on one side some woman with lightning and on the other a picture of the figurine inside, some red dragon with what looked like a mane of frills. Daniel took it from him, gave it a once over, and nodded.

“Alright, this one sparks joy,” he said. “Did you want to look around here, find something else for… I dunno, Chris or whoever.”

“I think this all might be a bit too old school for him.”

Daniel made a face at the comment.

“He liked the Legos I got him last year, didn’t he?” he asked, needing to make sure. Because otherwise things would be very awkward when Chris opened his present this year.

“Yeah. That’s what you got him this year, isn’t it?” Buck asked. Daniel just shrugged, because he wasn’t confirming anything. “What did you get Eddie?”

“What makes you think I got him anything?”

“Uh, the fact you got him presents three Christmases in a row?” Buck shot back.

“That was then,” Daniel retorted, taking his small haul to the check-out counter. “Why the hell would I get him anything now?”

Once upon a time, his baby brother looked up at him like Daniel held all the secrets in the world. None of that was present today. In fact, the expression on Buck’s face was almost insulting, some mix between open skepticism and the obvious (low) appraisal of Daniel’s intelligence.

“Shut up, squirt.”

“I didn’t even say anything,” Buck protested, laughter in his voice.

Daniel ignored him in favor of paying for everything, because that was better for the both of them.

Holiday mishaps seemed to account for a large portion of their calls in the last couple months of the year. Buck lost track of how many turkey related emergencies they responded to in the lead up to and on the day of Thanksgiving this year. The weeks before Christmas involved a startling number of electrocutions, electrical fires, and general decoration disasters.

Buck had his attention split between his phone, where Eddie was texting him about the woman who assaulted Santa Claus and her fellow customers over a toy duck, and Mehta, who was giving them the details of what they were heading for. From what Buck managed to gather, someone was in the middle of putting up lights on their eaves when their ladder fell over, sending them plummeting two stories to the ground. The caller was apparently their kid, with no other adult around.

When they arrived at the house, they found a front yard decorated to Buck’s tastes. That is, it looked very much like the holiday aisle vomited all over. There was a string of dead lights dangling down the side of the house, an extension ladder on the ground just below it where a man was lying on the ground with a boy standing over him

Jon Mendoza and one of the other paramedics on shift, Nola Dorsey, jogged over to the man with their kits. Buck was shortly behind them with Mehta and Danny. The man’s leg was bent at an awkward and painful looking angle, and he was groaning as he clutched at his head.

“God, my wife is going to kill me,” the man complained, moving to sit up. Jon grabbed him by the shoulder and pushed him back down.

“Let’s not make it any easier for her,” Nola told him, in the process of getting a collar around the man’s neck.

“Buckley,” Mehta suddenly spoke up. 

Buck wasn’t sure if it was directed at him or Danny, the captain hadn’t broken the habit yet of just saying their last name when he wanted Buck’s brother. When he looked, Mehta was pointing at the boy, a year or two younger than Chris, with his hair in an atrocious bowl cut and his eyes streaming tears. And there was blood coming from a wound on his head, though Buck couldn’t tell where exactly he was hurt.

“Hi.” Buck crouched in front of him with a wide smile, blocking his view of the paramedics working on his father. “How about we go over here, okay?”

“Okay.” The boy sounded subdued, understandable, but he allowed Buck to direct him to the curb, far enough out of the way, where they both sat down.

“I’m Dan, that’s Buck,” Danny said, kneeling beside them. He dug through his own kit, the one they kept on the engine, and pulled out disposable gloves to tug on. “What’s your name, kid?”

“Billy.”

“Nice to meet you, Billy. How’d you hurt your head?”

“Ladder hit me when it felled,” Billy mumbled, reaching up to rub at his eye. “I was helping Daddy. He’s been sick. Mama told him to wait, that she’d fix the lights when she got home, but Daddy said he could do it.”

“So your dad’s stubborn when he’s sick,” Buck remarked. “Y’know, that reminds me of someone. I wonder who?”

He shot his brother his most shit-eating grin, earning himself an eye roll. Danny was looking much better now, weeks later. Buck had coerced various people in their lives to help enforce the doctor’s orders of rest and fluids. Even Christopher had gotten in on it, to everyone’s amusement but Danny’s.

“Shut it, squirt,” his brother sniped, and then held up an antiseptic wipe. He told Billy, “I’m gonna wipe around where you’re bleeding, so I can see it. It’s gonna sting, do you want to hold Buck’s hand?”

The kid gave a jerky nod, so Buck gave him his hand. Billy winced and flinched when Danny got a little too close to the source of the bleeding as he wiped away the mess, his grip on Buck squeezing tightly. There was a lot of blood, head wounds bled like crazy and it was still bleeding, but they could make out the gash near Billy’s hairline.

“That looks nasty, Billy,” Danny said, trading the wipes for some gauze to press over it. “You must’ve broken your dad’s ladder, I hope you can pay for it.”

“I don’t have a job,” Billy responded, face scrunched up in offense. “I’m seven.”

“Freeloader.” He peeled away the gauze, made an exaggerated face at whatever he saw, and then pressed it back down again. “Okay, I have good news and I have bad news. Which one do you want first?”

Billy frowned and looked to Buck.

“I always like to hear the good news first,” Buck told him, lowering his voice like he was sharing a secret.

“Good news,” Billy declared with a nod.

“The good news is that I’m pretty sure we won’t have to amputate your head,” Danny said. He pulled out an adhesive pad, holding it out to Buck to open it so he didn’t have to let off pressure from the head wound. “We have a special saw for it on the truck, y’know. The bad news is that all of our Band-Aids are boring.”

“No Superman?” Buck asked him, handing back the pad and tossing the wrapping with the bloody antiseptic wipe.

“No Superman,” Danny confirmed with a shake of his head, being careful he didn’t get any of Billy’s hair as he pressed the pad over the wound. “Not even any Paw Patrol.”

“The dalmatian’s his favorite,” Buck told Billy.

“That show’s for babies,” the seven year old told them, with an honest-to-God sneer on his face.

“Of course you wouldn’t watch that. You’re a big kid,” Danny agreed without missing a beat, even as he pulled out another wad of wipes to start cleaning the kid’s tearstained face, along with the blood he hadn’t gotten to already. He glanced over Billy’s head, to where they were treating his dad. When Buck turned to look, he saw that Jon and Nola were loading him up on the gurney. “Okay, kiddo. You’re ready to ride with your dad? Buck’ll take you right over there.”

He pointed over at the ambulance, its back doors wide open. Billy gave a nod and stood up with Buck, once again holding his hand. The pair of them walked to the ambulance, leaving Danny behind to pack up. 

“Comin’ through, Baby Buckley,” Jon announced, arriving with the father on the gurney.

He and Nola worked together, with a little assistance from Buck, to load their patient up, and then Jon rounded the ambulance to get into the driver’s seat. Buck helped Billy climb into the back, where Nola greeted him with a smile and helped him get situated in a seat where he wouldn’t be in the way. When Buck closed the doors, his last view of them was Billy laughing at something Nola said.

“Well that was fun,” Danny remarked, coming to join him. He smacked him lightly on the arm with the back of his - now ungloved - hand. “Hey, how many ladder tumbles does that make this week?”

“Uh.” Buck frowned in thought. “Five. Wait, are we counting the one where we found the woman dangling from the gutter?”

“Nah, she didn’t fall to the ground,” his brother said, leading them both to the engine, where everyone else was climbing in. He paused to throw the med kit into its proper compartment before going for the cab. “It’s Nola’s turn to cook for everyone, so I really hope they have to wait forever for a bed to open up. I know she’s planning on making jambalaya again, and I just cannot.”

“You’re just a wuss who can’t handle spice.”

“Just shut up and get in.”

(Nola and Jon were not held up at the hospital for long. And when she was making her jambalaya for them, Buck managed to convince her that it needed to be hotter. Because sometimes, he expressed his love for his brother by making him suffer.)

The holiday spirit was feeling rather weak in the Diaz household compared to last year. At least that was the case for Christopher. For Eddie, the lead up to Christmas last year was spent repeating the same cycle of mistakes with Shannon that led to him screwing everything up to begin with, just in newer and more toxic ways, until he took a leap of faith and helped Santa make his son’s Christmas wish come true. There was a copy of one of the pictures they took that morning, of the three of them together, up on the mantle in the living room. It sat right next to the picture of him and Buck, from way back when they were stupid teenagers. 

Christopher had spent that Christmas as an ecstatic bundle of joy. This year, though…

“It got ugly,” Eddie told Hen. She’d come over with Denny, so the two of them could commiserate in their parenting woes. It also served as a good opportunity for Buck to see her, not that Eddie’s boyfriend was spending much time at the adults table at the moment. Joke was on him, though: the adults table had freshly baked gingerbread men. “After I said I had to work on Christmas, he went straight to his room and wouldn’t speak to me for the rest of the night.”

“It’s just new,” Hen assured him. “He’ll get over it.”

“Abuela’s bringing him to my aunt’s,” he said. Pepa’s kids and grandkids were visiting her for Christmas. They’d probably all keep him too suitably distracted to be a Scrooge. “I know he’s gonna have a great time. It’s just that last Christmas was… Special. This one’s harder.”

“Is that why you invited us over?” she asked him, smiling. “Trying to cram in as much Christmas cheer between now and then that you can?”

“I thought it’d be nice to bring the boys together for a playdate.”

“All three of them,” Hen quipped with a laugh.

The boys, which included Buck, were in the living room, sitting around the coffee table which was being used as the construction site for a pair of gingerbread houses. The living room was suitably decorated, along with the rest of the house, with a large christmas tree by the window, and various bows and paper snowflakes tacked to the walls. There was even a reindeer patterned throw pillow on the couch with a nativity scene - courtesy of Abuela - sitting on the small table behind it. 

It had nothing on the Buckley brothers’ apartment, however. He’d made the mistake of staying over there too late on November 30th, losing track of the time, and so he ended up bearing witness to the utter insanity that befell the space. Buck’s phone started playing Jingle Bell Rock at 12:01 AM, exactly, and it had the man tripping over himself as he raced for the closet where he and Danny kept the decorations.

That same attitude followed Eddie to his home, where Buck would look around the place and sigh, pointedly and often, until Eddie finally caved and started decorating before the first week of the month was through.

(Eddie didn’t actually know where the throw pillow came from, he didn’t have it last year and he hadn’t bought it this year. He suspected that Buckley shenanigans were afoot, because Buck’s explanation of “Magic Christmas Elves” was obviously bullshit. But it was his now and he wasn’t giving it back.)

“Alright, who needs more cement?” Buck asked, happily stirring a cup of icing. Both boys shouted affirmations, and he sat the cup down to pick up what Eddie assumed was meant to be a wall. “Denny, we’ll do you first with this big ol’ piece.”

“Hey, Buck?” Chris sounded quiet and hesitant.

“Yeah?”

“Can I spend Christmas with you and Danny?”

Out of the corner of Eddie’s eye, he saw Hen tilt her head with a curious expression on her face.

“Uh. Maybe?” Buck glanced across the room to Eddie and shrugged, before spreading icing mortar along the edges of Denny’s gingerbread wall. “I’ll have to check if it’s okay with your dad and your abuela, okay?”

“Fine.”

From the tone used, Eddie could tell that the non-answer didn’t exactly cheer Chris up any. It was obvious to Buck, too, judging from the way he frowned as he handed the gingerbread wall to Denny. He crossed his arms on the table and leaned forward so he was closer to Chris.

“The first year Danny had to work on Christmas, I was kinda bummed, too,” he said, voice just loud enough to carry over to Eddie and Hen. “I spent the day with Tia Alma and her girls. But I spent the next day with Danny, opening presents together. And that’s what we’ve done every time he had to work on Christmas.”

As Eddie watched, those words seemed to do the job of lifting up Chris’ mood. At the very least, he looked less dour and upset about their current circumstances.

“See?” Hen gestured at them with a gingerbread man. “I promise you, he’ll forget he was even mad by New Year’s.”

Buck got to his feet and made his way to the dining room, patting both boys as he went past. He threw himself into the chair beside Eddie with a melodramatic sigh, grabbing a gingerbread man from the tray in the middle of the table.

“Is it just me,” he said, “or does Christmas kinda suck this year? Like, I’m tryna feel it, but I’m just not.”

“Definitely not just you,” Eddie confirmed, viciously biting the head off his own gingerbread man.

“It’s been a rough year, for everyone,” Hen said. “So I am happy to immerse myself in the magic of Christmas, and I just look forward to putting this year behind me.”

“With the way this year’s been, though,” Buck said. “I wouldn’t be surprised if there’s one more smack in the face to come.”

“Probably.” Eddie sighed, and then he frowned at his boyfriend. “Wait, did you manage to get Christmas off three years in a row?”

“Nah.” Buck shook his head. “I volunteered to work it my first year, since Danny was working. Bobby did, too, come to think of it. I’ll have to work it next year, though.”

“Poor you,” Eddie and Hen chorused, and then laughed at the insulted expression on Buck’s face.

Chapter End Notes

fyi, here's what Daniel bought Austin:
"Niv-Messet the Red Dragon" and this pack.

Chapter 25

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Ever since Buck transferred to the 133, it wasn’t unusual for him to call Eddie or for Eddie to call Buck when they had a bit of downtime, just to hear the other’s voice during their twenty-four shifts. Buck was the one who started the habit, despite the way he usually preferred to send several texts in a row, full of so many abbreviations that it was essentially in code, over calling someone. Apparently the appreciation for actually talking on the phone, which had been entirely absent when they were in high school, could be credited to Abby, the dispatcher he dated for a few months.

Eddie wasn’t entirely certain how he felt about that.

Anyways, this fact meant Eddie was not surprised that his phone began to ring not long after they got back from their last call-out. That had involved someone who’d gotten sucked into an airplane’s turbine engine, saved from a gruesome end only by the expensive set of golf clubs that went in before him and clogged the machinery. Eddie paused what he was doing, wiping down the kitchen island, and pulled out his phone to answer it.

“Hey, Bu-”

“Where’s Bobby?”

“Um.” Eddie pulled his phone from his ear to squint at the screen for a moment. “He’s downstairs in the bay, I think.”

He dropped the rag he was using on the countertop and walked to the loft’s railing, peering down to the truck bay below. There was Bobby, sitting on the rear bumper of the engine and going through what looked like their votes for Christmas dinner.

“Take me to him,” Buck ordered.

“Did you call me just to talk to Bobby? You could’ve called him directly, I know you have his number.”

“Just do it!”

Eddie huffed out a breath and, figuring it was easier to just go along with it, went for the firepole. Sure, he could just take the stairs, but what was life without moments of whimsy? When his boots hit the ground, he headed for Bobby. The captain looked up at his approach, as friendly and welcoming as ever.

“Buck wants to talk to you,” Eddie said, putting his phone on speaker and holding it out.

Bobby’s face scrunched up in confusion.

“Buck?”

“Your nose started bleeding!?”

“What?” the two of them chorused. Eddie glared at his phone, and asked, “That’s what this is about?”

“Who even told you?” Bobby asked, and then, “Wait.”

He peered around and scowled at something. Eddie turned slightly to follow Bobby’s gaze, spotting Austin standing across the bay as he went through one of the rolling tool boxes. As if feeling their eyes on him, he raised his head and stared back. He gave an almost sarcastic wave before going back to the tools.

“Do you have Austin spying on Bobby for you?” Eddie asked.

“That doesn’t matter.” That would be a yes, then. “What matters is- Bobby had a nosebleed!”

The sigh that Bobby let out was very familiar to Eddie. It was the same kind of sighing that Eddie caught himself doing with Chris, one part exasperated and one part fond and several parts tired on a spiritual level.

“It’s just a nosebleed, Buck.” The captain reached up to pinch the bridge of his nose. Eddie hoped the shrug he gave him came across as sympathetic as he meant it to. “Could’ve been allergies, could’ve been the Santa Anas. Could’ve been how cold it was out there on that tarmac.”

“Was this your first nosebleed? Have your gums been bleeding? Do you have a rash anywhere?” Buck rattled off the questions like a machine gun, barely a breath in between them. “Eddie, does he have any rashes?”

“Not that I can see,” Eddie dutifully answered. “He might have one under his shirt, though.”

That qualifier earned a scowl.

“What are you doing?” Bobby asked, cutting things off before Buck’s demands inevitably went somewhere awkward for everyone. He was more exasperated than fond, now. “Are you trying to diagnose me over the phone?”

“No one’s said anything to me about shortness of breath or any bruises,” Buck continued, ignoring Bobby’s question. “But you could be hiding symptoms like a headache, dizziness, irregular heart rate. Are you?”

Bobby looked to Eddie, as if he was expecting him to intercede here or something. Like Eddie wasn’t the one who brought his phone over here and put it on speaker. Honestly, this wasn’t even Buck at his most ridiculous. There was a reason, after all, that Buck wasn’t exactly popular with most of their teachers in high school.

“I’m not hiding any symptoms,” Bobby told Buck. “Because I’m not having any symptoms of whatever it is you think I have.”

Eddie figured he could hazard a solid guess as to what checklist of symptoms Buck was running through. 

“I know you were in that tunnel way too long. Okay, that much exposure to radiation can cause aplastic anemia.” So not cancer, but close enough. “Did you know that’s what killed Marie Curie?”

“No, I didn’t,” Bobby said. He rolled his eyes as he said it, so it was probably a good thing Buck wasn’t here in person. “I also did not know that you knew who Madame Marie Curie was.”

“Well, I had to look her up-”

“Born in Warsaw, moved to Paris, first woman to win a Nobel Prize,” Eddie recited, shrugging at the look Bobby gave him. He tended to be in the splash zone for random facts whenever Buck was on a new hyperfixation.

“She was the first person to win two Nobel Prizes, and the only person to win them in different fields.” Buck’s smile was evident in his tone. Eddie decided that he would start regurgitating the random facts his boyfriend shared with him more often. “She was a really smart lady. But! But! She still died from too much exposure to radiation.”

“Okay, listen, I appreciate your concern, but I am fine. So, please, stop it with the internet research,” Bobby said, almost begged.

Eddie let out a soft snort.

“Good luck with that,” he said under his breath.

“The real problem is that we have a tie for Christmas dinner,” Bobby said, directed more to Eddie than to Eddie’s phone. He got to his feet, holding the little basket filled with paper slips, on which they wrote their votes for what they were going to eat on Christmas. “Five votes for burgers, five votes for Chinese.”

“Burgers? Chinese?” Buck’s tinny voice sounded appalled.

“I think I’m gonna switch my vote to Chinese,” Bobby said, more to himself than to Eddie or Buck. “I am just not up to cooking Christmas dinner this year.”

“Ah! Fatigue!” Buck exclaimed. “Eds, check his temperature for me.”

“Do not,” Bobby warned, leaning away from Eddie. “Buck, I know you’re worried, but there is nothing to worry about yet. So as my grandmother used to say, don’t go borrowing trouble.”

“Bobby, I-” Buck started to say, but Eddie had to interrupt to tell him that,

“Bobby’s already gone.” 

The captain was heading for the stairs, the basket full of ballots in hand. He clearly wasn’t feeling all that fatigued, because he was getting away from this conversation rather quickly.

“I can’t believe you’re having Chinese for Christmas dinner,” Buck groused.

“I know.” Eddie was rather disappointed: he’d voted for burgers. “Hey, how much of that was because you’re still upset about Danny hiding the cancer scare from you?”

There was a beat of silence, and then he heard a sigh followed by,

“I don’t know.” Buck’s shrug was almost audible through the phone. “About a quarter?”

“I figured. If it makes you feel better, Athena told Hen that all of Bobby’s tests have been clear.”

“...that does make me feel better.”

In the months since everything with Doug, since the bastard nearly took Maddie away from them again, Daniel did his best to carve out time to spend with his sister. Just the two of them, no boyfriends (real or fake), no bratty brother. Once upon a time, they’d been each other’s best friend. Even after the cancer, after Daniel took to heart Mom’s words that Buck was made for him, that continued to be the case. Until Doug.

This installment of quality time took place early in the morning, in a cafe bedecked in Christmas cheer. Nothing quite on what they inflicted upon their apartment whenever the month rolled around - their electric bill come January used to hurt Daniel on a deep and spiritual level, back in the day, but the lights always made Buck happy - but it was festive enough. There was even a tiny little fake Christmas tree sitting in the middle of the table that he and Maddie sat at with their drinks that smelled very strongly of peppermint.

This time was meant to be for the two of them, to try and bridge the distance over the yawning maw of a canyon that had grown between them since high school. And yet, somehow, Doug was still managing to haunt it.

“Okay, run this by me again,” Daniel said. “What exactly are you going to be doing with this Frank guy?”

“We’re going to Big Bear,” Maddie said, taking a sip of her latte. It left a smear of whipped cream on her lip; Daniel decided not to say anything about it and wait to see how long it took her to notice. “Back to where he… Died.”

“Why?” Daniel blurted.

“He said he wants me to go there and talk to him out loud. Let him go,” she explained. “It’s supposed to be where he hurt me the most, but honestly? I don’t even know where that would even be. That place just feels like where I managed to hurt him back for once.”

“He was a rat bastard, and I’m just sorry I didn’t kill him first. But at least we can piss on his grave.”

His sister let out a snort and then covered her mouth with a guilty expression. She squinted at her hand when she pulled it away, and then grabbed a napkin to wipe off the whip cream from both her hand and her face. 

“Do you want me to go with you?” Daniel asked. “I can call Athena and Tabitha just as easily from the car. Hell, we can grab Buck, make it a family trip.”

“I’m not sure what you’d even be doing. I’m not even sure what I’m supposed to be doing,” she told him. “I’m just tired of Doug ruining everything for me, from you and Buck to Christmas, so if this helps…”

“If it helps,” he agreed, raising his drink in toast. “Hey, how um… How is Frank?”

“How’s Frank?” Maddie’s face scrunched up a bit in confusion at the question.

“You know, as- as a therapist.”

“Why? Are you going to see him for yourself?”

“Just curious,” Daniel told her with a shrug. “Buck had a bad experience with one of the department’s therapists, his first year.”

Thinking about that nameless woman still had the violent urges rearing up. He hadn’t ever asked Athena for an update about her, knowing it wouldn’t be anything substantial and that it would just make him angrier.

Maddie let out a little hum.

“He’s fine,” she said. “It’s not like I’ve been to any therapists before him, so I don’t have anything to compare him to.”

The two of them sat there, chatting and catching up, until their drinks were drained and it was time for Maddie to go. They parted ways on the sidewalk outside, with Daniel wishing her good luck and her promising to see him on Christmas. His car was a block away and around the corner, not that far away when compared to how stupid parking in LA could be.

He was halfway there when a man bumped into him. It wouldn’t have been anything major, except Daniel caught the acrid stench of cigarette smoke at the same time. He practically leaped away from the man, pressing himself against the wall of the building. The action earned him startled and annoyed looks from everyone around him, especially from the guy he’d bumped into, a lit cigarette dangling from his mouth. Daniel ignored them, though, shaking his head and huffing in annoyance with himself. Once he’d calmed down, he continued on to his car. 

On the way back to the apartment, he called up first Athena and then Tabitha, Austin’s mother. They were both very much on board with Buck’s plan to save Christmas, with Athena promising to get in touch with all those whose numbers Buck didn’t have. Unfortunately, while Athena was a quick and easy phone call, simply explaining what his brother was plotting and asking for her assistance, calling Tabitha was an entirely different matter. Because Tabitha liked to talk.

Daniel didn’t manage to escape until he was walking into his apartment. When he was finally able to hang up, he heaved out a sigh so heavy it had Buck cracking up where he was lounging on the couch.

“I’ve done my part,” Daniel told him, toeing off his shoes and setting them by the wall. “Athena’s calling everyone else, and she said she’ll get the food taken care of.”

Buck cheered and pumped his fist.

“This might be my best idea yet,” he bragged. “I don’t even have to do anything, not really.”

Daniel rolled his eyes and, after a moment’s hesitation, walked over to take a seat on the coffee table. His brother immediately sat up properly to regard him with suspicion.

“What is this?” Buck demanded. “What’s going on? Are you about to tell me you actually have cancer now?”

“No,” Daniel intoned. “Austin’s been nagging at me to see a therapist. And I think… I think I might take him up on it.”

“Why?” his brother asked, fingers twisting at the fabric of the sweatpants he was wearing.

“Because he’s worried about me,” he answered. Austin actually came at the topic with what must’ve been a prepared argument for it, because he just kept throwing out reasons. Everytime he brought it up, there was more. It was Daniel’s own fault, for telling him far too much. “And I don’t think he’s entirely wrong to be.”

The way he could be so easily freaked out just by the smell of cigarette smoke was a rather solid piece of evidence for it.

“Oh.”

“I’ll probably hate it,” Daniel said. “But I was… I was thinking that, maybe, it would be good for you to go, too.”

Part of the reason for that was how, every time he tried to bat away Austin’s argument, the man kept coming back at him with, What if it was Buck? So technically speaking, this was all Austin’s fault.

“What?” Buck squawked, jumping back like a startled cat. “No, no, I’m good. I don’t need therapy, Danny. I’m perfectly fine.”

“Do you remember what you said, just before the tsunami?” Daniel asked him, leaning forward into his brother’s space. “When Eddie came in and dragged you out of bed? After he told you how lucky you were that the embolism didn’t kill you?”

His brother refused to meet his gaze, and he didn’t answer the question. So Daniel answered it for him.

“You said that maybe it should have. You asked what the point of you was, you said that you were useless.”

Buck scowled at the wall, jaw stiff. Then his brow furrowed and he turned his head to actually look at Daniel.

“Is that why you said all of that? When you thought you had cancer, is that why you said all that weird stuff?”

“Yes,” Daniel honestly answered. “You’re not useless, Buck. The point of you is just to be you.”

Buck scoffed and shook his head, in clear disagreement with him. It made Daniel’s chest ache.

“You don’t… We can find a guy therapist for you, if you want,” he offered. Buck’s eyes went wide and then darted to the side, his hands gripping tightly to his pants. “Maddie says that Frank’s missing a leg, that he’s in a wheelchair. Or- Or, if it would make you more comfortable, I can go with you, sit just outside. If… If they try to get you to do something you don’t… want to do, you can just get up and come get me, and we can just leave.”

Buck swallowed, let go of his pants to wrap his arms around his middle.

“I’ll… I’ll think about it,” he mumbled.

“Okay.” That was probably the best he was going to get. It was the best that Austin got from him, after all. “How excited was Chris, when he heard about the plans for Christmas?”

His brother looked momentarily thrown by the subject change, but he took to it quickly after a moment spent blinking at him.

“He doesn’t know yet, I just told Isabel,” Buck said, a shaky smile growing on his face. “I’m surprising them both.”

He just looked so damn proud of himself for it, too.

Christmas morning was greeted the same way it always was, whenever neither Buckley brother had to work on it. Buck rolled out of bed and immediately went to go wake up Danny, laughing at the colorful cursing that it earned him. Years ago, this would’ve been followed by him diving into the modest pile of presents that had migrated under the tree. These days, Buck turned on the appropriate music and sang along as he made them both breakfast.

After they had breakfast, and after Buck finally did dive into his presents, they called Maddie and then Tia Alma. After that, they spent the morning lounging about in their pajamas watching Christmas movies until it was time to follow through with Buck’s little plan to fix the holidays for everyone. They got ready and carried everyone’s gifts to the jeep, since it had more room, and made their way to the station.

The trucks were missing from the bay, meaning that the 118 were out on a call, which was very convenient. Everyone else was upstairs, Karen standing at the loft railing and waving at them. When Buck jogged up the stairs, he found that the tables were already set and the food was either done or almost ready.

“You’re late,” Athena tutted.

“He had to look pretty for his boyfriend,” Danny told her, reaching up to mess with Buck’s hair.

“Shut up,” Buck sniped, ducking away from him and then dropping to a knee when Chris called out his name as he hurried over. “Superman!”

“Merry Christmas, Buck,” the kid said, hugging him. “We’re surprising Dad!”

“Yeah we are. You’ve got presents in the jeep.”

Chris cheered, before going over to a group of kids that Buck didn’t recognize. He stood and looked at Athena in question, but it was Karen who answered.

“They’re staying at a group home,” she explained. “I figured, it’s Christmas…”

“Yeah, no, I get it.” There was a call that Eddie told him about last night, a single mother having a heart attack and her son that didn’t have anyone to stay with. Buck figured it was safe to assume he was one of the kids there. “Maddie not here, yet?”

“She’s on her way,” Danny told him, pocketing his phone. He pointed at some of the kids who were playing pool. “I’m gonna teach ‘em some valuable life skills.”

“Please,” Athena said as they watched him swagger towards the kids. “Please tell me he’s not about to hustle a bunch of children.”

Buck, unable to tell her anything of the sort, simply shrugged.

He spent the time while they waited for Eddie and the others to arrive chatting with Isabel and, after he got bored watching Danny build up and then knock down the confidence of a bunch of kids, Christopher. When the trucks returned, the entire group fell into mischievous silence as they listened to Bobby directing them into the bay.

“A little farther, come on back,” he could hear Bobby say, his words carrying up to them. “Three feet, two feet… We’re good.”

Athena made her way to the loft’s railing.

“I’d say so, Captain,” she said, raising her voice. “Merry Christmas.”

“Athena!” Bobby sounded surprised. “What are you doing here?”

“We came to save you. Someone told me you were ordering takeout for Christmas dinner.”

Buck went over to stand beside her and waved down at them all, laughing at the surprised faces everyone was wearing. They hurried up the stairs, and Buck braced himself for the wall of sound as everyone present shouted out “Merry Christmas” at the top of their lungs. He spotted Eddie’s wide smile as he made a beeline for Chris and Isabel.

There was mistletoe hanging from one of the beams, and Buck made his way to it. The Wilsons were right there, though, and they caught him first.

“I can’t believe you did this,” Hen told him, clinging to Denny. “Thank you.”

“You did say you wanted to immerse yourself in the magic of Christmas,” Buck reminded her. He reached up and snagged the mistletoe, dangling it over her head and smacking a kiss on her cheek that made her laugh. “Now, if you’ll excuse me, my boyfriend is right over there.”

“Keep it decent,” Hen told him.

Buck blew a raspberry at her. When he got to Eddie, he wrapped an arm around him from behind and held the mistletoe over the both of them. Eddie glanced up and rolled his eyes, before leaning in. They kept it decent, though not by choice - Maddie arrived at that point, accompanied by Chimney, and the pair made pointed comments as they went by and then laughed at their own jokes.

After dinner, Buck and Danny went back down to the jeep to carry up the presents they’d brought. On his way to delivering Chris’, a large toy fire truck, Buck caught a glimpse of his brother shyly presenting the gifts he’d picked up at the game store the other day, wrapped neatly in Santa-patterned wrapping paper. It looked intimate, even more than witnessing them beneath the mistletoe last year, and he hurriedly looked away to watch with Eddie and Isabel as Chris tore into his own gift.

As he looked around, he spotted Bobby sitting with Athena at one of the tables. Today had been the first day he’d actually seen his former captain since he switched stations.

“I’m gonna go say hi,” he told Eddie.

His boyfriend glanced over, knowing who he was talking about without having to ask, and nodded.

“Good luck,” Eddie told him, leaning in to kiss him again.

Buck made his way to the table, feeling somewhat nervous. The pair stopped talking and looked up at him; he gave them a shy smile and a lame wave,

“Hey, Bobby.”

“Buck.” Bobby nodded to him and then gestured for him to sit down. “How have you been? How’s the One Thirty-three?”

“Good.” Buck hurriedly sat down. “The One Thirty-three’s… Different. I like everyone there, though.”

“Good. That’s… That’s good.”

Athena glanced between them and rolled her eyes.

“I was telling Bobby how you helped with this,” she said, making a vague encompassing gesture. Then she reached over and tapped him on the forehead. “Your New Year’s resolution should be to use that head for good instead of evil.”

“I will put that at the top of my list,” he promised her with a laugh.

“Maybe less Doctor Google, too,” Bobby said.

“Yeah, sorry.”

They talked, Buck relaxing more by the word, until Bobby excused himself to go speak with Michael, who was standing off by himself. Athena pursed her lips as she watched her ex- and her current husband together, before shaking her head and returning her attention to Buck.

“Go get your brother and Eddie,” she told him, standing up. “I want to take a picture with everyone.”

“Yes, ma’am.”

It was easy to follow her directions, because the two of them were together. Danny was presenting Eddie the present he claimed not to have bought for him. It was wrapped in the same Santa-patterned paper as the rest of theirs, and had a little red bow on the end.

It was also distinctly baseball bat shaped.

Daniel tucked his phone back into his pocket and threw himself onto one of the couches, jarring his sister as he did so. He threw back his head and dragged a hand down his face, all the festive cheer sucked out with a single conversation.

“So,” Maddie said. For once, Chimney wasn’t with her; he was off talking to Hen. “Who was that on the phone?”

“Mom,” Daniel answered. “I figured I couldn’t keep ignoring her all day.”

His sister made a face.

“She hasn’t called me,” she said, pulling out her phone to check. “Nope, not a single missed call.”

“You’re more than welcome to her,” he told her, lifting his head up. He spotted Austin sitting at one of the tables across the loft, in a conversation with Tabitha and Alan and showing off the dragon figurine that Daniel gave him.

“Okay, no,” Maddie suddenly said, poking at his face. “I’ve had a year of this now, and I am tired of it.”

“Tired of what?” Daniel asked, tearing his eyes away to look at her.

“This!” she said, gesturing in Austin’s direction. “The two of you. You keep saying that you’re not actually dating, that it’s all fake, but then you go and make gooey eyes at him from across the room. C’mon, Dan, just admit that you love him and put me out of my misery.”

“When did I ever say I didn’t love him?”

His sister’s expression slowly shifted from shock to something on the near side of murderous.

“Then why,” she said, “do you keep saying that you’re not dating?”

“Because we aren’t,” he answered with a shrug. Maddie made a strangled noise. “I’m his friend, Mads, that’s all. He doesn’t… He just doesn’t.”

“I cannot…” 

His sister shook her hand and got to her feet, stomping off while muttering unkind things about him. Daniel watched her go, laughing to himself, until a shadow fell over him. He turned and saw Nash standing there, a serious if hesitant expression on his face.

“What?” Daniel asked, wary.

“Can I talk to you for a moment?” the fire captain asked.

Daniel gave him a narrow-eyed stare, glanced about at everyone around them, and nodded with a shrug. Nash looked relieved and gestured for him to follow, so he did. The man led him to where Michael, face scored with healing scratches and scrapes, was standing by his lonesome.

“Michael has a brain tumor,” Nash said, without much of a lead up.

“Bobby!” Michael snapped, glaring at him. “I asked you not to say anything.”

“You said you didn’t want them to know.”

Daniel sighed and draped an arm around Michael’s shoulders, turning him slightly and leading him away from Nash, towards the rest of the crowd.

“C’mon,” he told the man. “Let’s go embarrass the brats-” Buck and Eddie, who were once again canoodling beneath the mistletoe. At least Eddie wasn’t being shy about public displays of affection anymore, however much Daniel might wish that to still be the case. “-and then we can drown ourselves in eggnog later.”

Michael looked more resigned than anything else.

 

Fin.
